But when you pray, go into your closet, shut your door, and pray to your Father, who is in secret. And your Father, who sees in secret, will reward you openly. — Matthew 6:6

Scripture is quoted from the Berean Standard Bible, kept closer to the author’s own Bible where his argument rests on its wording; capitals for God in quotations (He, His) follow the BSB. “You+” marks every plural “you” (the older “ye”); a plain “you” is singular or general.

Preface

Dear friends, I heartily commend the following discourse on closet prayer to your+ serious reading. I have many reasons to hope that when you+ have once read it through, you+ will be more in love with closet prayer than ever, that you+ will set a higher value on closet prayer than ever, and that you+ will make better and fuller use of closet prayer than you+ have ever yet done. Consider what I say in my epistle to the reader, and labor to use this little treatise that I now put into your+ hands in such a way that God may be glorified, that your+ own souls may be edified, comforted, and encouraged in the ways of the Lord, and that you+ may be my crown and joy in the great day of our Lord Jesus (1 Thess 2:19–20). So, wishing that “the favor of Him who dwelt in the burning bush” (Deut 33:16) may rest on you+ and yours+ forever, I take my leave and remain, dear friends, your+ souls’ servant in our dear Lord Jesus,

—Thomas Brooks

To the Reader

Christian reader, since the epistle dedicatory has run so long, as the occasion required, I will do little more than give you the grounds and reasons for sending this little piece out into the world, especially in such a day as this. Now, my reasons are these:

First, because God by His present dealings calls more loudly for closet prayer now than He has at any time in the last twenty years that have passed over our heads. See more on this in the 16th argument for closet prayer.

Secondly, because I have various reasons to fear that many Christians do not clearly or fully understand the necessity, excellence, and usefulness of this subject; that many—oh, if only I could say none—neglect this indispensable duty far too much; and that more than a few, for lack of light, go wrong in the very practice of it.

Thirdly, for the refreshing, support, and encouragement of all those churches of Christ that walk in the fear of the Lord and in the comfort of the Holy Spirit, especially that particular church to which I am joined.

Fourthly, to preserve and keep up the power of religion and godliness in men’s houses, hearts, and lives. The power of religion and godliness lives, thrives, or dies as closet prayer lives, thrives, or dies. Godliness never rises to a higher pitch than when men keep closest to their closets.

Fifthly, because closet prayer is a most sovereign remedy, a most precious antidote of God’s own prescribing, against the plague that is now raging among us (1 Kgs 8:37–39).

Sixthly, because every man is really what he is secretly. Never tell me how handsomely, how neatly, how finely this or that man acts his part before others; but tell me, if you can, how he acts his part before God in his closet, for the man is certainly what he is secretly. There are many who sweat on the stage who are stone-cold in their closets.

Seventhly, though many worthy men have written worthily on all the other parts of prayer, yet of all the writers, earlier or later, who have come under my eye, none has written any treatise on this subject. I have wondered not a little that so many eminent writers should pass over this great and princely duty of closet prayer, either with a few brief touches, or else in a very great silence. If you+ consult various bodies of theology, you+ will find that all they say clearly and distinctly about closet prayer could be put into a very small space, if not into a nutshell. I have also asked various old disciples whether they had ever heard one sermon on closet prayer among all the thousand sermons they had heard in their lives, and they answered, “No.” I have also asked them whether they had ever read any treatise on the subject, and they answered, “No.” And truly this has been no small encouragement to me to offer my mite. And if this small attempt of mine is so blessed as to provoke others who have better heads, and hearts, and hands than I have, to do Christ and His people more service by handling this choice point more fully than I have been able to, I will rejoice in that.

Eighthly and lastly, the favor, the good acceptance, and the fair treatment that my other poor labors have found, not only in this nation but also in other countries, have moved me to put pen to paper once more. And I hope that “the favor of Him who dwelt in the burning bush” (Deut 33:16) will rest on this, as it has, to the glory of free grace, rested on my former endeavors. I could add other reasons, but let these suffice.

Good reader, when you are in your closet, pray hard for a poor, weak, worthless worm, that I may be found faithful and fruitful until death, so that at last I may receive a crown of life. So, wishing you all happiness both in this lower world and in that upper world, I remain,

Yours in our dear Lord Jesus,

Thomas Brooks.

Introduction

But when you pray, go into your closet, shut your door, and pray to your Father, who is in secret. And your Father, who sees in secret, will reward you openly.

— Matt 6:6

These words of our Savior are plain and are to be taken literally, not allegorically, for He speaks of shutting the door of the room. In this chapter there is a clear contrast between the Pharisees praying in the synagogues and on the street corners, and others praying in secret.

In the text, you+ have a positive precept for every Christian to pray alone: “But when you pray.” He does not say, “when you+ pray,” in the plural, but “when you pray,” in the singular, “go into your closet,” as speaking not so much of a joint duty of many praying together, as of a duty which each person is to do alone. The command in the text sends us to the closet as well as to the church. And he is a hypocrite through and through who chooses the one and neglects the other, for by this he tells the world that he cares for neither and makes neither a matter of conscience. He who puts on the garb of religion in public to gain himself a great name among men, and at the same time lives like an atheist at home, will at last be unmasked by God and presented before all the world as a most egregious hypocrite.

Bellarmine and some others turn the text into an allegory. They say that there are two allegories in these words. First, the door of the room is the senses: “shut your door,” that is, they say, your senses, so that vain imaginations and worldly thoughts do not distract your mind in praying. Secondly, the door, they say, is our mouth: “shut your door,” that is, your lips, they say, and let your prayer be like the prayer of Hannah, conceived in your mind, but not uttered with your mouth. It is common for papists, and other monkish men who lie in wait to deceive, to turn the blessed Scriptures into a nose of wax under the pretense of allegories and mysteries. Origen was a great admirer of allegories. By the strength of his talents and his fanciful mind, he turned most of the Scriptures into allegories. And by the just judgment of God on him, he foolishly understood and absurdly applied Matt 19:12 literally, “others have made themselves eunuchs for the sake of the kingdom of heaven,” and so castrated himself. And indeed he might as well have plucked out one of his eyes on the same grounds, because Christ says, “It is better for you to enter life with one eye than to have two eyes and be thrown into the fire of hell” (Matt 18:9). In all ages heretics have commonly defended their heresies by turning Scriptures into allegories. The apostle speaks of men who, denying the resurrection of the body, turn all the testimonies of the resurrection into an allegory, meaning by them only the spiritual resurrection of the soul from sin. Such were Hymenaeus and Philetus, who destroyed the faith of some, saying “that the resurrection has already occurred” (2 Tim 2:17–18). And are there not many among us who turn the whole history of the Bible into an allegory, and who turn Christ, sin, death, the soul, hell, heaven, and all into an allegory? Many have miserably perverted the Scriptures, and many still do, by turning them into vain and groundless allegories. Some fanciful minds have explained paradise as the soul, the man as the mind, the woman as the sense, the serpent as delight, the tree of the knowledge of good and evil as wisdom, and the rest of the trees as the virtues and endowments of the mind. O friends, it is dangerous to bring in allegories where the Scripture does not clearly and plainly warrant them, and to take figuratively words that should be taken literally.

The word ταμεῖον, which in the text is rendered closet, has only three common meanings among Greek authors. First, it may be taken for a secret room, or a private and locked parlor; secondly, for a pantry or cupboard to keep food in; thirdly, for a locked chest or cupboard in which treasure is usually kept.

The best and most judicious interpreters I have cast my eye on, both of earlier and later date, all expound my text of private prayer in secluded places, and I agree with them. And so the main doctrine that I will gather from the words is this:

Doctrine: Closet prayer or private prayer is an indispensable duty that Christ Himself has laid on all who are unwilling to bear the woeful brand of being hypocrites.

I urge you+ seriously to lay these five things to heart:

First, if any prayer is a duty, then secret prayer must be a duty, for secret prayer is as much prayer as any other prayer is prayer. And secret prayer prepares and fits the soul for family prayer and for public prayer. Secret prayer sweetly inclines and strongly disposes a Christian to all other religious duties and services.

Therefore, secondly, if secret prayer is not an indispensable duty that lies on you, by what authority does conscience so upbraid you, and so accuse you, and so condemn you, and so terrify you, as it often does for the neglect of this duty?

Thirdly, was it ever God’s way or method to promise a reward again and again, an open reward, for a work or service that He Himself never commanded? Surely not. Now, the Lord has again and again promised an open reward for this duty of secret prayer (Matt 6:6, 18). So, without any doubt, this is a duty binding on all Christians.

Fourthly, our Savior in the text takes it for granted that every child of God will pray often to His heavenly Father; and therefore He encourages them all the more in the work of secret prayer. “When you+ pray”—as if He had said, “I know you+ can no more live without prayer than you+ can hear without ears, or live without food, or fight without hands, or walk without feet. And therefore, when you+ go to wait on God, or to pay your+ heavenly Father a visit, go into your+ closet, and shut your+ doors,” etc.

Fifthly, if closet prayer is not an indispensable duty that Christ has laid on all His people, why does Satan oppose it so much? Why does he labor so industriously and so tirelessly to discourage Christians in it, and to draw Christians away from it? Certainly Satan would never make such a fierce and constant war as he does on private prayer if it were not a necessary duty, a real duty, and a soul-enriching duty. But you+ will find more of this in the following discourse, and therefore let this touch suffice for the present.

Now, these five things very clearly and plainly demonstrate that to hold fellowship with God secretly and alone is the undoubted duty of every Christian. But for a fuller opening and confirmation of this great and important point, I will lay down these twenty arguments or considerations.

Twenty Arguments for Private Prayer

1. The Most Eminent Saints in Private Prayer

First, the most eminent saints, both in the Old and New Testament, have applied themselves to private prayer. Moses was alone on the mountain with God forty days and forty nights (Exod 34:28). So Abraham fills his mouth with arguments and reasons the case out alone with God in prayer to prevent Sodom’s desolation and destruction, and never stops pleading and praying until he has brought God down from fifty to ten (Gen 18:22–32). And in Genesis 21:33 you+ have Abraham again at his private prayers: “And Abraham planted a grove in Beersheba, and there he called upon the name of the Lord, the Eternal God.” Why did Abraham plant a grove, except to have a most private place in which to pray and pour out his soul before the Lord? So Isaac: “Early in the evening, Isaac went out to the field to meditate” (Gen 24:63). The Hebrew word lasuach that is here rendered “meditate” means to pray as well as to meditate, and it is often used that way. It is a comprehensive word that takes in both prayer and meditation. So you+ will find Jacob at his private prayer: “So Jacob was left all alone, and there a man wrestled with him until daybreak” (Gen 32:24–28). When Jacob was all alone in the dark night, and when his joints were out of joint, he so wrestles and weeps, and weeps and wrestles, in private prayer that at last, as a prince, he prevails with God (Hos 12:3–4). So David: “But I call to God, and the Lord saves me. Evening, morning, and noon I pray and cry aloud, and He hears my voice” (Ps 55:16–17).

So Daniel was in private prayer three times a day: “Now when Daniel learned that the document had been signed, he went into his house, where the windows of his upper room opened toward Jerusalem, and three times a day he got down on his knees, prayed, and gave thanks to his God, just as he had done before” (Dan 6:10). Daniel had accustomed himself to private prayer. He went to his closet before he went to his public employment and affairs of state; and when he came back for his midday meal, he turned first into his room to serve his God and refresh his soul before he sat down to feast his body. And at the end of the day, when he had dispatched his business with men, he made it his business to wait on God in his room. So Jonah keeps up private prayer when he was in “the belly of the fish,” yes, when he was in “the belly of hell” (Jonah 2:1–2). So we have Elijah at prayer under the juniper tree (1 Kgs 19:4). So Hannah: “Hannah was praying in her heart, and though her lips were moving, her voice could not be heard” (1 Sam 1:13). The very soul of prayer lies in pouring out the soul before God, as Hannah did (v. 15). Nor was Rebekah a stranger to this duty, who, when the baby struggled in her womb, “went to inquire of the Lord” (Gen 25:22); that is, she went to some secret place to pray, say Calvin, Musculus, Mercerus, and others. So Saul is no sooner converted than he at once turns to private prayer: “Get up!” the Lord told him. “Go to the house of Judas on Straight Street and ask for a man from Tarsus named Saul, for he is praying” (Acts 9:11). Though he was a strict Pharisee, yet he had never prayed effectually before, nor ever prayed in private before. The Pharisees used to pray on the corners of the streets, and not in the corners of their houses. And after his conversion he was frequently in private prayer, as you+ may see by comparing these scriptures together: Rom 1:9; Eph 1:15–16; Phil 1:3–4; 2 Tim 1:3. So Epaphras was a fervent man in closet prayer (Col 4:12–13); so Cornelius had devoted himself to private prayer (Acts 10:2, 4); and so Peter goes up to the housetop to pray: “The next day at about the sixth hour, as the men were approaching the city on their journey, Peter went up on the housetop to pray” (Acts 10:9). Peter went up onto the roof not only to avoid distraction, but so that he might be all the more secret in his private devotion.

Eusebius tells us of James, called Justus, that his knees had grown hard and calloused from kneeling so much in private prayer. And Nazianzen reports of his sister Gorgonia that her knees seemed to cling to the ground from praying in private so often. And Gregory says of his aunt Trucilla that her elbows were as hard as horn from leaning so often on her desk at private prayer. I have read of a devout person who, when the set time for his private devotion came, would break away from whatever company he was in with this neat and graceful excuse: “I have a friend waiting for me; farewell.” And there was once a great lady of this land who would often withdraw from the company of lords and ladies of high rank who came to visit her, rather than lose her set times of waiting on God in her closet. She would, as they called it, rudely take her leave of them, so that she might attend the Lord of lords in private. She would give what time she could spare to showing her favors, civilities, and courtesies to her relatives and friends; but she would never allow them to rob God of His time, or her soul of the comfort and communion that she used to enjoy when she was with God in her closet. And indeed, one hour’s communion with God in one’s closet is to be preferred to the greatest and best company in the world.

There was a child of a Christian gentlewoman who was so given to prayer from his infancy that before he could speak well, he used to get off alone and go to prayer. As he grew, he was more frequent in prayer and in withdrawing himself from company. He would ask his mother very strange questions, far above the capacity of one of his years. But at last, when this child was only five years old and was whipping his top, he suddenly flung away his whip and top, ran to his mother, and with great joy said to her, “Mother, I must go to God; will you go with me?” She answered, “My dear child, how do you know you will go to God?” He answered, “God has told me so, for I love God, and God loves me.” She answered, “Dear child, I must go when God pleases. But why will you not stay with me?” The child answered, “I will not stay; I must go to God.” And the child did not live more than a month after, and never cared for play again; but falling sick, he would always be saying that he must go to God, he must go to God. Thus sometimes “from the mouths of children and infants” God has “ordained praise” (Matt 21:16). Certainly such persons will be ripe for heaven early who begin early to seek God in a closet, in a corner.

Eusebius reports of the emperor Constantine that every day he would shut himself up in some secret place in his palace and there, on bended knees, offer his devout prayers and soliloquies to God. “My God and I are good company,” said the famous Dr. Sibbes. A man whose soul holds fellowship with God in a closet, in a hole, behind the door, or in a desert, a den, a dungeon, will find more real pleasure, more choice delight, and fuller contentment there than in the palace of a prince.

By all these famous instances, you+ see that the people of God in all ages have devoted themselves to private prayer. O friends, these pious examples should be very awakening, very convicting, and very encouraging to you+. Certainly it is as much your+ duty as it is your+ glory to follow these pious patterns that are now set before you+. As these scriptures witness: “So you will follow in the ways of the good, and keep to the paths of the righteous.… You+ are to imitate me, just as I imitate Christ.… Join one another in following my example, brothers, and carefully observe those who walk according to the pattern we set for you+.… Whatever you+ have learned or received or heard from me, or seen in me, put it into practice. And the God of peace will be with you+.… And you+ became imitators of us and of the Lord when you+ welcomed the message … in spite of your+ great suffering.… Then you+ will not be sluggish, but will imitate those who through faith and patience inherit what has been promised” (Prov 2:20; 1 Cor 11:1; Phil 3:17; 4:9; 1 Thess 1:6; Heb 6:12). See also 2 Tim 3:10–12, 14; Titus 2:7. It was an excellent law that the Ephesians made, that men should set the best patterns before themselves and always bear some eminent man in mind. Bad men are wonderfully in love with bad examples (Jer 44:16–17). The Indian, hearing that his ancestors had gone to hell, said that then he would go there too. Some men have a mind to go to hell for company’s sake. Oh, that we were as much in love with the examples of good men as others are in love with the examples of bad men! Then we would be in our closets more often than we are now. Oh, that our eyes were more fixed on the pious examples of all who have in them aliquid Christi (anything of Christ), as Bucer said! Shall we love to look on the pictures of our friends, and shall we not love to look on the pious examples of those who are the living and lovely picture of Christ? The pious examples of others should be the mirrors by which we dress ourselves. He is the best and wisest Christian who copies the fairest pattern in Scripture, who imitates the Christians who are most eminent in grace, and who have been most exercised in closet prayer and in the most secret duties of religion.

Jerome, having read the life and death of Hilarion, one who lived most like a Christian and died full of comfort, folded up the book, saying, “Well, Hilarion will be the champion I follow. His good life will be my example, and his godly death my precedent.” It is a noble thing to live and die by the examples of the most eminent saints.

2. Christ Much Exercised Himself in Secret Prayer

Secondly, consider that when Christ was on earth He exercised Himself much in secret prayer. He was often with God alone, as you+ may see in these famous scriptures: “After He had sent the crowds away, He went up on the mountain by Himself to pray. When evening came, He was there alone” (Matt 14:23). Christ’s choosing solitary places for private prayer not only hints to us the danger of distraction and wandering of thoughts in prayer, but shows how necessary it is for us to choose the most suitable places we can for private prayers. Our own fickleness and Satan’s restlessness call on us to get into such corners where we may most freely pour out our souls into the bosom of God. “Early in the morning, a great while before day, Jesus got up and slipped out to a solitary place to pray” (Mark 1:35). As the morning is the fittest time for prayer, so solitary places are the fittest places for prayer. “After bidding them farewell, He went up on the mountain to pray” (Mark 6:46). He who would pray effectually needs to be quiet when he is alone. “Yet He frequently withdrew to the wilderness to pray” (Luke 5:16). The Greek has, He “was withdrawing and praying,” to let us understand that He did this often. When Christ was busy neither in teaching nor in working miracles, He was then very intent on private prayer. “In those days Jesus went out to the mountain to pray, and He spent all night in prayer to God” (Luke 6:12). Did Christ spend whole nights in private prayer to save our souls, and shall we think it too much to spend an hour or two in the day for the furtherance of the internal and eternal welfare of our souls?

“Every day Jesus taught at the temple, but every evening He went out to spend the night on the Mount of Olives” (Luke 21:37). Christ frequently joins praying and preaching together, and what Christ has joined together, let no man presume to separate. “Jesus went out as usual to the Mount of Olives, and the disciples followed Him.… And He withdrew about a stone’s throw beyond them, where He knelt down and prayed.… And in His anguish, He prayed more earnestly, and His sweat became like great drops of blood (clotted or congealed blood) falling to the ground (never was a garden watered with blood, before or since, as this was). When Jesus rose from prayer and returned to the disciples, He found them asleep, exhausted from sorrow” (Luke 22:39, 41, 44–45). Ah, what sad pieces of frailty the best of men are in an hour of trial and temptation! These very men, who a little before had boldly professed and promised that they would never leave Him nor forsake Him, and that they would go to prison for Christ and die for Christ, could not so much as watch with Him one hour when the day of trial came. They had neither eyes to see nor hands to wipe off Christ’s bloody sweat. So John 6:15–17. Thus you+ see by all these famous instances that Christ was frequent in private prayer. Oh, that we would daily set this noble pattern before ourselves for our imitation, and make it our business, our work, our heaven, to copy this blessed pattern that Christ has set us: to be much with God alone! Certainly Christianity is nothing else but an imitation of the divine nature, a man’s bringing himself back to the image of God in which he was “created … in true righteousness and holiness” (Eph 4:24). A Christian’s whole life should be nothing but a visible representation of Christ. The heathen had this notion among them, as Lactantius reports, that the way to honor their gods was to be like them. I am sure that the highest way of honoring Christ is to be like Christ: “Whoever claims to abide in Him must walk as Jesus walked” (1 John 2:6). Oh, that this blessed Scripture might always lie warm on our hearts! Christ is the sun, and all the watches of our lives should be set by the dial of His motion. Christ is a pattern of patterns; His example should be to us instead of a thousand examples. It is not only our liberty, but our duty and glory, to follow Christ absolutely in all His moral virtues. Other patterns are imperfect and defective, but Christ is a perfect pattern; and of all His children, they are the happiest who come nearest to this perfect pattern.

Heliogabalus loved his children better for resembling him in sin. But Christ loves His children more for resembling Him in sanctity. I have read of some springs that change the color of the cattle that drink from them to the color of their own waters, as Du Bartas sings:

Cerona, Xanth, and Cephisus do make

The thirsty flocks, that of their waters take,

Black, red, and white; and near the crimson deep,

The Arabian fountain makes crimson sheep.

Certainly Jesus Christ is such a fountain: whoever bathes in it, and whoever drinks of it, will be changed into the same likeness (2 Cor 3:18).

Question. But why was our Lord Jesus so much in private prayer? Why was He so often with God alone?

Answer 1. First, it was to put a very high honor and value on private prayer; it was to enhance and raise the worth of this duty. Men are naturally very apt and prone to have low and undervaluing thoughts of secret prayer. But Christ, by exercising Himself in it so frequently, has put an everlasting honor and an inestimable value on it.

Answer 2. Secondly, He was much in private prayer, He was often with God alone, so that He might not be seen by men and might avoid all show and appearance of ostentation and popular applause. He who has commanded us to “abstain from every appearance of evil” (1 Thess 5:22) would not Himself, when He was in this world, venture on the least appearance of evil. Christ was very wary of everything that so much as looked like sin; He was very wary of the very show and shadow of pride or vainglory.

Answer 3. Thirdly, to avoid interruptions in the duty. Secrecy is no small help in carrying on a private duty seriously and with life. Interruptions and disturbances from outside are often water thrown on the coals of private prayer. Even the best Christians do no better than bungle when they meet with interruptions in their private devotions.

Answer 4. Fourthly, to set us such a blessed pattern and gracious example that we should never be pleased or content with public prayers only, nor with family prayers only, but should also apply ourselves to secret prayer, to closet prayer. Christ was not always in public, nor always in His family, but He was often in private with God alone, so that by His own example He might encourage us to be often with God in secret. Happy are those who tread in His steps and who copy His pattern.

Answer 5. Fifthly, that He might prove Himself, to our understandings and consciences, a most just and faithful High Priest (Heb 2:17; John 17). Christ was wonderfully faithful and careful in both parts of His priestly office: satisfaction and intercession. He was His people’s only spokesman. Ah, how earnest, how frequent He was in pouring out prayers, and tears, and sighs, and groans for His people in secret when He was in this world (Heb 5:7)! And now that He is in heaven, He is always making intercession for them (Heb 7:25).

Answer 6. Sixthly, to convince us that His Father hears and observes our private prayers, and bottles up all our secret tears, and that He is no stranger to our closet desires, wrestlings, breathings, hungerings, and thirstings.

3. Secret Prayer Distinguishes You from Hypocrites

Thirdly, consider that regularly exercising yourselves+ in secret prayer is what will distinguish you+ from hypocrites, who do all they do to be seen by men. “Be careful not to perform your+ righteous acts before men to be seen by them. If you+ do, you+ will have no reward from your+ Father in heaven. So when you give to the needy, do not sound a trumpet before you, as the hypocrites do in the synagogues and on the streets, to be honored by men. Truly I tell you+, they already have their full reward” (Matt 6:1–2). Self is the only oil that makes the chariot-wheels of the hypocrite move in all religious matters. “And when you pray, do not be like the hypocrites. For they love to pray standing in the synagogues and on the street corners to be seen by men. Truly I tell you+, they already have their full reward.… When you+ fast, do not be somber like the hypocrites, for they disfigure their faces to show men they are fasting. Truly I tell you+, they already have their full reward” (Matt 6:5, 16). Thus you+ see that these hypocrites look more at men than at God in all their duties. When they give alms, the trumpet must sound; when they pray, it must be in the synagogues and on the street corners; and when they fasted, they disfigured their faces to show men they were fasting. Hypocrites live on the praises and applause of men. Naturalists report of the Chelydonian stone that it will keep its power only as long as it is enclosed in gold. So hypocrites will keep up their duties only as long as they are fed, and encouraged, and enclosed with the golden praises and applause of men. Hypocrites are like blazing stars, which, as long as they are fed with vapors, shine as if they were fixed stars; but let the vapors dry up, and at once they vanish and disappear.

Closet duty shows the most sincerity. He prays in earnest who prays without a witness. The more sincere the soul is, the more it will give itself to closet duty (Job 31:33). Where in all the Scripture do you+ read that Pharaoh, or Saul, or Judas, or Demas, or Simon Magus, or the scribes and Pharisees made it their practice to pour out their souls before the Lord in secret? Secret prayer is not the hypocrite’s ordinary walk, his ordinary work or trade. There is great cause to fear that the heart of a man whose whole devotion is spent among men, or among many, was never right with God; otherwise our Savior, in drawing the hypocrite’s picture, would never have made this the very look of his face, as He does in Matthew 6:5. It is well worth noting that Christ commands His disciples not to be like the hypocrites. It is one thing to be hypocrites, and another thing to be like the hypocrites. Christ does not want His people to look like hypocrites, or to be like hypocrites. Only sincerity will enable a man to make a trade of private prayer. In praying with many, there are many things that may bribe and provoke a carnal heart, such as pride, vainglory, love of applause, or the wish to make a name. A hypocrite in all his duties trades more for a good name than for a good life, for a good report than for a good conscience—like fiddlers who are more careful in tuning their instruments than in composing their lives. But in private prayer no such trade can be carried on.

4. In Secret We May Freely Unbosom Our Souls to God

Fourthly, consider that in secret we may open our souls to God more freely, and fully, and safely than we can in the presence of many or a few. That is why the husband is to mourn apart, and the wife apart (Zech 12:12–14), not only to show the soundness of their sorrow, but also to show their sincerity by their secrecy. They must mourn apart so that their sins may not be disclosed or revealed to one another. Here they are separated to show that they did not weep for company’s sake, but for their own particular sins, by which they had pierced and crucified the Lord of glory. In secret a Christian may go into particulars that he will not, he may not, he ought not to mention in public or before others. Ah, how many Christians there are who would blush and be ashamed to walk in the streets, and to keep company with sinners or saints, if the infirmities, enormities, and wickednesses which they freely and fully lay open to God in secret were so much as written on their foreheads, or known to others! There are many sins that many men have fallen into, before conversion and since, which, if they were known to the world, would make the men themselves stink, and religion stink, and their profession of faith stink in the nostrils of all who know them. Yes, if the weaknesses and wickednesses that many have been guilty of, before receiving grace or since, were proclaimed on the housetops, how weak Christians would be staggered, young beginners in the ways of God discouraged, many mouths of blasphemy opened, and many sinners’ hearts hardened against the Lord, His ways, His reproofs, and the things of their own peace! Yes, how Satan’s banner would be displayed, and his kingdom strengthened, and he himself infinitely pleased and delighted! It is an infinite mercy in God, and His gracious stooping to us, to lay a law of restraint on Satan, who otherwise would be the greatest telltale in all the world. It would be mirth and music to him to be always laying open the follies and weaknesses of the saints.

Ambrose brings in the devil boasting against Christ, and claiming Judas as his own: “He is not Yours, Lord Jesus, he is mine. His thoughts beat for me; he eats with You, but is fed by me; he takes bread from You, but money from me; he drinks with You, and sells Your blood to me.” There is not a sin that a saint commits but Satan would trumpet it out to all the world if God would only give him leave. No man in his right mind will lay open to everyone his bodily infirmities, weaknesses, diseases, ailments, griefs, etc., but only to some near relative, or bosom friend, or skilled physician. So no man in his right mind will lay open to everyone the infirmities, weaknesses, diseases, ailments, griefs, etc., of his soul, but only to the Lord, or to some particular person who is wise, faithful, and able to contribute something to his soul’s relief. If a Christian were to lay open or rip open all his follies and vanities to the world, how cruelly some would mock and scorn him! And how severely and bitterly others would censure and judge him! When David was alone in the cave, then he poured out his complaint to God, and showed his trouble before Him (Ps 142:2). And when Job was all alone, then his eyes poured out tears to God (Job 16:20). There is no hazard, no danger, in ripping everything open before God in a corner, but there may be a great deal of hazard and danger in ripping everything open before men.

5. Secret Duties Shall Have Open Rewards

Fifthly, secret duties will have open rewards. “And your Father, who sees in secret, will reward you openly” (Matt 6:6). God will reward His people here in part, and hereafter in all perfection (v. 18). He is a rewarder of those who diligently seek Him in a corner. Those who sow in tears secretly will reap in joy openly. Private prayer will be rewarded publicly, before men and angels. How openly God rewarded Daniel for his secret prayer (Dan 6:10, 23–28)! Mordecai privately revealed a treasonous plot against the person of King Ahasuerus, and he is rewarded openly (Esth 2:21–23; with chapter 6). Darius, before he came to the kingdom, privately received a garment as a gift from one Syloson; and when he became king, he rewarded him openly with the rule of his country, Samos. God, in the great day, will recompense His people before all the world for every secret prayer, and secret tear, and secret sigh, and secret groan that has come from them. God, in the great day, will declare to men and angels how often His people have been pouring out their souls before Him in such and such holes, corners, and secret places; and He will reward them accordingly.

Ah, Christians, if you+ really believed this, and seriously dwelt on this, you+ would

  • walk more thankfully;
  • work more cheerfully;
  • suffer more patiently;
  • fight against the world, the flesh, and the devil more courageously;
  • spend yourselves+ for God, His cause, and His glory more freely;
  • live with what providence has cut out as your+ portion more quietly and contentedly; and,
  • you+ would pray in private more frequently, more abundantly.

6. God Reveals Himself Most to His People in Secret

Sixthly, consider that God has usually revealed Himself most to His people when they have been in secret, when they have been alone at the throne of grace. Oh, the sweet meltings, the heavenly warmings, the blessed cheerings, the glorious manifestations, and the choice communion with God that Christians have found when they have been alone with God in a corner, in a closet, behind the door! When did Daniel receive that vision and comforting message, that blessed news by the angel that he was “greatly beloved,” if not when he was all alone at prayer? “While I was speaking, praying, confessing my sin and that of my people Israel, and presenting my petition before the Lord my God concerning His holy mountain—while I was still praying, Gabriel, the man I had seen in the earlier vision, came to me in swift flight about the time of the evening sacrifice. He instructed me and spoke with me, saying: ‘O Daniel, I have come now to give you insight and understanding. At the beginning of your petitions, an answer went out, and I have come to tell you, for you are greatly beloved. So consider the message and understand the vision’” (Dan 9:20–23). While Daniel was at private prayer, God, by the angel Gabriel, reveals to him the secret of His counsel concerning the restoration of Jerusalem and how long it would last, even to the Messiah. And while Daniel was at private prayer, the Lord appears to him, and in an extraordinary way assures him that he was a man “greatly beloved,” or as the Hebrew chumudoth has it, “a man of desires”: that is, a man toward whom God’s desires go out, a man singularly beloved by God, and highly in favor with God, a man who is very pleasing and delightful to God. God loves to load the wings of private prayer with the sweetest, choicest, and chiefest blessings. Ah, how often has God kissed a poor Christian at the beginning of private prayer, and spoken peace to him in the midst of private prayer, and filled him with light and joy and assurance at the close of private prayer!

And so Cornelius is highly commended and graciously rewarded on account of his private prayer. “At Caesarea there was a man named Cornelius, a centurion in what was called the Italian Regiment. He and all his household were devout and God-fearing. He gave generously to the people and prayed to God regularly. One day at about the ninth hour, he had a clear vision of an angel of God who came to him and said, ‘Cornelius!’ Cornelius stared at him in fear and asked, ‘What is it, Lord?’ The angel answered, ‘Your prayers and gifts to the poor have ascended as a memorial offering before God.’ … Cornelius answered: ‘Four days ago I was fasting until this hour (that is, until three o’clock in the afternoon, v. 3); and at the ninth hour I was praying in my house. Suddenly a man in bright clothing stood before me and said … “Cornelius, your prayer has been heard, and your gifts to the poor have been remembered before God”’” (Acts 10:1–4, 30–31). Mark this: as he was praying in his house, that is, by himself alone, a man in bright clothing (an angel in a man’s shape, v. 3) appeared to him, and said, “Cornelius, your prayer has been heard” (v. 31). He does not mean only the prayer he made when he fasted and humbled himself before the Lord, but, as verses 2–4 show, his prayers, the prayers he made alone. For it seems no one else was with him then, for he alone saw that man in bright clothing, and to him alone the angel addressed his present speech, saying, “Cornelius, your prayer has been heard” (v. 31). Here you+ see that Cornelius’s private prayers are not only heard, but kindly remembered, graciously accepted, and gloriously rewarded. Praying Cornelius is not only remembered by God, but he is also visited, perceptibly and plainly, by an angel, and assured that his private prayers and good deeds are a fragrance, “a fragrant offering, an acceptable sacrifice, well-pleasing to God” (Phil 4:18).

And so when did Peter have his vision but when he was praying alone on the housetop? “The next day at about the sixth hour, as the men were approaching the city on their journey, Peter went up on the housetop to pray. He became hungry and wanted something to eat, but while the meal was being prepared, he fell into a trance. He saw heaven opened and something like a large sheet being let down to earth by its four corners. It contained all kinds of four-footed animals and reptiles of the earth, as well as birds of the air. Then a voice said to him: ‘Get up, Peter, kill and eat!’” (Acts 10:9–13). When Peter was on the housetop at prayer alone, then he fell into a trance and saw heaven opened. Then his spirit was raised, his mind lifted up, and all the faculties of his soul filled with a divine revelation.

And so when Paul was at prayer alone, he saw “in a vision … a man named Ananias come and place his hands on him to restore his sight” (Acts 9:12). Paul had not been long at private prayer before it was revealed to him that he was a chosen vessel, and before he was filled with the gifts, graces, and comforts of the Holy Spirit.

And when John was alone on the island of Patmos, where he was, as he says, “because of the word of God and my testimony about Jesus” (Rev 1:9), banished there by Domitian (Rev 1:9–20; 5:1–9), a most cruel emperor, then he had a glorious sight of the Son of Man, and then the Lord revealed to him most deep and profound mysteries, concerning both the present and the future state of the church, to the end of the world. And when John was weeping, in private prayer no doubt, then the sealed book was opened to him.

So when Daniel was at private prayer, God dispatches a heavenly messenger to him, and his errand was to open the blessed Scripture to him more clearly and fully. This holy man of God had attained some comforting and encouraging knowledge before, by his frequent and constant study of the Word. This eggs him on to private prayer, and private prayer sends an angel post-haste from heaven to give him a clearer and fuller light. Private prayer is a golden key to unlock the mysteries of the Word to us. The knowledge of many choice and blessed truths is only the return of private prayer. The Word dwells most richly in the hearts of those who pour out their hearts most before God in their closets. When Bonaventure, that seraphic doctor, as some call him, was asked by Aquinas from what books and helps he drew such holy and divine expressions and contemplations, he pointed to a crucifix, and said, “Prostrate in prayer at the feet of this image, my soul receives greater light from heaven than from all study and disputation.” Though this is a monkish tradition and superstitious fiction, yet some use may be made of it. Certainly, the Christian or the minister who in private prayer lies most at the feet of Jesus Christ will understand most of the mind of Christ in the gospel, and he will have most of heaven, and the things of his own peace, brought down into his heart.

There is no service in which Christians have such near, intimate, and friendly fellowship with God as in this of private prayer. Nor is there any service in which God more delights to make known His truth and faithfulness, His grace and goodness, His mercy and bounty, His beauty and glory to poor souls than this of private prayer. Luther declared that he profited more in the knowledge of the Scripture by private prayer in a short space than he did by study in a longer space, as John by weeping in a corner got the sealed book opened. Private prayer crowns God with the honor and glory due to His name; and God crowns private prayer with a revelation to His servants of those blessed, weighty truths that are a sealed book to others. Certainly, the soul usually enjoys the most communion with God in secret. When a Christian is in a wilderness, which is a very solitary place, then God delights to speak to him in a friendly and comforting way. “Behold, I will allure her and lead her to the wilderness, and speak to her comfortingly” (Hos 2:14), or as the Hebrew has it, “I will speak to her heart.” When I have her alone, says God, in a solitary wilderness, I will speak such things to her heart as will greatly cheer her and comfort her, and even make her heart leap and dance within her. A husband opens his mind most freely and fully to his wife when she is alone; and so does Christ to the believing soul. Oh, the secret kisses, the secret embraces, the secret visits, the secret whispers, the secret cheerings, the secret sealings, the secret revelations, etc., that God gives to His people when they are alone, when in a hole, when under the stairs, when behind the door, when in a dungeon! When Jeremiah was calling on God alone in his dark dungeon, he was shown great and wonderful things that he did not know (Jer 33:1–3).

Ambrose used to say, “I am never less alone than when I am alone; for then I can enjoy the presence of my God most freely, fully, and sweetly, without interruption.”

And it was a most sweet and divine saying of Bernard: “O saint, do you not know that your husband Christ is bashful, and will not be intimate in company? Withdraw yourself therefore by prayer and meditation into your closet or the fields, and there you will have Christ’s embraces.”

A gentlewoman, at private prayer and meditation in her parlor, had such sweet, choice, and full enjoyment of God that she cried out, “Oh, if only I could always enjoy this sweet communion with God!”

Christ loves to embrace His spouse, not so much in the open street as in a closet; and certainly the gracious soul never has sweeter views of glory than when it is most out of the view of the world. Wise men give their best, their choicest, and their richest gifts in secret; and so Christ gives His people the best of the best when they are in a corner, when they are all alone. But those who cannot spare time to seek God in a closet, to serve Him in secret, show clearly enough that they have little fellowship or friendship with God, since they so seldom come to Him.

7. This Life Is the Only Time for Private Prayer

Seventhly, consider that the time of this life is the only time for private prayer. Heaven will admit no secret prayer. In heaven there will be no secret sins to trouble us, no secret needs to pinch us, no secret temptations to betray us, no secret snares to entangle us, no secret enemies to supplant us. We need to live much in the practice of that duty here on earth that we will never be exercised in after death. Some duties that are binding on us now, such as praising God, wondering at God, exalting and lifting up God, rejoicing and delighting in God, etc., will be binding on us forever in heaven; but this duty of private prayer we must take our leave of when we come to lay our heads in the dust.

8. The Great Prevailing Power of Secret Prayer

Eighthly, consider the great prevailing power of secret prayer. Private prayer is porta coeli, clavis paradisi, the gate of heaven, a key to let us into Paradise. Oh, the great things that private prayer has done with God (Ps 31:22)! Oh, the great blessings that have been obtained by private prayer (Ps 38:8–9)! And oh, the great threatenings that have been turned aside by private prayer! And oh, the great judgments that have been removed by private prayer! And oh, the great judgments that have been prevented by private prayer! I have read of a malicious woman who gave herself to the devil on condition that he would do harm to a certain neighbor whom she hated with a deadly hatred. The devil went again and again to do his errand, but at last he returned and told her that he could do that man no hurt, for whenever he came, he found him either reading the Scriptures or at private prayer. Private prayers pierce the heavens, and are usually blessed and loaded with gracious and glorious returns from there. While Hezekiah was praying and weeping in private, God sent the prophet Isaiah to him to assure him that his prayer was heard, and that his tears were seen, and that He would add fifteen years to his days (Isa 38:5). So, when Isaac was all alone in the fields, meditating and praying and pleading with God for a good wife, he meets Rebekah (Gen 24:63–64).

So it was with Jacob: “So Jacob was left all alone, and there a man wrestled with him until daybreak. When the man saw that he could not prevail against Jacob, he struck the socket of Jacob’s hip and dislocated it as they wrestled. Then the man said, ‘Let me go, for it is daybreak.’ But Jacob replied, ‘I will not let you go unless you bless me.’ ‘What is your name?’ the man asked. ‘Jacob,’ he replied. Then the man said, ‘Your name will no longer be Jacob, but Israel, for as a prince you have power with God and with men, and you have prevailed’” (Gen 32:24–28). In this scripture we have an elegant description of a duel fought between the Almighty and Jacob, and in it these things are most worth observing:

a. The Combatants: Jacob, and God in the Appearance of a Man

(1) First, we have the combatants or duelists, Jacob and God, who appeared in the shape or appearance of a man. He who is here said to be a man was the Son of God in human shape, as appears from the whole narrative, and from Hosea 12:3–5. Now, that this man who wrestled with Jacob was indeed God, and not really a man, is most evident for these reasons:

First, Jacob desires a blessing from Him (v. 26). Now, it is God’s royal prerogative to bless, not that of angels or men.

Secondly, he calls Him by the name of God. “As a prince you have power with God” (v. 28). And Jacob says, “I have seen God face to face” (v. 30). Not that he saw the majesty and essence of God, for no man can see the essential glory of God and live (Exod 33:20, 23); but he saw God more plainly, more manifestly, more gloriously than he ever had before. Jacob saw some created shape, some glimpse of glory, by which God was pleased for the time to show His more immediate presence; but he did not see God Himself.

Thirdly, the same person that Jacob wrestles with here is He Whom Jacob remembers in his blessing as his deliverer from all evil (Gen 48:16). It was the God who appeared to him at Bethel “as he fled from his brother” (Gen 35:7).

Fourthly, Jacob is reproved for his prying inquiry or asking after the Angel’s name (v. 29), which is a clear argument or demonstration of His majesty and glory, God being above all notion and name. God is a substance above all substance, an understanding not to be understood, a word never to be spoken. One man, asked what God was, answered that he would have to be God Himself before he could know God fully. We are as able to hold the sea in a cockleshell as we are to comprehend the Almighty, or that nomen majestativum (majestic name), as Tertullian puts it. “In searching after God,” says Chrysostom, “I am like a man digging in a deep spring: I stand here, and the water rises on me; and I stand there, and still the water rises on me.”

In this conflict you+ have not one man wrestling with another, nor a man wrestling with a created angel, but a poor, weak, mortal man wrestling with an immortal God: weakness wrestling with strength, and a finite being with an infinite being. Though Jacob had no second, though he was all alone, though he was overmatched beyond measure, yet he wrestles and keeps his hold, and all in the strength of the One he wrestles with.

b. The Place: Beside the Ford Jabbok

Secondly, you+ have the place where they fought, and that was beside the ford Jabbok (Gen 32:22). This is the name of a brook or river rising by Rabba, the chief city of the Ammonites, and flowing into the Jordan below the Sea of Galilee (Num 21:24; Deut 2:37; Judg 11:13, 15; Deut 3:16). Jacob never enjoyed so much of the presence of God as when he had left the company of men. Oh, the sweet communion that Jacob had with God when he had withdrawn from his family and was all alone with his God by the ford Jabbok! Certainly, Jacob was never less alone than at this time, when he was so alone. Saints often meet with the best wine and the strongest heart-medicines when they are all alone with God.

c. The Time: The Night, and It Lasted Until Break of Day

Thirdly, you+ have the time of the combat, and that was the night. At what time of the night this wrestling, this duel, began, we do not read anywhere. But it lasted until break of day; it lasted until Jacob had got the better of the Angel. How many hours of the night this conflict lasted, no mortal man can tell. God’s design was that no one but Jacob should be a spectator or witness of this combat; and therefore Jacob had to be wrestling while others were sleeping.

d. The Ground of the Quarrel: Jacob’s Fear of Esau, and His Desire for a Blessing

Fourthly, you+ have the ground of the quarrel, and that was Jacob’s fear of Esau, and his importunate desire for a blessing. Jacob flees to God so that he would not fall before man; he flees to God so that he would not flee before men. In a storm there is no shelter like the wing of God. He who places himself under divine protection is safest and happiest and wisest. Jacob knew this, and so he runs to God as to his only city of refuge. In this conflict God was ready to break off: “Let me go, for it is daybreak” (v. 26); but Jacob keeps his hold and tells Him boldly to His very face that he would not let Him go unless He blessed him. Oh, the power of private prayer! It has a kind of omnipotence in it. It takes God captive; it holds Him as a prisoner; it binds the hands of the Almighty; indeed, it will wring a mercy, a blessing, out of the hand of heaven itself. Oh, the power of that prayer that makes a man victorious over the greatest, the highest power! Jacob was a man, a single man, a traveling man, a tired man; indeed, he was a worm that is easily crushed and trodden underfoot, and no man (Isa 41:14). Yet in private prayer he is so potent that he overcomes the omnipotent God; he is so mighty that he overcomes the Almighty.

e. The Nature of the Combat: Both Outward and Inward, Bodily and Spiritual

Fifthly, you+ have the nature or manner of the combat, and that was both outward and inward, both bodily and spiritual. It was by might and skill; it was as much by the strength of his body as by the force of his faith. He wrestled not only with spiritual strugglings, tears, and prayers (Hos 12:4), but with bodily ones too, in which God assailed him with one hand and upheld him with the other. In this conflict Jacob and the Angel of the covenant really did lock arm with arm, set shoulder to shoulder, and put foot to foot, and used all the other holds and tricks that men use when they wrestle with one another. The Hebrew word ואבק, from אבק, which is here rendered wrestled, signifies the raising of the dust. For those who wrestled in old times not only wrestled naked, as the custom then was, but also used to throw dust on one another, so that they might take surer hold of one another. Some conclude from this word abak that Jacob and the Angel tugged, strove, and turned each other until they were dripping with sweat, for so much the word implies. Jacob and the Angel did not wrestle in jest, but in good earnest; they wrestled with all their might, as it were, for the victor’s garland; they strove for victory as for life.

But as this wrestling was bodily, so it was spiritual also. Jacob’s soul takes hold of God, Jacob’s faith takes hold of God, Jacob’s prayers take hold of God, and Jacob’s tears take hold of God (Hos 12:4–5). Certainly, Jacob’s weapons in this warfare were mainly spiritual, and so “mighty through God” (2 Cor 10:4). There is no overcoming God except in His own strength. Jacob did more by his royal faith than by his noble hands, more by weeping than by sweating, and more by praying than by all his bodily strivings.

f. The Issue of the Combat: Victory over the Angel

Sixthly and lastly, you+ have the outcome of the combat, and that is victory over the Angel (v. 28). Jacob wrestles in the Angel’s arms and armor, and so overcomes Him. As a prince, he overpowers the Angel by the very power he had from the Angel. The Angel was as freely and fully willing to be conquered by Jacob as Jacob was willing to be the conqueror. When lovers wrestle, the stronger is willing enough to take a fall from the weaker; and so it was here. The father, wrestling with his child, is willing enough, for his child’s comfort and encouragement, to take a fall now and then; and so it was between the Angel and Jacob in the present case. Now in this blessed story, as in a crystal mirror, you+ may see the great power, the prevailing power, of private prayer. It conquers the great Conqueror; it is so omnipotent that it overcomes an omnipotent God.

Now you+ may see this cleared up more fully and sweetly in Hosea 12:3–4: “In the womb he grasped his brother’s heel, and by his strength he had power with God. Yes, he had power over the angel and prevailed; he wept and made supplication to Him; he found Him at Bethel and spoke with Him there.” When Jacob was all alone, and in a dark night, and standing on only one leg, even then he played the prince with God, as the Hebrew has it. Jacob, by prayers and tears, so played the prince with God that he carried off the blessing. Jacob’s wrestling was by weeping, and his prevailing by praying. Prayers and tears are not only very pleasing to God, but also very prevailing with God. And so you+ see that this great instance of Jacob proclaims out loud the prevailing power of private prayer.

See another instance of this in David: “I am weary from groaning; all night I flood my bed with weeping and drench my couch with tears” (Ps 6:6). These are all hyperbolic figures of speech, to set forth the greatness of his sorrow and the multitude of his tears. David in his seclusion makes the place of his sin, his bed, the place of his repentance. David sins privately on his bed, and David mourns privately on his bed. Every place that we have polluted by sin, we should sanctify and water with our tears. “Depart from me, all you+ workers of iniquity, for the Lord has heard the voice of my weeping” (Ps 6:8). As blood has a voice, and as the rod has a voice, so tears have a voice. Tears have tongues, and tears can speak. No noise compares with the noise that tears in secret make in the ears of God. A wise and tender father can better make out the needs of his children by their secret tears than by their loud complaints, by their weeping than by their words; and do you+ think that God cannot do as much? Tears are not always mute (Lam 2:18). “Cry aloud,” says one, “not with your tongue, but with your eyes; not with your words, but with your tears; for that is the prayer that makes the most forcible entry into the ears of the great God of heaven.” Penitent tears are ambassadors that cannot be denied, and that never return from the throne of grace without a gracious answer. Tears are a kind of silent prayer: though they say nothing, yet they obtain pardon; and though they do not plead a man’s cause, yet they obtain mercy at the hands of God. You+ see this in that great instance of Peter, who, though he said nothing that we read of, yet by weeping bitterly obtained mercy (Matt 26:75). I have read of Augustine, who, coming as a visitor to the house of a sick man, saw the room full of friends and kindred, all silent, yet all weeping: the wife sobbing, the children sighing, the kinsfolk lamenting, all mourning. At this Augustine uttered this short, sudden prayer: “Lord, what prayers do You hear, if not these?” “The Lord has heard my cry for mercy; the Lord receives my prayer” (Ps 6:9). God sometimes answers His people before they pray: “Even before they call, I will answer” (Isa 65:24). And sometimes while they are praying; so it follows in the same verse: “and while they are still speaking, I will hear.” So Isa 30:19 says: “He will surely be gracious when you cry for help; when He hears, He will answer you.” And sometimes after they have prayed, as the experience of all Christians can testify. Sometimes God neither hears nor receives a prayer, and this is the common case and lot of the wicked (Prov 1:28; Job 27:9; Isa 1:15). Sometimes God hears the prayers of His people, but does not answer them at once, as in the case of Paul (2 Cor 12:7–9). And sometimes God both hears and receives the prayers of His people, as He did David’s here. Now in this instance of David, as in a mirror, you+ may read at a glance the prevailing power of private prayer and of secret tears.

You+ may take another instance of this in Jonah: “From the belly of the fish, Jonah prayed to the Lord his God, saying: ‘In my distress I called to the Lord, and He answered me. From the belly of hell I called for help, and You heard my voice. For You cast me into the deep, into the heart of the seas, and the current swirled about me; all Your breakers and waves swept over me. … The waters engulfed me to take my life; the watery depths closed around me; the seaweed wrapped around my head. … As my life was fading away, I remembered the Lord. My prayer went up to You, to Your holy temple.’ … And the Lord commanded the fish, and it vomited Jonah onto dry land” (Jonah 2:1–3, 5, 7, 10). When Jonah was all alone, and in the midst of many dangers and deaths; when he was in the whale’s belly, indeed, in the belly of hell (so called because it was horrid and hideous, deep and dismal), yet even then private prayer fetches him out of it. Let a man’s dangers be ever so many and ever so great, yet secret prayer has a certain omnipotence in it that will deliver him out of them all. In multiplied afflictions, private prayer prevails most with God. In the very midst of drowning, secret prayer will keep both head and heart above water. On Jonah’s private prayer, God sends out His writ of command, and the fish serves Jonah for a ship to sail safely to shore. When the case is quite desperate, even then private prayer can do much with God. Private prayer has such power that it can open the doors of leviathan, as you+ see in this great instance, though that is reckoned a thing that cannot be done (Job 41:14).

You+ have another instance of the prevailing power of private prayer in 2 Kings 4:32–33: “When Elisha reached the house, there was the boy lying dead on his bed. So he went in, shut the door behind the two of them, and prayed to the Lord.” Privacy is a good help to fervency in prayer. “Then Elisha got on the bed and lay on the boy, mouth to mouth, eye to eye, and hand to hand. As he stretched himself out over him, the boy’s body became warm. Elisha turned away and paced back and forth across the room. Then he got on the bed and stretched himself out over the boy again, and the boy sneezed seven times and opened his eyes” (vv. 34–35). Oh, the power, the prevailing power, the omnipotence of private prayer, that raises the dead to life! And the private prayer of Elijah had the same effect in raising the son of the widow of Zarephath to life (1 Kgs 17:18ff). The great prevailing power of Moses’ private prayers you+ may read in the following scriptures: “Soon the people began to complain about their hardship in the hearing of the Lord, and when He heard them, His anger was kindled, and fire from the Lord blazed among them and consumed the outskirts of the camp. And the people cried out to Moses, and he prayed to the Lord, and the fire died down” (Num 11:1–2). Moses by private prayer rules and overrules with God; he was so powerful with God in private prayer that he could have what he wanted from God (see also Num 21:7–9; Ps 106:23; Exod 32:9–14; Exod 14:15–17). You+ may see the same in Nehemiah 1:11 compared with 2:4–8. So Luther, seeing the cause of God and the work of reformation greatly strained and in danger, went into his closet, and never stopped wrestling with God until he had received a gracious answer from heaven. On this he came out of his closet to his friends, leaping and triumphing, with vicimus, vicimus, “We have overcome, we have overcome,” in his mouth. It is observed that at that very time a proclamation came out from Charles the Fifth that no one should be harassed any further for the profession of the gospel. At another time, Luther, being in private prayer for a sick friend of his who was a great comfort and help to him, had a particular answer that he would recover. On this he was so confident that he sent word to his friend that he would certainly recover; and so it turned out. And so Latimer prayed with great zeal for three things: 1) that Queen Elizabeth might come to the crown; 2) that he might seal the truth with his heart’s blood; and 3) that the gospel might be restored once again, once again, which he expressed with great vehemence of spirit. God heard him in all three.

Constantine commanded that his image should be engraved, not like other emperors, leaning in their armor, but in a posture of prayer, kneeling, to show the world that he won more by secret prayer than by open battles.

Mr. Dod reports that many good people had often sought the Lord on behalf of a woman who was possessed by the devil, and yet could not prevail. At last they appointed a day for fasting and prayer, and at that time a poor woman came to the door of the room where the service had begun and asked to come in; but because she was poor they would not let her in. On that, the poor woman knelt behind the door and sought God by prayer. But she had not prayed long before the evil spirit raged, roared, and cried out in the possessed woman, “Take away the old woman behind the door, for I must be gone; take away the old woman behind the door, for I must be gone.” And so, by the old woman’s prayers behind the door, he was cast out. Oh, the prevailing power of prayer behind the door! And so you+ see by all these great instances the great prevailing power of private prayer.

Like Saul’s sword and Jonathan’s bow, private prayer never returns empty when both the person who prays and the act of praying are as they should be. It hits the mark; it carries the day with God. It pierces the walls of heaven, though they are made of brass and iron like those of Gaza (Isa 45:2). Oh, who can express the powerful oratory of private prayer!

9. Secret Duties Are the Most Soul-Enriching

Ninthly, consider that secret duties are the most soul-enriching duties. Just as secret meals make fat bodies, so secret duties make fat souls; and as secret trade brings in great earthly riches, so secret prayers make many rich in spiritual blessings and heavenly riches. Private prayer is that secret key of heaven that unlocks all the treasures of glory to the soul. God usually gives the best riches and the sweetest blessings to His people when they are in their closets on their knees. Just as the warmth that the chicks find by sitting close under the hen’s wings nurtures them, so the graces of the saints are enlivened and nurtured and strengthened by the sweet, secret influences that their souls come under when they are in their closet communion with God. Private prayer, conscientiously performed, is the secret key of heaven that has unlocked treasures and secrets that the craftiest devil has never had the skill to find out. Private prayer is the midwife that delivers the choicest blessings and the greatest riches to us.

Certainly, no one is so rich in gracious experiences as those who most exercise themselves in closet duties. “This poor man cried,” says David, “and the Lord … saved him from all his troubles” (Ps 34:6). David, pointing to himself, tells us that he “cried,” that is, silently and secretly, as Moses did at the Red Sea, and as Nehemiah did in the presence of the king of Persia. “And the Lord … saved him from all his troubles” (v. 6; Exod 14:15; Neh 1:11; 2:4). And oh, how much these deliverances added to his experiences! O my friends, the tender dew that falls in the silent night makes the grass and herbs and flowers flourish and grow more abundantly than great showers of rain that fall in the day. Just so, secret prayer will make the sweet herbs of grace and holiness grow and flourish in the soul more abundantly than all those more open, public, and visible duties of religion, which are too, too often mingled and mixed with the sun and wind of pride and hypocrisy.

Beloved, you+ know that a favorite at court often gets more by one secret petition, by one private request to his prince, than a tradesman or a merchant gets by twenty years’ labor and pains. So a Christian often gets more by one secret petition, by one private request to the King of kings, than many others do by trading long in the more public duties of religion. O sirs, remember that in private prayer we have a far greater advantage for exercising our own gifts and graces and talents than we have in public; for in public we only hear others exercise their talents and gifts. In public duties we are more passive, but in private duties we are more active. Now, the more our gifts and talents and graces are exercised, the more they are strengthened and increased. All acts strengthen habits. The more sin is put into practice, the more it is strengthened. And so it is with our gifts and graces: the more they are exercised, the more they are strengthened.

10. All Christians Have Their Secret Sins

Tenthly, take many things together. All Christians have their secret sins. “Who can understand his errors? Cleanse me from secret faults” (Ps 19:12). These are faults secret not only to other men, but to himself: even such secret sins as grew from errors that he did not understand. It is common to every man to err, and then to be ignorant of his errors. Many sins I see in myself, he says, and there are more that I cannot see, that I cannot find out; indeed, I think, he says, that every man’s sins run beyond his reckoning. There is not the best, the wisest, nor the holiest man in the world who can give a full and complete list of his sins. “Who can understand his errors?” This question has the force of an affirmation: “Who can?” “No man!” No, not the most perfect and innocent man in the world. Friends, who can count up the secret sinful imaginations, the secret sinful inclinations, or the secret pride, the secret blasphemies, the secret hypocrisies, the secret atheistic risings, the secret murmurings, the secret frettings, the secret discontents, the secret insolences, the secret filthinesses, the secret unbelief that God might charge on his soul every day? If the best and holiest man on earth had only his secret sins written on his forehead every day, it would not only put him to a crimson blush, but it would make him pull his hat over his eyes, or cover his face with a double scarf. “Whatever prayer or petition is made by any man or by all Your people Israel, each knowing the plague of his own heart” (1 Kgs 8:38). Sin is the greatest plague in the world, but it is never more dangerous than when it reaches the heart. Now, secret sins commonly lie nearest the heart, the fountain from which they draw a living, immediate, and continual supply. Secret sins are as near to original sin as the first droppings are to the spring head. And as every secret sin lies nearest the heart, so every secret sin is the plague of the heart. Now, as secret diseases are not to be laid open to everyone, but only to the wise physician, so our secret sins, which are the secret plagues, the secret diseases of our souls, are not to be laid open to everyone, but only to the Physician of souls, Who alone is able both to cure them and to pardon them. And as all Christians have their secret sins, so all Christians have their secret temptations (2 Cor 12:8–9). And as they have their secret temptations, so they have their secret needs; indeed, many times they have such particular and personal needs that there is not one in the congregation, nor one in the family, who has the same. And as they have their secret needs, so they have their secret fears, and secret snares, and secret straits, and secret troubles, and secret doubts, and secret misgivings. And how all these things call aloud to every Christian to be frequent and constant in secret prayer!

11. Christ Delights in His People’s Secret Prayers

Eleventhly, consider that Christ is greatly moved by and delighted in the secret prayers of His people. “O my dove, in the clefts of the rock, in the secret places of the stairs, let me see your face, let me hear your voice; for your voice is sweet, and your countenance is lovely” (Song 2:14). Christ watches His spouse when she is in the clefts of the rock. When she has gone off into a corner to pray, He looks on her with singular delight and with special intimations of His love. Nothing is more sweet, delightful, and welcome to Christ than the secret services of His people. Their secret breathings are like lovely songs to Him (Mal 3:4); their secret prayers in the clefts of the rock, or under the stairs, are like sweet incense to Jesus. The spouse withdraws to the secret places of the stairs not only for safety, but also for secrecy, so that she might pour out her soul into the bosom of her beloved more freely, without suspicion of hypocrisy. The great delight that parents take in the secret lispings and whisperings of their children is no delight compared with the delight Christ takes in the secret prayers of His people. And therefore, if you+ want to be friends and furtherers of Christ’s delight, be much in secret prayer.

12. God Has Chosen You to Reveal His Secrets To

Twelfthly, consider that you+ are the only persons in all the world whom God has chosen to reveal His secrets to. “No longer do I call you+ servants, for a servant does not understand what his master is doing. But I have called you+ friends, because everything I have learned from My Father I have made known to you+” (John 15:15). Everything that God the Father had imparted to Christ as mediator, to be revealed to His servants, He made known to His disciples as to His bosom friends. Christ loves His people as friends, He treats them as friends, and He opens His heart to them as friends. There is nothing in the heart of Christ that concerns the internal and eternal welfare of His friends that He does not reveal to them. He reveals Himself, His love, His eternal goodwill, the mysteries of faith, and the secrets of His covenant to His friends. Christ loves to share with His friends things that are not commonly and popularly known. Christ will reveal the secrets of His mind, the secrets of His love, the secrets of His thoughts, the secrets of His heart, and the secrets of His purposes, to all His bosom friends. Samson could not hide his mind, his secrets, from Delilah, though it cost him his life (Judg 16:15–17); and do you+ think that Christ can hide His mind, His secrets, from those for whom He has laid down His life? Surely not. O sirs! Christ is

  • a friend in all things,
  • an omnipotent friend, an almighty friend. He is called Almighty no fewer than thirty times in the book of Job; He can do more than words can express and more than minds can grasp.
  • He is an omniscient friend.
  • He is an omnipresent friend.
  • He is an unfailing friend.
  • He is an independent friend.
  • He is an unchangeable friend.
  • He is a watchful friend.
  • He is a tender and compassionate friend.
  • He is a close and faithful friend.

Therefore, He cannot help opening and unbosoming Himself to all His bosom friends. To be reserved and secretive is against the very law of friendship. Faithful friends are very free in imparting their thoughts, their minds, their secrets, to one another. A real friend counts nothing worth knowing unless he makes it known to his friends. He lays open his greatest and most inward secrets to his friends. Job calls his friends “inward friends,” or the men of his secrets (Job 19:19). All Christ’s friends are inward friends; they are the men of His secrets. “His secret is with the righteous” (Prov 3:32); that is, His covenant and fatherly affection, which is hidden and secret from the world. He who is righteous in secret, where no man sees him, is the righteous man to whom God will impart His closest secrets, as to His dearest bosom friend. It is only to a bosom friend that we will unbosom ourselves. “The secret of the Lord is with those who fear Him, and He reveals His covenant to them” (Ps 25:14).

Now, there are three sorts of divine secrets:

(1) First, there are secrets of providence, and these He reveals to the righteous and to those who fear Him (Ps 107:43; Hos 14:9). The prophet Amos speaks of these secrets of providence: “Surely the Lord God does nothing without revealing His secret to His servants the prophets” (Amos 3:7). Micah knew the secret of the Lord concerning Ahab, which neither Zedekiah nor any other of the false prophets knew. “And the Lord said, ‘Shall I hide from Abraham what I am about to do?’” (Gen 18:17). The destruction of Sodom was a secret that lay in the bosom of God; but Abraham being a bosom friend, God imparts this secret to him (vv. 19-21). Abraham was a friend, a faithful friend, a friend in a special way (Jas 2:23); and therefore God makes him a member both of His court and of His council. Oh, how greatly God stoops to His people! He speaks to them as a man would speak to his friend; and there are no secrets of providence that may be for their advantage that He will not reveal to His faithful servants. As all faithful friends have the same friends and the same enemies, so they freely share their secrets with one another; and so it was between God and Abraham.

(2) Secondly, there are the secrets of His kingdom, and these He reveals to His people. “The knowledge of the mysteries of the kingdom of heaven has been given to you+, but not to them…At that time Jesus declared, ‘I praise You, Father, Lord of heaven and earth, because You have hidden these things from the wise and learned, and revealed them to little children’” (Matt 13:11; 11:25). “Let us not think,” says Jerome, “that the gospel is in the words of Scripture, but in the sense; not in the outside, but in the marrow; not in the leaves of words, but in the root of reason.” Augustine humbly begged God that, if it were His pleasure, He would send Moses to him to interpret some of the more obscure and intricate passages in his book of Genesis.
