There are many choice, secret, hidden, and mysterious truths and doctrines in the gospel which Christ reveals to His people, and to which this poor, blind, ignorant world is a stranger. There are many secrets wrapped up in the plainest truths and doctrines of the gospel, which no one can effectually open and reveal but the Spirit of the Lord, who “searches all things, even the deep things of God” (1 Cor 2:10). There are many secrets and mysteries in the gospel that all the learning and labor in the world can never give a man insight into. There are many who know the doctrine of the gospel, the history of the gospel, who are utter strangers to the secrets of the gospel. There is a secret power, a secret authority, a secret efficacy, a secret prevailing power, a secret goodness, a secret sweetness in the gospel, that none experience but those to whom the Lord is pleased to impart gospel secrets. “Seal the law among my disciples” (Isa 8:16; 29:11–12). The law of God is to wicked men a sealed book that they cannot understand (Dan 12:9–10). It is like blotted paper that they cannot read. Just as a private letter to a friend contains secret matter that no one else may read, because it is sealed, so the law of grace is sealed up under the privy seal of heaven, so that no man can open it or read it except Christ’s faithful friends, to whom it is sent. The whole Scripture, says Gregory, is only one entire letter sent from the Lord Christ to His beloved spouse on earth. The rabbis say that there are four keys that God keeps at His belt: 1) the key of the clouds; 2) the key of the womb; 3) the key of the grave; 4) the key of food. I may add a fifth key that is at His belt, and that is the key of the Word, the key of the Scripture; and none can turn this key but the One “who holds the key of David. What He opens no one can shut, and what He shuts no one can open” (Rev 3:7).

O sirs! God reveals Himself and His mind and will and truth to His people in a more friendly and intimate way than He does to others. “He replied, ‘The mystery of the kingdom of God has been given to you+, but to those on the outside everything is expressed in parables’…He replied, ‘The knowledge of the mysteries of the kingdom of God has been given to you+, but to others I speak in parables, so that though seeing, they may not see; though hearing, they may not understand’” (Mark 4:11; Luke 8:10). Great theologians, and profound scholars, and deep-studied but unsanctified divines may know much of the doctrines of the gospel, and warmly commend the doctrines of the gospel, and dispute much for the doctrines of the gospel, and glory much in the doctrines of the gospel, and take great pains to dress and trim up the doctrines of the gospel with the flowers of rhetoric or eloquence—though it is much better to present truth in her native plainness than to hang her ears with counterfeit pearls (the Word, without human adornments, is like the stone Garamantides that has drops of gold in itself, enough to enrich the believing soul). Yet the special, spiritual, powerful, and saving knowledge of the doctrines of the gospel is a secret, a mystery, indeed, a hidden mystery to them (Rom 16:25; 1 Cor 2:7).

Chrysostom compares the mysteries of Christ, with regard to the wicked, to a written book that the ignorant man can neither read nor spell. He sees the cover, the leaves, and the letters, but he does not understand the meaning of what he sees. He compares the mystery of grace to a dictated letter, which an uneducated person looks at but cannot read, cannot understand. He knows it is paper and ink, but the sense, the matter, he does not know, he does not understand. So unsanctified persons, however learned they are, and though they may perceive the bark of the mystery of Christ, yet do not perceive, do not understand, the mystery of grace, the inward sense of the Spirit in the blessed Scriptures. Though the devil is the greatest scholar in the world, and has more learning than all the men in the world have, yet there are many thousands of secrets and mysteries in the gospel of grace that he does not know really, spiritually, feelingly, efficaciously, powerfully, thoroughly, savingly.

Oh, but now Christ makes Himself, His mind, His grace, His truth, known to His people in a more clear, full, intimate, and friendly way. “For You, O Lord of Hosts, the God of Israel, have revealed this to Your servant” (2 Sam 7:27). So you+ read it in your+ Bibles; but the Hebrew reads: “Lord, You have revealed this to the ear of Your servant.” Now, the emphasis lies in the words “to the ear,” which are left out in your+ Bibles. When God makes Himself known to His people, He reveals things to their ears, as we do with a friend who is intimate with us: we speak a thing into his ear. Jesus Christ speaks many a secret into the ears of His servants that others never come to know. “For God, who said, ‘Let light shine out of darkness,’ made His light shine in our hearts to give us the light of the knowledge of the glory of God in the face of Jesus Christ” (2 Cor 4:6).

The six distinct gradations in this scripture are worthy of our most serious consideration. Here is:

First, knowledge;

Second, the knowledge of the glory of God;

Third, the light of the knowledge of the glory of God;

Fourth, shining;

Fifth, shining in our hearts; and,

Sixth, shining in our hearts in the face of Jesus Christ.

And so you+ see that the Lord reveals the secrets of Himself, His kingdom, His truth, His grace, His glory, to the saints.

(3) Thirdly, there are the secrets of His favor, the secrets of the special love that He bears to them, the secret purposes of His heart to save them. And these are those great secrets, those “deep things of God,” which none can reveal “except the Spirit of God” (1 Cor 2:10–11). Now these great secrets, these deep things of God, God reveals to His people by His Spirit. “But God has revealed them to us by the Spirit. The Spirit searches all things, even the deep things of God. For who among men knows the thoughts of man except his own spirit within him? So too, no one knows the thoughts of God except the Spirit of God. We have not received the spirit of the world, but the Spirit who is from God, that we may know the things that God has freely given us” (1 Cor 2:10–12). Now what are the things that God has freely given us, but our election, calling, justification, sanctification, and glorification? And why has God given us His Spirit, but that we should know “the things that God has freely given us” (v. 12)? Some understand by “secret” in the 25th Psalm a particular assurance of God’s favors, by which happiness is secured to us, both for the present and for the future. They understand by “secret” the sealing of the Spirit, the hidden manna, the white stone, and the new name written on it, which no one knows but he who has it. And the words “He reveals His covenant to them” seem to mean as much (Ps 25:14); for what greater secret can God impart to His people than opening the covenant of grace to them in its freeness, fullness, sureness, sweetness, suitableness, and everlastingness, and sealing up to them His good pleasure and all the spiritual and eternal blessings of the covenant? Those who love and serve the Lord will be of His cabinet council; they will know His soul secrets, and be admitted into a very gracious intimacy and friendship with Him. “Whoever has My commandments and keeps them is the one who loves Me. The one who loves Me will be loved by My Father, and I will love him and reveal Myself to him. Judas (not Iscariot) asked Him, ‘Lord, why are You going to reveal Yourself to us and not to the world?’ Jesus replied, ‘If anyone loves Me, he will keep My word. My Father will love him, and We will come to him and make Our home with him’” (John 14:21–23). God and Christ will keep house with them, and make known the secrets of Their love to those who are observant of Their commands. And so you+ see that the saints are the only persons to whom God will reveal the secrets of His providence, the secrets of His kingdom, and the secrets of His love. Christ came out of the bosom of His Father, and He opens all the secrets of His Father only to His bosom friends.

Now what an exceedingly high honor it is for God to open the secrets of His love, the secrets of His promises, the secrets of His providences, the secrets of His counsels, and the secrets of His covenant to His people! Tiberius Caesar thought no man fit to know his secrets. And among the Persians only noblemen, lords, and dukes might share in state secrets. They regarded secrecy as a godhead, a divine thing, as Ammianus Marcellinus affirms. But God has put such honor on all His saints that He makes them lords and nobles, and the only statesmen privy to the secrets of the court of heaven. The highest honor and glory that earthly princes can put on their subjects is to share their greatest secrets with them. Now the King of kings has put this high honor and glory on His people. “The secret of the Lord is with those who fear Him, and He reveals His covenant to them” (Ps 25:14). It was a high honor to Elisha that he could tell the secrets that were spoken in the king’s bedroom (2 Kgs 6:12). Oh, what an honor it must be, then, for the saints to know the secrets that are spoken in the presence chamber of the King of kings!

Now I appeal to the very consciences of all who fear the Lord: is it not a just, fair, righteous, and necessary thing that the people of God should freely and fully lay open all the secrets of their hearts before the Lord, Who has honored them so highly that He reveals the secrets of His providence, kingdom, and favor to them? Yes, I appeal to all serious and sincere Christians: is it not against the light and law of nature, and against the law of love and the law of friendship, to be reserved and secretive, indeed, to hide our secrets from Him Who reveals His greatest and His choicest secrets to us? And if it is, why then do you+ not lay open in secret all your+ secret sins, and secret needs, and secret desires, secret fears, etc., to Him who sees in secret? You+ know that all secrets are to be shared only in secret. Only utter fools will share secrets on a stage or in front of many people.

13. Private Prayer in Times of Great Straits and Trials

Consider that in times of great straits and trials, in times of great afflictions and persecutions, private prayer is the Christian’s food and drink. It is his chief city of refuge; it is his shelter and hiding place in a stormy day. When the saints have been driven by violent persecutions into holes, and caves, and dens, and deserts, and howling wildernesses, private prayer has been their food and drink, and under Christ their only refuge. When Esau came out with hostile intentions against Jacob, secret prayer was Jacob’s refuge. “When the messengers returned to Jacob, they said, ‘We went to your brother Esau, and now he is coming to meet you—he and four hundred men with him,’” all cutthroats. “In great fear and distress, Jacob divided his people into two camps, as well as the flocks and herds and camels. He thought, ‘If Esau comes and attacks one camp, then the other camp can escape’” (Gen 32:6–8). When all is at stake, it is Christian prudence to save what we can, though we cannot save what we would. Then Jacob said, “O God of my father Abraham, God of my father Isaac, the Lord who told me, ‘Go back to your country and to your kindred, and I will make you prosper’” (v. 9). Promises must be prayed over in private. God loves to be sued on His own bond when He and His people are alone. “Please deliver me from the hand of my brother Esau, for I am afraid that he may come and attack me, and the mother with the children” (v. 11)—or “upon the children,” meaning he will put all to death. Some take the words to be a metaphor drawn from fowlers, who kill and carry off the young and the mother birds together, contrary to that old law (Deut 22:6). Others say it is a phrase that most vividly represents the tenderness of a mother who, seeing her children in distress, does not spare her own body or life, but risks them for her children’s preservation by putting herself between, even to be massacred together with them and upon them (Hos 10:14). When Jacob and all that was near and dear to him were in imminent danger of being cut off by Esau and the men of blood who were with him, he turns to private prayer as his only city of refuge against the rage and malice of the mighty.

And so, when Jeremiah was in a solitary and loathsome dungeon, private prayer was his food and drink; it was his only city of refuge. “While Jeremiah was still confined in the courtyard of the guard, the word of the Lord came to him a second time: Thus says the Lord who made the earth, the Lord who formed it and established it, the Lord is His name: Call to Me, and I will answer and show you great and unsearchable,” or hidden, “things you do not know” (Jer 33:1–3). When Jeremiah was in a lonesome, loathsome prison, God encourages him by private prayer to seek further disclosures and revelations of the choice and singular favors that He purposed to confer on His people in future times.

“So the Lord brought against them the military commanders of the king of Assyria, who captured Manasseh, put a hook in his nose, bound him with bronze shackles,” or chains, “and took him to Babylon. And in his distress, Manasseh sought the favor of the Lord his God and earnestly humbled himself before the God of his fathers. And when he prayed to Him, the Lord received his plea and heard his petition; so He brought him back to Jerusalem and to his kingdom. Then Manasseh knew that the Lord is God” (2 Chr 33:11–13). When Manasseh was in shackles in his enemy’s country, when he was stripped of all his princely glory and led captive into Babylon, he turns to private prayer as his only city of refuge; and by this means he prevails with God for his restoration to his crown and kingdom.

Private prayer is a city of refuge that no power or plotting, no craft or cruelty, no violence or force, is ever able to capture. Though the joint prayers of the people of God together were often obstructed and hindered in the times of the ten persecutions, the persecutors were never able to obstruct or hinder secret prayer, private prayer. When men and devils have done their worst, every Christian will be able to keep up his private trade with heaven. Private prayer will shelter a Christian against all the national, domestic, and personal storms and tempests that may threaten him. When a man is lying on a sickbed alone, or when a man is in prison alone, or when a man is left with Job on the dunghill alone, or when a man is banished with John for the testimony of Jesus to this or that island alone, oh, then private prayer will be his food and drink, his shelter, his hiding place, his heaven. When all other trades fail, this trade of private prayer will hold good.

14. God Is Omnipresent

Fourteenthly, consider that God is omnipresent. We cannot get into any blind hole, dark corner, or secret place where the Lord does not have an eye; the Lord will keep us company there. “And your Father, who sees in secret, will reward you openly” (Matt 6:6, 18). A saint cannot creep into the darkest, dirtiest hole in the world without God having a favorable eye there. God never lacks an eye to see our secret tears, or an ear to hear our secret cries and groans, or a heart to grant our secret requests; and therefore we ought to pour out our souls to Him in secret. “O Lord, my every desire is before You; my groaning is not hidden from You” (Ps 38:9). However confused our private desires are, however broken our private requests are, and however much our private groanings are hidden from men, yet God eyes them all, God records them all, and God puts them all on file in heaven, and will one day crown them with glorious answers and returns. We cannot sigh out a prayer in secret without Him seeing us; we cannot lift up our eyes to Him at midnight without Him observing us. The eye that God has on His people when they are in secret is such a special, tender eye of love that it opens His ear, His heart, and His hand for their good. “For the eyes of the Lord are on the righteous, and His ears are open to their prayers”—or as the Greek has it, “His ears are to their prayers” (1 Pet 3:12). If their prayers are so faint that they cannot reach up as high as heaven, then God will bow the heavens and come down to their prayers.

God’s eye is on every secret sigh, and every secret groan, and every secret tear, and every secret desire, and every secret pant of love, and every secret breathing of soul, and every secret melting and working of heart. All this should encourage us to be much in secret duties, in closet services. As a Christian is never out of the reach of God’s hand, so he is never out of the view of God’s eye. If a Christian cannot hide himself from the sun, which is God’s minister of light, how impossible it will be for him to hide himself from Him Whose eyes are ten thousand times brighter than the sun! In every private duty, a Christian is always under the eye of God’s omniscience. When we are in the darkest hole, God has windows into our breasts and observes all the secret actions of our inner man (1 Tim 2:8). The eye of God is not confined to this place or that, to this company or that. God has an eye on His people as much when they are alone as when they are among a multitude, as much when they are in a corner as when they are in a crowd. Diana’s temple was burned down when she was busy at Alexander’s birth and could not be in two places at once. But God is present both in paradise and in the wilderness, both in the family and in the closet, both in public and in private at the same time. God is an omnipresent God; He is everywhere. “There is nowhere where God is not.” As He is enclosed in no place, so He is shut out of no place. “Can a man hide in secret places where I cannot see him?” declares the Lord (Jer 23:24). “The eyes of the Lord are in every place, observing the evil and the good,” or, “contemplating the evil and the good,” as the Hebrew may be read (Prov 15:3). Now, to contemplate is more than simply to observe, for contemplation adds to a simple perception a deeper degree of knowledge, entering into the very inside of a matter. And so indeed God discerns the very inward intentions of the heart and the most secret stirrings of the spirit. God is an infinite and immense being Whose center is everywhere and Whose circumference is nowhere. Now, if our God is omnipresent, then wherever we are, our God is present with us. If we are in prison alone with Joseph, our God is present with us there; or if we are in exile alone with David, our God is present with us there; or if we are alone in our closets, our God is present with us there. God sees us in secret, and therefore let us seek His face in secret. Heaven is God’s palace, but it is not His prison.

15. He Who Neglects the Closet Is Neglected in Public

He who willingly neglects private prayer will certainly be neglected in his public prayer. He who will not call on God in secret will find by sad experience that God will neither hear him nor regard him in public. The lack of private duties is the great reason why the hearts of many are so dead and dull, so formal and carnal, so barren and unfruitful under public ordinances. Oh, that Christians would seriously lay this to heart! Certainly, the man or woman who is most frequent in private has the best heart in public. Those who are most conscientiously exercised in closet duties make the most gains in public ordinances. No man’s graces rise so high, nor do any man’s experiences rise so high, nor does any man’s communion with God rise so high, nor do any man’s divine enjoyments rise so high, nor do any man’s springs of comfort rise so high, nor do any man’s hopes rise so high, nor do any man’s talents and gifts rise so high, as they do in those who conscientiously wait on God in their closets before they wait on Him in the assembly of His people, and who, when they return from public ordinances, retire into their closets and look up to heaven for a blessing on the public means. It is certain that private duties fit the soul for public ordinances. He who makes it a matter of conscience to wait on God in private will find by experience that God will wonderfully bless public ordinances to him (Mic 2:7). My design is not to set up one ordinance of God above another, nor to make one ordinance of God clash with another—the public with the private, or the private with the public—but that every ordinance may have its proper place and right; for the desire of my soul is to prize every ordinance, and to praise every ordinance, and to practice every ordinance, and to make use of every ordinance, and to bless the Lord for every ordinance. But if you+ ever want to see the beauty and glory of God in His sanctuary, if you+ ever want public ordinances to be lovely and lively to your+ souls, if you+ ever want your+ drooping spirits revived, and your+ languishing souls refreshed, and your+ weak graces strengthened, and your+ strong corruptions weakened under public ordinances, be more careful and conscientious in performing closet duties (Ps 63:1–3). Oh, how strong in grace! Oh, how victorious over sin! Oh, how dead to the world! Oh, how alive to Christ! Oh, how fit to live! Oh, how prepared to die many a Christian might have been, had they only been more frequent, serious, and conscientious in the discharge of closet duties! And yet I do think there is a truth in that saying of Bede—the word church being rightly understood—that he who does not come willingly to church will one day go unwillingly to hell.

16. The Times in Which We Live Call for Secret Prayer

Consider that the times in which we live call aloud for secret prayer. Hell seems to have broken loose, and men to have turned into devils incarnate. Land-destroying and soul-damning wickednesses walk up and down the streets with a harlot’s forehead, without the least check or control. “Yet you have a harlot’s forehead; you refuse to be ashamed… Are they ashamed of the abomination they have committed? No, they have no shame at all; they do not even know how to blush” (Jer 3:3; 6:15). They had sinned away shame instead of being ashamed of sin. Custom in sin had quite banished all sense of sin and all shame for sin, so that they would not allow nature to draw her veil of blushing before their great abominations. They were like Caligula, a wicked emperor, who used to say of himself that there was nothing in himself he loved better than that he could not be ashamed. The same words are repeated in Jeremiah 8:12. I will leave the prudent reader to judge how applicable these scriptures are to the present time.

But what does the prophet do when they were as bold in sin and as shameless as so many harlots? You+ may see that in Jeremiah 13:17: “But if you+ do not listen, my soul will weep in secret places,” or secrecies, “because of your+ pride; my eyes will weep bitterly and overflow with tears.” The Hebrew is “weeping weep,” or “shedding tears shed tears”; the doubling of the verb shows the bitter and grievous lamentation he would make for them. Now they had grown to such a height of sin and wickedness that they were above all shame and blushing; now they had grown so proud, so hardened, so obstinate, so rebellious, so mad on wickedness, that no blessings could melt them or win them, and no threatenings or judgments could in any way terrify them or stop them. The prophet goes into a corner; he withdraws into the most secret places, and there he weeps bitterly; there he weeps as if he were resolved to drown himself in his own tears. When the springs of sorrow rise high, a Christian turns his back on company and withdraws into places of the greatest privacy, so that he may vent his sorrow and grief before the Lord more freely and more fully.

Ah, England, England! What pride, lust, lasciviousness, licentiousness, wantonness, drunkenness, cruelties, injustice, oppressions, fornications, adulteries, falsehoods, hypocrisy, bribery, atheism, horrid blasphemies, and hellish impieties are now to be found rampant in your midst! Ah, England! England! How the Lord’s Sabbaths are profaned, pure ordinances despised, Scriptures rejected, the Spirit resisted and derided, the righteous reviled, wickedness favored, and Christ crucified afresh many thousand times a day by these cursed practices! Ah, England! England! If our forefathers were alive, how sadly they would blush to see such a horribly degenerate posterity as is to be found in your midst! How our forefathers’ hospitality is turned into riot and luxury, their frugality into pride and extravagance, their simplicity into cunning, their sincerity into hypocrisy, their charity into cruelty, their chastity into sexual immorality and wantonness, their sobriety into drunkenness, their plain dealing into dissembling, their works of compassion into works of oppression, and their love for the people of God into utter enmity against the people of God! And what is the voice of all these crying abominations, but: every Christian to his closet, every Christian to his closet, and there weep bitterly with weeping Jeremiah for all these great abominations by which God is openly dishonored. Oh, weep in secret for the sins of those who openly glory in their sins, which should be their greatest shame. Oh, blush in secret for those who are past all blushing for their sins; for who knows but that the whole land may fare the better for the sake of a few who are mourners in secret? But however it goes with the nation, those who mourn in secret for the abominations of the times may be confident that when sweeping judgments come upon the land, the Lord will hide them in the secret chambers of His providence. He will set a secret mark of deliverance on the foreheads of those who mourn in secret for the crying sins of the present day, as He did on theirs in Ezekiel 9:4–6.

17. Your Relations to the Lord Call for Secret Prayer

Consider that the near and dear relation in which you+ stand to the Lord calls aloud for secret prayer (John 15:14–15). You+ are His friends. Now, a true friend loves to visit his friend when he may find him alone and enjoy privacy with him. A true friend loves to pour out his heart into the bosom of his friend when he has him in a corner, or in the field, or under a hedge. You+ are His favorites. And what favorite is there who hides his secret from his prince? Do not all favorites open their hearts to their princes when they are alone? You+ are His children. And what true-hearted child is there who does not delight to be much with his father when he is alone, when nobody is by? Oh, how free and open children are when they have their parents alone, beyond what they are when company is present! You+ are the spouse of Christ. And what spouse, what wife, is there who does not love to be much with her husband when he is alone? True lovers are always at their best when they are most alone. “I belong to my beloved, and his desire is for me. Come, my beloved, let us go out to the fields; let us spend the night among the wildflowers. Let us go early to the vineyards to see if the vine has budded, if the blossom has opened, if the pomegranates are in bloom—there I will give you my love” (Song 7:10–12). The spouse of Christ is very eager to enjoy His company in the fields, so that, having her beloved alone, she might open her heart to Him more freely and more secretly. As wives, when they are walking alone with their husbands in the fields, are freer to open their minds and the secrets of their hearts than they are in their houses with their children and servants about them, so it was with the spouse.

Beyond all doubt, those who seldom or never converse with Christ in their closets, who are shy of Christ when they are alone, who never make a habit of paying Christ secret visits, have very great reason to question whether they are Christ’s real friends, favorites, children, spouse. Christ may say to very many in our days what Delilah said to Samson: “How can you say, ‘I love you,’ when you have failed to reveal to me the source of your great strength?” (Judg 16:15)—the revealing of which secret at last cost him his life. How can you+ say you+ love Me when you+ never acquaint Me with your+ secrets? How can you+ say you+ love Me when you+ never pay Me any private visits? How can you+ say that you+ are My friends, My faithful friends, My bosom friends, when you+ never unbosom yourselves+ to Me in private? How can you+ say that you+ are My favorites, when you+ can spend one month after another, and one quarter of a year after another, and yet not let Me know one of all your+ secrets, when every day you+ might have My ear in secret if you+ pleased? How can you+ say that you+ are My children, and yet be as secretive and reserved as you+ are? How can you+ say that you+ are My spouse, and that you+ lie in My bosom, and yet never take any delight in opening your+ hearts, your+ secrets, to Me when I am alone? What Alexander said to a man who bore his name but was a coward, “Either lay down the name of Alexander, or fight like Alexander,” I say to you+. Either be frequent in closet duties, as befits a Christian, or else lay down the name of a Christian. Either unbosom yourselves+ in secret to Christ as friends, favorites, children, spouses, or else lay down these names.

18. God’s Special Mark on Those Who Pray in Secret

Consider that God has set a special mark of favor, honor, and notice on those who have prayed in secret. You+ may see this in Moses (Exod 34:28), and in Abraham (Gen 21:33), and in Isaac (Gen 24:63), and in Jacob (Gen 32:24–29), and in David (Ps 55:16–17), and in Daniel (Dan 6:10), and in Paul (Acts 9:11), and in Cornelius (Acts 10:2, 4), and in Peter (Acts 10:9–12), and in Manasseh (2 Chr 33:18–19). God has put on record all these worthies who have exercised themselves in secret prayer, to their everlasting fame and honor. The Persians seldom write their king’s name except in letters of gold. God has written, so to speak, in letters of gold the names of those who have made it a matter of conscience to exercise themselves in secret prayer. The precious names of those who have devoted themselves to closet duties are like statues of gold, which the polluted breath of men can never stain. They are like so many shining suns that no clouds can darken. They are like so many sparkling diamonds that shine brightest in the darkest night. A Christian can never get into a hole, a corner, a closet, to pour out his soul before the Lord without the Lord taking honorable notice of him and setting a secret mark of favor on him (Ezek 9:4–6). And how this should stir up all Christians to be much with God alone! The Romans were very ambitious to win a great name, a great report, in this world; why then should Christians not be as divinely ambitious to win a good name, a good report, in the other world (Heb 11:39)? A good name is always better than a great name, and a name in heaven is infinitely better than a thousand names on earth, and the way to both of these is to be much with God in secret.

19. Satan Is a Very Great Enemy to Secret Prayer

Consider that Satan is a very great enemy to secret prayer. Secret prayer is a scourge, a hell, to Satan. Every secret prayer adds to the devil’s torment, and every secret sigh adds to his torment, and every secret groan adds to his torment, and every secret tear adds to his torment. When a child of God is on his knees in his secret addresses to God, oh, the strange thoughts, the earthly thoughts, the wandering thoughts, the distracted thoughts, the hideous thoughts, the blasphemous thoughts, that Satan often injects into his soul, and all to wean him from secret prayer, and to weary him of secret prayer! Sometimes he tells the soul that it is in vain to seek God in secret. At other times he tells the soul it is too late to seek God in secret, for the door of mercy is shut and there is no hope, no help, for the soul. Sometimes he tells the soul that it is enough to seek God in public; at other times he tells the soul that it is only an overscrupulous trick to seek the Lord in private. Sometimes he tells the soul that it is not elected, and therefore all its secret prayers will be rejected. At other times he tells the soul that it is sealed up to the day of wrath, and therefore a secret prayer can never reverse that seal. All this is to dishearten and discourage a poor Christian in his secret retreats. Sometimes Satan will throw the greatness of his sins in a poor Christian’s face; at other times he will throw in a Christian’s face the greatness of his unworthiness. Sometimes he will throw in a Christian’s face his lack of grace; at other times he will throw in a Christian’s face his lack of gifts to carry out such a duty as it should be carried out. Sometimes he will throw in a Christian’s face his former lack of freedom in secret prayer; at other times he will throw in a Christian’s face the small gains he makes by secret prayer. All this is to make the soul fall out of love with secret prayer, yes, to make the soul loathe secret prayer, so deadly an enemy is Satan to secret prayer. Oh, the strange fears, fancies, and notions that Satan often raises in the spirits of Christians when they are alone with God in a corner, and all to bring them to cast off private prayer!

Not the least of Satan’s designs is to interrupt a Christian in his private trade with God. Satan watches every movement a Christian makes. The Christian cannot turn into his closet, or creep into any hole to commune privately with his God, without Satan following hard at his heels, always injecting one thing or another into the soul, or else objecting one thing or another against the soul. A Christian could as easily count the stars of heaven and number the sands of the sea as number all the various devices and tricks that Satan uses to obstruct the soul’s private addresses to God. Now from the great opposition that Satan makes against private prayer, a Christian may safely conclude these five things:

(1) First, the excellence of private prayer. Certainly, if it were not an excellent thing for a man to be in secret with God, Satan would never rise up against it so.

(2) Second, the necessity of this duty. The more necessary any duty is to the internal and eternal welfare of a Christian, the more Satan will stir himself to blunt a Christian’s spirit in that duty.

(3) Third, the usefulness or profit that comes with a conscientious discharge of this duty. Where we are likely to gain most, there Satan loves to oppose most.

(4) Fourth, the prevailing power of private prayer. If there were not a kind of omnipotence in it, if it were not able to do wonders in heaven, and wonders on earth, and wonders in the hearts and lives and ways of men, Satan would never bear such a grudge against it as he does.

(5) Fifth, that God is highly honored by this duty, or else Satan would never be so greatly enraged against it. This is certain: the more glory God has from any service we do, the more Satan will strive by all his wiles and tricks either to draw us away from that service, or to interrupt us in that service so that God may have no honor, nor we any good, nor he himself any harm, by our private retreats.

20. You Are the Lord’s Secret Ones, His Hidden Ones

In the last place, consider that you+ alone are the Lord’s secret ones, His hidden ones; and therefore, if you+ do not apply yourselves+ to private prayer and to your+ secret retreats, so that you+ may enjoy God in a corner, no one will. It is only God’s hidden ones, His secret ones, who are given His Spirit and a principle of grace, and are prepared to wait on God in secret. “… you+ will be My treasured possession out of all the nations” (Exod 19:5). The Hebrew word segullah signifies God’s special jewels, God’s own ones, or God’s secret ones, whom He keeps in store for Himself and for His own special service and use. Princes lock up their most precious and costly jewels in secret with their own hands; and so does God with His. “For the Lord has chosen Jacob as His own, Israel as His treasured possession” (Ps 135:4), or as His secret gem. “With cunning they scheme against Your people and conspire against Your hidden ones” (Ps 83:3), or Your secret ones. They are so called partly because God hides them in the secret of His tabernacle (Ps 31:20), and partly because God sets as high a value on them as men do on their hidden treasure, their secret treasure. Indeed, He sets more store by them than by all the rest of the world. And so the world will know when God arises to avenge the wrongs and injuries that have been done to His secret ones.

Nor are there any on earth who know so much of the secrets of His love, of the secrets of His counsels, of the secrets of His purposes, of the secrets of His heart, as His secret ones do. Nor are there any in all the world who are under those secret influences, those secret aids, those secret inflowings, those secret anointings of the Spirit, as His secret ones are. And therefore, no wonder if God calls them again and again and again His “secret ones.” Now, what can be more fitting or more desirable than to see their natures and their practices answer to their names? They are the Lord’s secret ones, His hidden ones; therefore, how much it concerns them to be much with God in secret, and to hide themselves with God in a corner! Shall Nabal’s nature and practice answer to his name? “My lord should pay no attention to this scoundrel Nabal, for he lives up to his name: Nabal is his name, and folly accompanies him” (1 Sam 25:25). Nabal signifies a fool, a dolt, a churl; it denotes one who is void of wisdom and goodness. It signifies one whose mind, reason, judgment, and understanding are withered and decayed. Now, if you+ look into the story, you+ will find that as face answers to face, so Nabal’s nature and practice echoed and answered to his name. Why then should not our natures and practices answer to our names also? We are called the Lord’s secret ones, His hidden ones; and how much, therefore, it concerns us to be much with God in secret!

Why should there be any jarring or discord between our names and our practices? It is worth noting that the practice and conduct of other saints have matched their names. Isaac signifies laughter, and Isaac was a gracious son, a dutiful son, a son who kept clear of those abominations with which many of the patriarchs had defiled themselves, a son who proved a cause of laughter to his father and mother all their days. So Josiah signifies “the fire of the Lord,” and his practice answered to his name. Witness his pulling down of Jeroboam’s altar, and his burning of the vessels that were made for Baal, and his putting down the idolatrous priests whom the kings of Judah had set up, and his burning the Asherah pole at the brook Kidron, and his stamping it to powder, and his breaking down the houses of the male shrine prostitutes, and his defiling of the high places where the priests had burned incense, and his breaking the images in pieces, and cutting down the Asherah poles, and filling their places with the bones of men, etc. (1 Kgs 13:2; 2 Kgs 23:4–21). So Joshua signifies “a savior,” and his practice matched his name. Though he could not save his people from their sins, yet he often saved them from their sufferings. Great and many were the deliverances, the salvations, that were brought about through Joshua as God’s instrument, as all who have read the book of Joshua know. So John signifies “gracious,” and his practice matched his name. He was so gracious in his teaching and in his walk that he gained favor in the very eyes of his enemies.

By all these instances, and by many more that might be given, you+ see that other saints’ practices have matched their names. Therefore let every one of us see to it that our practices also match our names. As we are called the Lord’s secret ones, so let us be much with God in secret, so that there may be a blessed harmony between our names and our practices, and so that we may never come to regret that we have been called God’s secret ones, His “hidden ones” (Ps 83:3), and yet never made it a matter of conscience to maintain secret communion with God in our closets.

Thus you+ see that there are no fewer than twenty arguments to persuade you+ to closet prayer, and to maintain private communion with God in a corner.

Application

1. The Use and Application of All

The use and application of all follows.

Is it true that closet prayer, or private prayer, is such an indispensable duty, one that Christ Himself has laid on all who do not want to bear the woeful brand of hypocrites? Then this truth looks very sourly and sadly on these five kinds of people.

a. Those Who Put Off Secret Prayer Until They Are Moved to It by the Spirit

First, it looks sourly and sadly on all those who put off secret prayer, private prayer, until they are moved to it by the Spirit; for by this sad delusion many have been kept from secret prayer many weeks, many months—oh, that I did not have to say it—many years! Though it is a very fit season to pray when the Spirit moves us to pray, yet it is not the only season to pray (Isa 62:1; Ps 123:1–2; Gal 4:6). He who makes religion his business will pray daily for daily grace as surely as he prays daily for daily bread. “Then Jesus told them a parable about their need to pray always and not lose heart” (Luke 18:1). “Pray without ceasing” (1 Thess 5:17). “Pray in the Spirit always, with every kind of prayer and supplication. To this end, stay alert with all perseverance in your+ prayers for all the saints” (Eph 6:18). “Persistent in prayer” (Rom 12:12). The Greek is a metaphor taken from hunting dogs that never give up the chase until they have caught their prey. A Christian must not only pray but hold on in prayer until he has won the heavenly prize. We are always in need; and therefore we need to be praying always. The world is always alluring; and therefore we need to be always praying. Satan is always tempting; and therefore we need to be always praying. And we are always sinning; and therefore we need to be always praying. And we are always in danger; and therefore we need to be praying always. And we are dying always (1 Cor 15:31); and therefore we need to be praying always. Man’s whole life is only a lingering death. No sooner does man begin to live than he begins to die. When one man was asked why he prayed six times a day, he gave only this answer: “I must die, I must die, I must die.” Dying Christians need to be praying Christians, and those who are always dying need to be always praying. Certainly, prayerless families are graceless families, and prayerless persons are graceless persons (Jer 10:25). It would be ten thousand times better that we had never been born into the world than that we should go stillborn out of the world.

b. Those Who Do Not Pray at All, Either in Their Families or in Their Closets

Secondly, this truth looks sourly and sadly on those who do not pray at all, either in their families or in their closets. Among all God’s children, there is not one possessed with a mute devil. Prayerless persons are forsaken by God, blinded by Satan, hardened in sin, and with every breath they draw are liable to all temporal, spiritual, and eternal judgments. Prayer is that part of natural worship due to God which no one will deny but out-and-out atheists (Ps 14:1).

It is worth noting that among the worst of men, the Turks, and among the worst of the Turks, the Moors, it is by their law a just objection against any witness that he has not prayed six times in every full day, since it is their custom to pray six times a day.

(1) Before daybreak they pray for the day to come.

(2) When it is day, they give thanks for the day.

(3) At noon, they thank God for half the day past.

(4) After that, they pray for a good sunset.

(5) And after that, they thank God for the day past.

(6) And then, sixthly and lastly, they pray for a good night after their day.

Certainly, these very Moors will one day rise in judgment against those who cast off prayer, who live in total neglect of prayer, who let so many suns and moons rise and set over their heads without ever solemnly calling on God. I have read of a man who, when he was sick and afraid of death, fell to praying; and to move God to hear him, he told Him that he was no common beggar, that he had never troubled Him with his prayers before, and that if He would only hear him this time, he would never trouble Him again. This world is full of such profane, blasphemous, atheistic wretches.

c. Those Who Are All for Public Prayer, but Never Regard Private Prayer

Thirdly, this truth looks very sourly and sadly on those who are all for public prayer but never regard private prayer, who are all for going up to the temple but never care about going into their closets. It is the most obvious hypocrisy for a man to be very zealous for public prayer but very cold and careless about private prayer. He who claims to be conscientious in the one and does not make a matter of conscience of the other is a hypocrite through and through (Matt 23:5; 6:1, 2, 5). And the devil knows well enough how to make his profit from all such hypocrites, who are all for the prayers of the church but care nothing for private prayer, like Gallio (Acts 18:17). Those who perform all their private devotion in the church but not in their room show too great a disregard for the authority of Christ, who says, “When you pray, go into your closet.” He does not say, “When you pray, go to the church,” but, “When you pray, go into your closet.”

d. Those Who in Their Closets Pray with a Loud, Clamorous Voice

Fourthly, this truth looks sadly and sourly on those who in their closets pray with a loud, clamorous voice. A Christian should shut both the door of his closet and the door of his lips so tight that no one outside should hear what he says inside. “Go into your closet,” says Christ, “shut your door, and pray” (Matt 6:6). But why need a man shut his closet door if he may pray with a clamorous voice, if he makes such a noise that everyone in the street or everyone in the house may hear him? The hen, when she lays her eggs, gets into a hole, a corner; but then she makes such a noise with her cackling that she tells everyone in the house where she is and what she is about. Christians who imitate the hen in their closets pray rather to be seen, heard, and observed by men than out of any noble design to glorify God, or to pour out their souls before Him who sees in secret. Sometimes children, when they are vexed or afraid of the rod, will run behind the door or get into a dark hole, and there they will lie crying, and sighing, and sobbing, so that the whole house may know where they are.

Oh, it is a childish thing to cry, and sigh, and sob in our closets in such a way that we tell everyone in the house where we are and what work we are about. Well, Christians, for an effectual remedy for this evil, frequently and seriously consider these five things:

First, that God sees in secret.

Secondly, that God has a quick ear, and is taken more with the voice of the heart than with the clamor of the mouth. God can easily hear the most secret breathings of your soul. God is more attentive to the messages delivered by the heart than to those delivered only by the mouth. He who prays aloud in private seems to tell others that God does not understand the secret desires, and thoughts, and workings of His people’s hearts.

Thirdly, it is not fitting, it is not suitable nor beneficial, that anyone should be acquainted with our secret prayers but God and our own souls. Now it is as much our duty to look to what is beneficial as it is to look to what is permissible (2 Cor 8:10). “Everything is permissible for me, but not everything is beneficial” (1 Cor 6:12). “Everything is permissible, but not everything is beneficial. Everything is permissible, but not everything is edifying” (1 Cor 10:23). Now it is so far from being beneficial that it is very great folly for men to lay open their secret infirmities to others, who will sooner mock them than lift up a prayer for them.

Fourthly, loud prayers may be a hindrance and disturbance to others who may be busy near us in some religious or secular exercises.

Fifthly and lastly, Hannah prayed and yet did not speak a word. Her heart was full, but her voice was not heard (1 Sam 1:13). Moses prays and cries and yet does not utter a word. “Then the Lord said to Moses, ‘Why are you crying out to Me?’” (Exod 14:15). Moses did not cry out with an audible voice, but with inward sighs, and secret breathings, and wrestlings of soul. And these inward and secret cries, which made no noise, carried the day with God; for Moses is heard and answered, and his people are delivered. Oh, the prevailing power of those prayers that make no noise in the ears of others!

e. Those Who Hinder and Discourage Others from This Duty of Duties

Fifthly and lastly, this truth looks sourly and sadly on those who do all they can to hinder and discourage others from this duty of duties, private prayer, either by mocking or vilifying the duty, or by denying that it is a duty, or by their own daily neglect of this duty, or by denying those under them time and opportunity for the discharge of this duty. In Matthew 23:13 you+ have a woe pronounced against those who will neither go to heaven themselves nor allow others to go who are willing to enter into an everlasting rest. And so I say, Woe to those parents, and woe to those husbands, and woe to those masters and mistresses, who will neither pray in their closets themselves nor allow their children, nor their wives, nor their servants to pour out their souls before the Lord in a corner. Oh, sirs! How will you+ answer this to your+ consciences when you+ lie on a dying bed? And how will you+ answer it to the Judge of all the world when you+ stand before a judgment seat? Certainly, all their sins, and all their neglects, and all their spiritual losses that might have been prevented by their secret prayers, by their closet communion with God, will one day be charged to your+ account. And oh, that you+ were all so wise as to lay these things to heart, so that you+ may never again hinder any who are under your+ care or charge from private prayer!

But secondly, this may serve to exhort us to keep close to our closets, to be frequent and constant in private prayer, to be often with God in a corner. The twenty considerations already laid down may serve as so many motives to stir up your+ hearts to this noble and necessary duty.

2. The Main Objections Against Closet Prayer Answered

Objection 1: We Have Much Business upon Our Hands

But many will be ready to object and say, “We have much business on our hands, and we cannot spare time for private prayer. We have so much to do in our shops, and in our warehouses, and out among others, that we cannot spare time to wait on the Lord in our closets.”

Now to this objection I will give these eight answers, so that this objection may never have a resurrection in any of your+ hearts.

1. What Is Your Business Compared to That of Abraham, Moses, David, and Daniel?

First, what are all the business matters on your+ hands compared to the business and weighty affairs that lay on the hands of Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, Moses, David, Daniel, Elijah, Nehemiah, Peter, and Cornelius? And yet you+ find all these worthies exercising themselves in private prayer. And the king is commanded to read some part of God’s Word every day, notwithstanding all his great and weighty employments (Deut 17:18–20). Now certainly, sirs, your+ great business affairs are little more than zeros compared with theirs. If there were any on earth who might have pleaded an exemption from private prayer on account of business, of much business, of great business, these might have done it; but they were too honorable and too noble to neglect so choice a duty on account of much business. These noble hearts made all their public affairs bow to private prayer; they would never allow their public affairs to tread private prayer underfoot.

2. None Ever Prospered More Than Those Who Devoted Themselves to Private Prayer

Secondly, I answer that no men’s outward affairs ever prospered more than the affairs of those who devoted themselves to private prayer, notwithstanding their many and great worldly employments. Witness the prosperity and outward flourishing condition of Moses, Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, Nehemiah, David, Daniel, and Cornelius. These were much with God in their closets, and God blessed their blessings to them (Gen 22:17). How their cups overflowed! What remarkable favors God heaped on them and theirs! No families have been so prospered, protected, and graced as the families of those who have maintained secret communion with God in a corner (1 Chr 11:9).

Private prayer best speeds our temporal affairs. He who prays well in his closet will be sure to fare well in his shop, or at his plow, or in whatever else he turns his hand to (1 Tim 4:8). It is true, Abimelech was rich as well as Abraham, and Laban was rich as well as Jacob, and Saul was a king as well as David, and Julian was an emperor as well as Constantine; but it was only Abraham, Jacob, David, and Constantine who had their blessings blessed to them. All the rest had their blessings cursed to them (Prov 3:33; Mal 2:2). They had many good things, but they did not have “the favor of Him who dwelt in the burning bush” with what they had (Deut 33:16); and therefore all their blessings were only bitter-sweets to them. Though all the sons of Jacob returned from Egypt laden with grain, with money in their sacks, yet only Benjamin had the silver cup in the mouth of his sack. So though the men of the world have their grain and their money, etc., yet it is only God’s Benjamins who have the silver cup, the grace cup, “the cup of blessing,” as the apostle calls it, for their portion (1 Cor 10:16). Oh, sirs, if ever you+ would prosper and flourish in the world, if ever you+ would have your+ water turned into wine, your+ temporal blessings into spiritual benefits, be much with God in your+ closets.

3. You Fool Away an Hour a Day, and Yet Plead the Lack of Time

Thirdly, I answer: it is ten to one that the objector every day fools away, or trifles away, or idles away, or sins away, one hour of the day; why then should he plead the lack of time? Even those who toil and moil and busy themselves most in their worldly employments spend an hour or more a day to little or no purpose, either in gazing about, or in dallying, or toying, or courting, or in telling stories, or in busying themselves in other men’s affairs, or in idle visits, or in smoking a pipe, etc. Why then should these men not redeem an hour a day for private prayer out of the time they usually spend so vainly and idly? Can you+, for all your+ great worldly employments, find an hour in the day to catch flies in, as Domitian the emperor did, and to play the fool in? And can you+ not find an hour in the day to wait on God in your+ closets?

There were three special faults of which Cato declared he had seriously repented: one was going by water when he might have gone by land; another was trusting a secret to a woman’s bosom; but the chief was spending an hour unprofitably. This heathen will one day rise up in judgment against those who, notwithstanding their great employments, spend many hours a week unprofitably, and yet cry out with the Duke of Alva that they have so much to do on earth that they have no time to look up to heaven. It was a base and sordid spirit in King Sardanapalus, who spent much of his time among women, spinning and carding, time which should have been spent in ruling and governing his kingdom. So it is a base, sordid spirit in anyone to spend any of his time in toying and trifling, and then to cry out that he has so much business to do in the world that he has no time for closet prayer, no time to serve God, nor to save his own precious and immortal soul.

4. No Man Dares Plead This Before the Lord Jesus in the Great Day of Account

Fourthly, I answer: no man dares plead this objection before the Lord Jesus in the great day of account (Eccl 11:9; Rom 14:10; 2 Cor 5:10). Why then should any man be so childish and foolish, so ignorant and impudent, as to plead before men what cannot be pleaded before the judgment seat of Christ? Oh, sirs, as you+ love your+ souls, and as you+ would be happy forever, never fob off your+ own consciences, or others’, with any pleas, arguments, or objections now that you+ dare not acknowledge and stand by when you+ lie on a dying bed, and when you+ appear before the whole court of heaven, etc. In the great day of account, when the secrets of all hearts will be made manifest, and God will call men to a reckoning before angels, men, and devils for the neglect of private prayer, all guilty persons will be found speechless. Not a man or woman will be found who will dare to stand up and say, “Lord, I would have waited on You in my closet, but I had so much business to do in the world that I had no time to enjoy secret communion with You in a corner.” It is the greatest wisdom in the world to plead nothing by way of excuse in this our day that we dare not plead in the great day.

5. It Is Our Duty to Redeem Time from All Our Secular Business

Fifthly, I answer that it is our duty to redeem time from all our secular business for private prayer. All sorts of Christians, whether slave or free, rich or poor, high or low, superiors or inferiors, are expressly charged by God to redeem time for prayer, for private prayer, as well as for other holy exercises. “Devote yourselves+ to prayer, being watchful and thankful, as you+ pray also for us, that God may open to us a door for the word, so that we may proclaim the mystery of Christ, for which I am in chains” (Col 4:2–3).

But here some may object and say, “We have so much business to do in the world that we have no time for prayer.” The apostle answers this objection in verse 5: “Act wisely toward outsiders, redeeming the time.” “Redeeming the time, because the days are evil” (Eph 5:16)—ἐξαγοραζόμενοι τὸν καιρόν, that is, buying up, gaining the time. The words are a metaphor taken from merchants who prefer the least profit that may be gained to their pleasures or delights, closely following their business while the markets are at their best. A merchant, when he comes to a market or fair, takes the first season and opportunity of buying his goods. He does not put it off, at the risk of the evening or the next morning, in hope of a better bargain, but he makes use of the present season and buys before the market is over.

Others take the words this way: “Purchase at any cost all occasions and opportunities of doing good, so that by this you+ may in some measure redeem that precious jewel of time which you+ have lost in the past.” They are like travelers who have loitered on the way or stayed long at their inn: when they find night coming on, they quicken their pace and go as many miles in an hour as they did before in many. Though time let slip is in itself beyond recovery, yet in a moral sense it is counted as regained when men double their care, diligence, and efforts to redeem it. The best Christian is he who is the greatest monopolizer of time for private prayer. There is no Christian like him who redeems time from his worldly affairs and his lawful comforts and recreations to be with God in his closet. David, having tasted the sweetness, goodness, and graciousness of God, cannot stay in his bed, but will borrow some time from his sleep, so that he might take some turns in Paradise and pour out his soul in prayer and praises when no eye was open to see him, nor any ear open to hear him, but all were asleep around him (Ps 63:6). “At midnight I rise to give You thanks … I rise before dawn and cry for help” (Ps 119:62, 147). David was up and at private prayer before daybreak. David was no sluggish Christian, no slothful Christian, no lazy Christian. He used to be in his closet when others were sleeping in their beds. “My eyes anticipate the watches of night, that I may meditate on Your word … My soul waits for the Lord more than watchmen wait for the morning—more than watchmen wait for the morning” (Ps 119:148; 130:6). Just as the weary sentinel on a dark, cold, wet night waits and peeps, and peeps and waits, for the appearance of the morning, so David waited and peeped, and peeped and waited, for the first and fittest season to pour out his soul before God in a corner. David would never allow his worldly business to crowd out holy exercises. He would often borrow time from the world for private prayer, but he would never borrow time from private prayer to spend it on the world.

Mr. Bradford, the martyr, counted any hour lost in which he did not do some good, either with his pen, his tongue, or his purse.

Ignatius, when he heard a clock strike, used to say, “Now I have one hour more to answer for.”

So the early Christians would redeem some time from their sleep so that they might be with God in their closets, as Clement observes.

And I have read of Theodosius the emperor that, after the various worldly employments relating to his civil affairs in the daytime were over, he used to consecrate the greatest part of the night to the study of the Scriptures and private prayer. For this purpose he had a lamp so skillfully made that it supplied itself with oil, so that he might in no way be interrupted in his private retreats.

That time ought to be redeemed is a lesson that has been taught by the very heathen themselves. It was the saying of Pittacus, one of the seven wise men, “Know time; do not lose a minute.” And so Theophrastus used to say, “Time is a costly thing.” And so Seneca: “Time is the only thing,” he says, “of which we can innocently be covetous; and yet there is nothing of which many are more lavishly and extravagantly wasteful.” And Chrestus, a sophist of Byzantium in the time of the emperor Hadrian, was much given to wine; yet he always counted time so precious that when he had misspent his time all day, he would redeem it at night.

When Titus Vespasian, who avenged Christ’s blood on Jerusalem, returned to Rome as victor, he remembered one night, as he sat at supper with his friends, that he had done no good that day, and he uttered this memorable and praiseworthy saying: “Amici, diem perdidi” (My friends, I have lost a day).

Chilo, one of the seven sages, being asked what was the hardest thing in the world to do, answered, “To use and employ a man’s time well.”

Cato held that an account must be given not only of our labor but also of our leisure. And Aelian gives this testimony of the Spartans, that “they were hugely covetous of their time, spending it all on necessary things, and allowing no citizen either to be idle or to play.” And, says another, “We trifle with what is most precious, and throw away what it is most in our interest to redeem.”

Certainly these heathen will rise in judgment, not only against Domitian the Roman emperor, who spent much of his time in killing flies; nor only against Archimedes, who spent his time drawing lines on the ground when Syracuse was taken; nor against Artaxerxes, who spent his time making handles for knives; nor only against Suleiman the Great Turk, who spent his time making notches of horn for bows; nor only against Aeropus, a Macedonian king, who spent his time making lanterns; nor only against Hyrcanus the king of Parthia, who spent his time catching moles; but also against many professing Christians who, instead of redeeming precious time, trifle and fool away much of their precious time at the mirror, the comb, the lute, the viol, the pipe, or at vain sports and foolish pastimes, or by idle jesting, immoderate sleeping, and superfluous feasting, etc. Oh, sirs! Good hours and blessed opportunities for closet prayer are merchandise of the highest rate and price; and therefore whoever has a mind to be rich in grace and to be high in glory should buy up that merchandise. They should always be redeeming precious time. Oh, sirs! We should redeem time for private prayer out of our eating time, our drinking time, our sleeping time, our buying time, our selling time, our sinning time, our sporting time, rather than neglect our closet communion with God!

6. Closet Prayer Is Either a Duty or It Is No Duty

Sixthly, I answer: closet prayer is either a duty or it is no duty. Now I have proved so strongly, I suppose, that it is a duty, that no man or devil can fairly or honestly deny it to be a duty. Why then do men cry out about their great business? Alas! Duty must be done, whatever business is left undone. Duty must be done, or the man who neglects it will be undone forever. It is a vain thing to plead business when a required duty is to be performed. Indeed, if the bare pleading of business, of much business, were enough to excuse men from duty, I am afraid there are very few duties of the gospel that men would not try to evade under a pretense of business, of much business. He who pleads business to evade private prayer will be just as ready to plead business to evade family prayer; and he who pleads business to evade family prayer will be just as ready to plead business to evade public prayer.

Well, sirs, remember what became of those who excused themselves out of heaven by their carnal excuses and secular business. “I have bought a field, and I need to go see it. Please excuse me,” says one. “I have bought,” says another, “five yoke of oxen, and I am going to try them out. Please excuse me.” And, “I have married a wife,” says another, “so I cannot come” (Luke 14:18–20). The true reason why they would not come to the supper to which the King of kings had invited them was not that they had bought farms and oxen, but that their farms and oxen had bought them. The things of the world and their relatives according to the flesh had taken up so much room in their hearts and affections that they had no appetite for heaven’s dainties. Therefore it is worth noting what Christ adds at the end of the parable: whoever “does not hate his father and mother and wife and children and brothers and sisters—yes, even his own life,” and much more his farm and oxen, “cannot be My disciple” (v. 26).

By these words it is evident that it was not simply the farm, nor the oxen, nor the wife, but a foolish, inordinate, carnal love and esteem of these things, above better and greater blessings, that made them refuse the gracious invitation of Christ. They refused the grace and mercy of God offered in the gospel under a pretense of worldly business; and God decisively concludes that not one man of them will taste His supper. And indeed, what can be more just and righteous than that those should never so much as taste spiritual and eternal blessings who prefer their earthly business to heaven’s dainties, who, with the Reubenites, prefer a country suitable for feeding their cattle to a share in the land of promise?

Private prayer is a work of absolute necessity, both for bringing the heart into a good frame and for keeping the heart in a good frame. It is of absolute necessity for the revealing of sin, and for the preventing of sin, and for the embittering of sin, and for the weakening of sin, and for the purging away of sin. It is of absolute necessity for the revealing of grace, and for a full exercise of grace, and for an eminent increase of grace. It is of absolute necessity to arm us against both inward and outward temptations, afflictions, and sufferings. It is of absolute necessity to fit us for all other duties and services. For a man to glorify God, to save his own soul, and to further his own everlasting happiness is a work of the greatest necessity. Now private prayer is such a work; why then should any man plead business, great business, when a work of such absolute necessity is before him? If a man’s child or wife were dangerously sick, or wounded, or near death, he would never plead, “I have business, I have a great deal of business to do; and therefore I cannot stay with my child, my wife, and I have no time to go or send for the physician.” Oh, no! He would rather argue this way: “It is absolutely necessary that I look after the preservation of the life of my child or my wife, and I will attend to this whatever happens to my business.” Oh, sirs! Your+ souls are of greater concern to you+ than the lives of all the wives and children in the world; and therefore your+ souls must be attended to, they must be saved, whatever business is neglected.

7. God Never Appointed Your Calling to Thrust Private Prayer Out of Doors

Seventhly, I answer that God never appointed or intended any man’s ordinary, particular calling to thrust private prayer out of doors. That it is a great sin for any professing Christian to neglect his particular calling under any religious pretense is evident enough from these scriptures: “Six days you shall labor and do all your work … Each one should remain in the calling he was in when he was called … For even while we were with you+, we gave you+ this command: ‘If anyone is unwilling to work, he shall not eat.’ Yet we hear that some of you+ are leading undisciplined lives and accomplishing nothing but being busybodies. We command and urge such people by our Lord Jesus Christ to begin working quietly to earn their own living … to aspire to live quietly, to attend to your+ own matters, and to work with your+ own hands, as we instructed you+. Then you+ will behave properly toward outsiders, without being dependent on anyone … he must work, doing good with his own hands, that he may have something to share with the one in need … If anyone does not provide for his own, and especially his own household, he has denied the faith and is worse than an unbeliever” (Exod 20:9; 1 Cor 7:20; 2 Thess 3:10–12; 1 Thess 4:11–12; Eph 4:28; 1 Tim 5:8). Indeed, our Lord Jesus Christ was a plain, honest carpenter, and worked hard in that particular calling until He entered on the public ministry, as all the ancients agree (Mark 6:3; Matt 13:55–56). And we read also that all the patriarchs had their particular callings. Abel was a keeper of sheep (Gen 4:2); Noah was a farmer (Gen 9:20); the sons of Jacob were shepherds and keepers of cattle (Gen 46:34); and all the apostles, before they were called to the work of the ministry, had their particular callings.

By the law of Muhammad, the Great Turk himself is bound to practice some manual trade or occupation.

Solon made a law that a son should not be bound to support his father in old age unless he had set himself to some occupation in his youth. And at Athens every man gave a yearly account to the magistrate of the trade or course of life by which he maintained himself; and if he could not, he was banished. And all writers condemn it as a very great vanity in Dionysius, who insisted on being the best poet, and in Caligula, who insisted on being the best orator, and in Nero, who insisted on being the best fiddler; and so they became the three worst princes, by minding other men’s business more than their own particular calling. But for a man to evade or neglect private prayer under pretense of his particular calling agrees with no scripture; indeed, it is contrary to very many scriptures, as is evident from the many arguments cited earlier.

Certainly no man’s calling is a calling away from God or godliness. It never entered the heart of God that our particular callings should ever drive our general calling of Christianity out of doors. Just as our general calling must not eat up our particular calling, so our particular calling must not eat up our general calling.

Certainly our particular calling must give way to our general calling. Did not the woman of Samaria leave her water jar, and run into the city, and say, “Come, see a man who told me everything I ever did. Could this be the Christ?” (John 4:28–29). Did not the shepherds leave their flocks in the field, and go to Bethlehem, and declare the good news of great joy that they had heard from the angel, that there was born that day in the city of David a Savior, who was Christ the Lord (Luke 2:8–21)? And did not Christ commend Mary for that holy neglect of her particular calling when she sat at His feet and heard His word (Luke 10:38ff)? And what do all these instances show, but that our particular callings must give the place of honor to the general calling of Christianity?

Certainly the works of our general calling are far greater and more glorious, more eminent and excellent, higher and nobler, than the works of our particular callings; and therefore it is much more tolerable for our general calling to borrow time from our particular calling than for our particular calling to borrow time from our general calling. Certainly those men are very ignorant or very profane who either think themselves so closely tied to follow their particular callings six days a week that they must not concern themselves with any religious services, or who think their particular callings to be a gulf or a grave designed by God to swallow up private prayer. God, who is the Lord of time, has reserved some part of our time to Himself every day. Though the Jews were commanded to labor six days of the week, yet they were also commanded to offer up a morning and evening sacrifice daily (Deut 6:6–8; Exod 29:38–39; Num 28:3).

The Jews divided the day into three parts:

The first for prayer;

The second for the reading of the law;

And the third for the works of their lawful callings.

As bad as the Jews were, yet every day they set part of the day apart for religious exercises. Certainly they are worse than Jews who spend all their time on their particular callings, and shut closet prayer quite out of doors. Certainly that man’s soul is in a very bad state who is so entangled with the encumbrances of the world that he can spare no time for private prayer. If God is the Lord of your blessings, the Lord of your time, and the Lord of your soul, how can you with any fairness or honor put off His service under a pretense of much business? That man is lost, that man is cursed, who can find time for anything but none to meet with God in his closet. That man is doubtless on the brink of ruin whose worldly business eats up all thoughts of God, of Christ, of heaven, of eternity, of his soul, and of his soul’s concerns.

8. The More Business Lies on Your Hands, the More Need to Keep Close to Your Closet

Eighthly and lastly, I answer: the more worldly business lies on your hands, the more need you have to keep close to your closet. Much business lays a man open to many sins, and to many snares, and to many temptations. Now the more sins, snares, and temptations a man’s business lays him open to, the more need that man has to be much in private prayer, so that his soul may be kept pure from sin, and his foot may not be caught in the devil’s trap, and he may stand fast in the hour of temptation.

Private prayer is so far from hindering a man’s business that it is the way of ways to bring down a blessing from heaven on a man’s business (Ps 1:2–3; 127:1–2; 128:1–2), just as the firstfruits that God’s people gave to Him brought down a blessing from heaven on all the rest (Deut 26:10–11). Whetting the blade is no hindrance. Prayer and feeding the horse never hinder a journey.

Private prayer is like Jacob, who brought down a blessing from heaven on all that Laban had (Gen 30:27, 30). Private prayer gives a man a sanctified use both of all his earthly comforts and of all his earthly business. David and Daniel found this by experience, and therefore their great public employments could not draw them away from their private duties. Time spent in heavenly employments is no time lost from worldly business (Deut 28:1–8). Private prayer makes all we take in hand successful. Closet prayer has made many rich, but it never made any man poor or a beggar in this world.

No man on earth knows what the emergencies or the events of a day may be. “Do not boast about tomorrow, for you do not know what a day may bring forth” (Prov 27:1). Every day is, as it were, a great-bellied day; every day is, as it were, with child of something. But what it will bring forth, whether a trial or a comfort, no man can tell. Just as no one can tell, while a woman is with child, what kind of birth it will be, so no man knows what mercies a day may bring forth. No man knows what miseries a day may bring forth. No man knows what good a day may bring forth. No man knows what evil a day may bring forth. No man knows what afflictions a day may bring forth. No man knows what temptations a day may bring forth. No man knows what liberty a day may bring forth. No man knows what bonds a day may bring forth. No man knows what good success a day may bring forth. No man knows what bad success a day may bring forth. And therefore a man needs to be in his closet with God every day, so that he may be prepared and fitted to receive and make use of all the events, successes, and emergencies that may meet him in the course of his life. And let this much suffice as an answer to this first objection.

Objection 2: We Are Servants, and Have No Time We Can Call Our Own

Secondly, others may object and say, “Sir, we grant that private prayer is an indispensable duty that lies on the people of God. But we are servants, and we have no time that we can call our own. Our master’s business will not allow us any time for private prayer, and so we hope we may be excused.”

1. The Text Is General, and Not Limited to Any Sort or Rank of Persons

First, the text is general and not limited to any sort or rank of persons, whether high or low, rich or poor, slave or free, servant or master. “But you, when you pray, go into your closet, and when you have shut your door, pray to your Father, who is in secret” (Matt 6:6). Here are three singular yous, you, you, you, which are to be understood generally. You servant as well as you master, you slave as well as you free man, you poor man as well as you rich man, you maid as well as you mistress, you child as well as you father, you wife as well as you husband. Private prayer is an indispensable duty that lies upon all sorts and ranks of persons. A man may as well say that the singular pronoun you that runs through the Ten Commandments—“You shall have no other gods before Me. You shall not make for yourself an idol…You shall not bow down to them or worship them…You shall not take the name of the Lord your God in vain…Six days you shall labor…You shall not murder. You shall not commit adultery. You shall not steal. You shall not bear false witness against your neighbor. You shall not covet your neighbor’s house. You shall not covet your neighbor’s wife, or his manservant or maidservant, or his ox or donkey, or anything that belongs to your neighbor” (Exod 20:3–17)—relates to the rich and not to the poor, to masters and not to servants, to the free and not to those who are in bonds, as he may say that the three yous in the text relate to the rich and not to the poor, to masters and not to servants, to those who are free but not to those who are bound. But certainly there is no man in his right mind who will say so, who will affirm such a thing. Doubtless this pronoun you reaches every man, whatever his rank or standing in this world.
