2. The Former Answers Are Here Very Applicable

Secondly, I answer that the first, the third, the fourth, the fifth, the sixth, the seventh, and the eighth answers given to the first objection apply very well here. And oh, that all masters and servants were wise enough, serious enough, and fair-minded enough to lay all those answers warm on their own hearts! It might help to prevent much sin, and move masters and mistresses to give their pious servants a little more time to lift up their hearts to Christ in a corner.

3. If You Have Liberty to Choose, Change Your Station

Thirdly, I answer: if you are a servant who has liberty to choose a new master, you had better change your station than live under the roof of a master who is such an enemy to God, to Christ, to religion, to himself, and to the eternal welfare of your poor soul that he will not give you half an hour a day to spend in your room, your closet, though the glory of God, the good of his own family, and the everlasting happiness of your own soul are at stake in it (Ps 84:10; 120:5). It is better for you to change your master than to neglect your duty. “Were you a servant when you were called? Do not let it concern you—but if you can gain your freedom, take the opportunity” (1 Cor 7:21). We lost our liberty by sin, and by nature we desire nothing more than liberty.

The rabbis say of liberty, “If the heavens were parchment, the sea ink, and every blade of grass a pen, its praises could not be contained or expressed.” Laban’s house was full of idols. Great houses often are. Jacob’s tent was little, but the true worship of God was in it. It is infinitely better to live in Jacob’s tent than in Laban’s house. It is best to be with masters where we may have the least of sin and the most of God, where we may have the most helps, the best examples, and the choicest encouragements to be holy and happy.

The religious servant should be as careful in choosing his master as the religious master is in choosing his servant. Gracious servants are great blessings to the families they live in; and a master who would rather part with a gracious servant than spare him a little time each day to pour out his soul before the Lord in a corner may well be called an unhappy master.

4. A Gracious Servant Is Given a Principle by God to Cry, Abba, Father

Fourthly, I answer: if you are a gracious servant, then God has given you His Spirit and a principle of grace for this very purpose, that you may cry, Abba, Father, when you are alone, when you are in a corner, and no eye sees you but His Who sees in secret (Rom 8:15; Gal 4:6; 1 Cor 6:19; 2 Tim 1:14). If you are a gracious servant, then you have received not the spirit of the world but the Spirit who is from God (1 Cor 2:12). Now, he who has this tree of life also has the fruit that grows upon this tree. “But the fruit of the Spirit is love, joy, peace, patience, kindness, goodness, faithfulness, gentleness, and self-control,” etc. (Gal 5:22–23). Now, grace is called not the works of the Spirit but the fruits of the Spirit,

(1) Because all grace is derived from the Spirit as the fruit is derived from the root. And,

(2) To show how pleasant and delightful grace is; for what is more pleasant and delightful than sweet and wholesome fruits (Song 4:16; 6:2)?

(3) To note the profit and advantage that flows to those who have the Spirit. For as many grow rich by the fruits of their gardens and orchards, so many grow rich in grace, in holiness, in comfort, in spiritual experiences, by the fruits of the Spirit. Now why has God given you His Spirit, and why has He laid into your soul a stock of supernatural graces, but that you may be in every way qualified, disposed, and fitted for private prayer, and to maintain secret communion with God in a corner?

Certainly, God never gave any poor servant a talent of gifts or a talent of grace except so that he might drive a secret trade heavenward.

5. Daniel Was Both a Subject and a Servant to King Darius

Fifthly, I answer: though King Darius had made a decree that none should ask any petition of any god or man for thirty days, on the penalty of being cast into the den of lions, yet Daniel kept up his private devotions; and Daniel was both a subject and a servant to King Darius, and one on whose hands the chief and greatest affairs of the kingdom lay. In the first and second verses of the sixth chapter of Daniel, you+ will find that Daniel had an abundance of great and weighty employments on his hands. He was set over the whole affairs of the whole empire of Persia. He and two other presidents, of whom he himself was chief, were to receive the accounts of the whole kingdom from all those hundred and twenty princes who were employed in all public business in the Persian monarchy. And yet, notwithstanding such a multitude of business as lay on his hands, and notwithstanding his condition as a servant, he was very careful to redeem time for private prayer. Indeed, it is very noteworthy that the heart of Daniel, in the midst of all his mighty business, was so much set upon private prayer, upon his secret retirements for religious exercises, that he ran the risk of losing all his honors, profits, pleasures, yes, and life itself, rather than be deprived of fitting time and opportunities to wait upon God in his room. Certainly, Daniel will one day rise in judgment against all those subjects and servants who think to evade private prayer by their pleas of much business and of their being servants.

6. What Singular Thing Do You Do? What Do You Do More Than Others?

Sixthly, I answer: if you+ who are gracious servants cannot, in spite of your+ masters’ business, redeem a little time to wrestle with God in a corner, what singular thing are you+ doing? What are you+ doing more than others? Do you+ hear sermons? So do others. Do you+ read? So do others. Do you+ follow your+ masters to public prayers? So do others. Do you+ join with your+ masters in family prayers? So do others. Oh, but gracious servants should go beyond all other servants in the world; they should do singular things for God. “What are you+ doing more than others?” (Matt 5:47)—τί περισσὸν ποιεῖτε; What extraordinary thing are you+ doing? What is more ordinary than to find servants following their masters to public prayers and to family prayers? Oh, but to find poor servants redeeming a little time from their masters’ business to pour out their souls before the Lord in a corner—this is not ordinary; indeed, this is extraordinary, and this is wonderfully fitting for gracious servants. Oh, that all men’s servants who are servants to the Most High God would seriously consider,

(1) How singularly they are privileged by God above all other servants in the world. They are called, adopted, reconciled, pardoned, justified before the throne of God, which other servants are not (1 Cor 3:22–23). And why then should such servants not be singular in their services, when they are so singular in their privileges?

(2) Secondly, gracious servants are made partakers of a more excellent nature than other servants are. “Through these He has given us His precious and magnificent promises, so that through them you+ may become partakers of the divine nature” (2 Pet 1:4). The apostle in this expression does not aim at any essential change and conversion of our substance into the nature of God and Christ, but only at the raising and dignifying of our nature by Christ. That real, that near, that dear, that choice, that mysterious, that special, that singular union that Christians have with Christ raises them up to a higher resemblance and likeness of God and Christ than they ever had attained in their original perfection. Yet it does not bring in any real transformation of either our bodies or our souls into the divine nature. It is certain that our union and conjunction with Christ neither mingles persons nor unites substances, but it joins our affections and brings our wills into a league of friendship with Christ. To be made a partaker of the divine nature means two things, say some:

First, a fellowship with God in His holiness;

Secondly, a fellowship with God in His blessedness, in the beatific vision and brightness of glory.

To be made “partakers of the divine nature,” say others, is to be made partakers of those holy graces, those divine qualities, which are at times called the image of God, the likeness of God, the life of God, etc. (Eph 4:24; Col 3:10), by which we resemble God, not only as a picture resembles a man in outward features, but as a child resembles his father in face and disposition. Now, take the words whichever way you+ will: how much it concerns those servants who are made partakers of the divine nature to do singular things for God, to do things for God that other servants, who are not partakers of the divine nature, have no mind, no heart, no spirit to do—indeed, things that they refuse and scorn to do!

(3) Thirdly, gracious servants are worthily descended; they have the most illustrious ancestry and honorable origin (1 John 5:19; John 3:8; Jas 2:5).

(4) Fourthly, gracious servants are worthily attended they are nobly guarded (Ps 34:15; Heb 1:14; Deut 33:26–27; Zech 2:5).

(5) Fifthly, gracious servants are worthily dignified; they are dignified with the highest and most honorable titles (1 Pet 2:9; Rev 1:5–6; 5:10).

(6) Sixthly, take many things in one: gracious servants have more excellent graces, experiences, comforts, communions, promises, assurances, manifestations, hopes, helps, principles, food, clothing, portion, than all other servants in the world have. And therefore God may well expect better and greater things from them than from all other servants in the world. God may very well expect that they should do singular things for His glory, Who has done such singular things for their good. Certainly God expects that gracious servants should be blessing Him when other servants are blaspheming Him; that they should be magnifying Him when other servants are debasing Him; that they should be redeeming precious time when other servants are trifling, fooling, playing, or sinning away precious time; that they should be weeping in a corner when other servants are sporting and making themselves merry among their jovial companions; that they should be mourning in secret when other servants are sinning in secret; and that they should be at their private devotions when other servants are sleeping and snoring.

Solomon, the wisest prince who ever sat upon a throne, and who was guided by an infallible Spirit, laid it down as a standing maxim more than two thousand years ago that “the righteous man is more excellent than his neighbor” (Prov 12:26). When Solomon dropped this aphorism from his royal pen, there was not a man in the world who was legally righteous, Adam and all his posterity having fallen from all their honor, glory, dignity, and excellence into a most woeful gulf of sin and misery. And therefore Solomon must be understood as speaking of the man who is evangelically righteous (Ps 14:1–3; Rom 3:9–12; Lam 5:16). He who is evangelically righteous, whether he is master or servant, rich or poor, slave or free, high or low, is more excellent than his neighbor. And oh, that all masters would seriously consider this, so that they may no longer behave so proudly, so loftily, so scornfully, so perversely, so distantly, so sourly, so bitterly, so harshly towards their pious servants that they will not give them a little time to pour out their souls before the Lord in a corner!

I have read of Ingo, an ancient king of the Draves and Veneds, who, giving a stately feast, had all his pagan nobles sit in the hall below, and at the same time ordered certain poor Christians to be brought up into his presence chamber to sit with him at his table, so that they might eat of his royal fare. When many wondered at this, he told them that he counted Christians, however poor, a greater ornament at his table and more worthy of his company than the greatest nobles who had not been converted to the Christian faith. “For,” he said, “when these pagan nobles are thrust down to hell, these poor Christians will be my companions and fellow princes in heaven.” Certainly, this noble prince will one day rise in judgment against all sour, churlish Labans who treat their gracious servants so harshly and so severely that they will not allow them a little time to wait on God in a hole (Eph 6:9).

Why should not gracious masters give their gracious servants a little time for closet prayer now, considering that they are sharers with them in all the fundamental good that comes by Christ in this world, and considering that they shall be partakers with them in all the glory of another world? The poorest servant in a family has a soul more precious than heaven and earth; and the greatest work that lies on his hands in this world is to look to its eternal safety and security. For if that is safe, all is safe. If that is well, all is well. But if that is lost, all is lost. Every gracious servant, however poor and lowly he may be, has the image of God, the image of the King of kings, stamped upon him. And woe to him who wrongs, or despises, or tramples upon that image! Certainly, God Himself is wronged by the injury that is done to His image. The contempt and spite that is shown to the image or coin of a king is shown to the king himself, and accordingly he will avenge it.

If it was a capital crime in Tiberius’ days to carry the image of Augustus on a ring or coin into any filthy place, as Suetonius says it was, what a crime it must be in those masters who despise, revile, reproach, scorn, abuse, and tread under foot such servants as have the image of the great God stamped upon their souls, and all because they look Godward, Christward, heavenward, holinessward, dutyward! Masters should never taunt their servants with their inferiority, penury, poverty, misery, lowly parentage, or servile condition, but should remember that these things are more the Creator’s pleasure than the servant’s fault, and that the God who has made the master rich and the servant poor can as quickly make the master poor and the servant rich (Prov 22:2; 17:5). God many times puts down the mighty from their seats and exalts those of low degree (Luke 1:52). Certainly, no master or mistress should dare to insult or triumph over such servants as have souls as noble as their own. But they should seriously and frequently consider Solomon’s aphorism, “The righteous man,” though a servant, though the lowliest among all the servants, “is more excellent than his neighbor” (Prov 12:26), and accordingly give them a little time and liberty to converse with God in secret. And oh, that all gracious servants would show themselves to be more excellent than their neighbors: by making private prayer more a matter of conscience than their neighbors do, by being more in their closets than their neighbors are, by delighting themselves in their secret retirements more than their neighbors will, and by redeeming some time for God, for their souls, and for eternity, more than their neighbors do!

7. God Is the Only Lord of Time

Seventhly, I answer that God is the only Lord of time. Time belongs more to the Lord than to your master, and so it is no neglect of your master’s business to take a little time each day for private prayer. Times belong to providence as well as outcomes do; and as God is the God of our blessings, so He is the Lord of our times. “My times are in Your hands,” says David (Ps 31:15). Not only the times of his sorrows but also the times of his comforts, not only the times of his miseries but also the times of his blessings, not only the times of his dangers but also the times of his duties, were in the hands of God.

It is noteworthy that the psalmist does not say time, but times, in the plural, to show that every point and period of time depends upon the hand of God.

One, complaining of those who say, “Come, let us talk together, to pass away the time,” cries out with grief of spirit, O donec praetereat hora, “Oh, until the hour is gone! Oh, until the time is past which the mercy of your Maker has bestowed on you to repent, to procure pardon, to gain grace, and to obtain glory.” The servant who borrows a little time every day to seek the face of God in a corner borrows it from God rather than from his master; and therefore why should his master fume, rage, or complain, considering that God never made him lord of time?

8. Redeem Time from Sleep, Recreations, and Meals Rather Than Neglect the Closet

Eighthly, I answer that servants should rather redeem time from their sleep, their recreations, their daily meals, than neglect closet duty for a day. And certainly those servants who, out of conscience towards God and out of a due regard for the internal and eternal welfare of their own souls, every day redeem an hour from their sleep, or sports, or meals, to spend with God in secret, will find by experience that the Lord will make a few hours’ sleep sweeter and better to them than many hours’ sleep. Their outward sports shall be made up with inward delights, and instead of their common bread God will feed them with the bread that came down from heaven.

Sirs, was Christ not His Father’s Servant? “Here is My Servant, whom I uphold, My elect (or choice one), in whom My soul delights (or is well pleased). I will put My Spirit on Him, and He will bring justice to the nations” (Isa 42:1). And did He not redeem time from His natural rest rather than omit private prayer? “Early in the morning, a great while before day, Jesus got up and slipped out to a solitary place to pray” (Mark 1:35). Christ spent the day in preaching, in healing the sick, in working miracles; and rather than let these noble works shut out private prayer, He rose a great while before day, so that He might have some time to wrestle with His Father in secret. “In those days Jesus went out to the mountain to pray, and He spent all night in prayer to God” (Luke 6:12). O sirs! Did Christ spend whole nights in private prayer for the salvation of your+ souls, and will you+ think it too much to redeem an hour from your+ natural rest to seek and to serve Him in a corner, and to make sure the things of your+ everlasting peace?

Redeeming time for private prayer is redeeming a precious treasure which, once lost, can never be fully recovered. If riches sprout wings and fly away, they may come back again, as they did to Job. If credit, and honor, and worldly greatness and renown fly away, they may come back again, as they did to Nebuchadnezzar. If success, and famous victories and conquests, sprout wings and fly away, they may come back again, as they did to many of the Roman conquerors and others. But if time, which the poets paint with wings to show its fleetness and swiftness, flies from us, it will never come back to us.

A great lady [Queen Elizabeth] of this land cried out on her deathbed, “Call time again, call time again! A world of wealth for an inch of time!” But time past never was, nor ever could be, recalled.

The Egyptians drew the picture of time with three heads. The first was of a greedy wolf gaping for time past, because it has ravenously devoured even the memory of so many things past recalling. The second was of a crowned lion roaring for time present, because it holds the rule over all action, for which it calls aloud. And the third was of a deceitful dog fawning for time to come, because it feeds foolish men with many flattering hopes, to their eternal undoing. Oh, that all this might prevail with servants to redeem time for private prayer! And if my counsel might be taken, I would rather advise servants to redeem some time for private prayer from their sleep, or lawful recreations, or set meals, etc., than to spend in private prayer the time which their masters call their own, especially if their masters are unconverted and “poisoned by bitterness and captive to iniquity” (Acts 8:23); and that for these five reasons:

(1) First, because this may be a means to prevent much sin on the master’s side. Masters who are in their unregenerate state are very apt to storm, and rage, and let fly against God, and Christ, and religion, and the profession of faith, etc., when they see their servants spend on private prayer, or on any other religious exercise, time which, as they understand it, is their own and ought to be wholly spent in following their business. Now gracious servants should have such honorable respect, such tender affection, and such Christian compassion for their masters’ souls that they do all they possibly can to keep their masters from bringing guilt upon their souls, or from making work for repentance, for hell, or for the Physician of souls (Jude 22–23).

The Persians, the Turks, and many Indians are so compassionate that they build hospitals not only for lame and diseased men, but also for birds, beasts, and dogs that are old, starved, or hurt. Oh, then, what tender compassion should gracious servants show towards their masters’ souls, which are jewels worth more than heaven and earth!

(2) Secondly, because this may be a means to convince the judgments and consciences of their masters that there is some worth, some excellence, some sweetness to be found in private prayer and in other closet duties. For when masters observe their servants redeeming time for closet duties from their very sleep, recreations, dinners, and suppers, they will be ready to conclude that there is certainly more worth, more goodness, more sweetness, more excellence, more glory, more gain in closet duties than they have ever understood, felt, or experienced, and that their own poor servants are better and more righteous than they are. Sozomen reports that the devout life of a poor captive Christian woman led a king and all his family to embrace the faith of Jesus Christ. Good works convince more than miracles do.

I have read of one Pachomius, a soldier under the emperor Constantine, how, when his army was almost starved for lack of necessary provisions, he came to a city of Christians, and they of their own charity relieved them speedily and freely. Wondering at their free and noble charity, he inquired what kind of people these were whom he saw so bountiful. He was told that they were Christians, who profess to hurt no man and to do good to every man. At this Pachomius, convinced of the excellence of this religion, threw away his arms and became a Christian, a saint. Just as husbands are sometimes won by the conduct of their wives without the Word (1 Pet 3:1–2), so masters may sometimes be won by the gracious bearing and conduct of their servants without the Word. The servant’s redeeming of time for private duties on the hardest and severest terms may be so blessed to the master that it may result in his conviction, conversion, and salvation. There is a maybe for it, and even a maybe should be sufficient encouragement for every gracious servant to do all he can to save the soul of his master from going down into the infernal pit.

(3) Thirdly, because the servant’s redeeming of time from his sleep, recreations, and meals for private prayer will most clearly and abundantly show the singular love, the great delight, and the high esteem that he has for private prayer. We say that children who will be at their books when others have gone to bed, and who will be at their books before others can get out of bed, love their books well and delight much in learning. Certainly, those who will be praying when others are sleeping, and who will be addressing their souls to God in a corner before their mistress is dressing herself at the mirror or their fellow servants are dressing themselves in the shop, love private prayer well and delight much in closet communion with God.

(4) Fourthly, because the servant’s redeeming of time for private prayer from his sleep, set meals, recreations, etc., may be of the greatest use to other fellow servants. It may both awaken them and convince them that the things of religion are of the greatest and highest importance, and that there is no trade, or pleasure, or profit to compare with that private trade carried on between God and a man’s own soul. It may also keep them from trifling or fooling away the time which is truly and rightfully their masters’ time, and which by the royal law of heaven ought to be spent solely and wholly in their service and business. For what fair-minded servant is there in the world who will not argue like this? “I see that such and such of my fellow servants will redeem time for private prayer and for other closet services from their very sleep, meals, recreations, etc., rather than borrow or make free with the time which my master says is his, etc. And why then should I be so foolish, so brutish, so mad, as to trifle, or idle, or play, or toy away the time that should be spent in my master’s service and for my master’s advantage?”

(5) Fifthly and lastly, because the servant’s redeeming of time for private prayer from his sleep, his meals, his recreations, etc., cannot fail to be infinitely pleasing to God, and will give him the most comfort when he comes to die. The more any poor heart acts contrary to flesh and blood, the more he pleases God. The more any poor heart denies himself, the more he pleases God. The more any poor heart acts against the stream of sinful examples, the more he pleases God. The more difficulties and discouragements a poor heart meets with in the discharge of his duty, the more love he shows to God. And the more love a poor heart shows to God, the more he pleases God. “Go and proclaim in the hearing of Jerusalem that this is what the Lord says: ‘I remember the devotion of your youth, your love as a bride, how you followed Me in the wilderness, in a land not sown. Israel was holy to the Lord, the firstfruits of His harvest. All who devoured her found themselves guilty; disaster came upon them,’ declares the Lord” (Jer 2:2–3). God was very highly pleased and greatly delighted with the singular love and choice affections of His people towards Him when they followed after Him and kept close to Him in that wearisome and strange passage through the waste, howling wilderness. I leave it to the fair-minded reader to judge how well all these things fit the poor pious servant who redeems time for private prayer on the hardest terms imaginable. And certainly, on a deathbed, no tongue can express, nor any heart conceive, but his who feels it, the unspeakable comfort that closet duties will give to the man who has practiced them on the hard terms we are now considering.

9. Your Near and Dear Relation to God Calls Aloud for Private Prayer

Ninthly, I answer: if you are a gracious servant, then the near and dear relation between God and you, and the choice privileges that you share in, call aloud for private prayer (John 8:32–33, 36). As you are your master’s servant, so you are the Lord’s freeman. “For he who was a servant when he was called by the Lord is the Lord’s freeman. Conversely, he who was free when he was called is Christ’s servant. You+ were bought at a price; do not become servants of men” (1 Cor 7:22–23)—that is, either when they command you+ things forbidden by Christ, or forbid you+ things commanded by Christ, or when they would exercise a dominion over your+ faith or a lordship over your+ consciences. Do not allow yourselves+ in spiritual things to be brought into such bondage by any men or masters in the world as not to use the freedom and liberty that Christ has purchased for you+ with His dearest blood (Gal 5:1; Col 2:20; Gal 2:4). No servants are to serve their masters in opposition to Christ; nor are any servants to serve their masters as spiritual masters; nor are any servants to serve their masters as supreme masters, but as subordinate masters (Eph 6:5–7). And as every gracious servant is the Lord’s freeman, so every gracious servant is the Lord’s friend (Isa 41:8; Jas 2:23; John 15:13–15). And as every gracious servant is the Lord’s friend, so every gracious servant is the Lord’s son (Gal 4:5–6; Rom 8:16). And as every gracious servant is the Lord’s son, so every gracious servant is the Lord’s spouse (Hos 2:19–20; 2 Cor 11:2).

And now I appeal to the consciences of all who have tasted that the Lord is gracious, whether the near and dear relation between the Lord and pious servants does not call aloud on them to take every opportunity and advantage they possibly can to pour out their souls before the Lord in secret, and to make known to Him in a corner all their secret needs, and weaknesses, and wishes. And as gracious servants are so closely and dearly related to God, so gracious servants are very highly privileged by God. Gracious servants are as much freed from the reign of sin, the dominion of sin, and the condemning power of sin as gracious masters are (Rom 6:14). Gracious servants are as much freed from hell, from the curse of the law, and from the wrath of God as their gracious masters are (Rom 8:1). Gracious servants are as much adopted, as much reconciled, as much pardoned, as much justified, and as much redeemed as their gracious masters are (Gal 3:13). Gracious servants are as much heirs, heirs of God and joint heirs with Christ, as their gracious masters are. Gracious servants are as much a chosen generation, a royal priesthood, a holy nation, a people for God’s own possession, called out of darkness into His marvelous light, as their gracious masters are. And therefore, since they all share alike in all these great and glorious privileges which belong to saints as saints, they are beyond all doubt equally obliged and bound to all the duties which lie on saints as saints. Private prayer is one of these; and therefore they are to buckle down to this duty against all carnal reasons and objections whatever.

10. The Promised Reward Lies as Open to the Servant as to the Master

Tenthly and lastly, I answer that the promised reward in the text lies as fair and as open to the servant as to the master, to the slave as to the free, to the peasant as to the prince. Whoever prays to his heavenly Father in secret, be he high or low, rich or poor, honorable or lowly, servant or master, shall receive an open reward. The reward in the text is not to be confined or limited to this or that sort or rank of men, but is to be extended to all ranks and sorts of men who make conscience of private prayer, of closet duties. “Servants, obey your+ earthly masters with respect and fear and sincerity of heart, just as you+ would obey Christ. And do this not only to please them while they are watching, but as servants of Christ, doing the will of God from your+ heart. Serve with good will, as to the Lord and not to men, because you+ know that the Lord will reward each one for whatever good he does, whether he is slave or free…Servants, obey your+ earthly masters in everything, not only to please them while they are watching, but with sincerity of heart and fear of the Lord. Whatever you+ do, work at it with your+ whole being, for the Lord and not for men, because you+ know that you+ will receive the reward of the inheritance from the Lord. It is the Lord Christ you+ are serving” (Eph 6:5–8; Col 3:22–24).

Servants who serve their masters faithfully, heartily, and in sincerity of spirit will receive the reward of grace and the reward of the inheritance. The lowliest servant who is faithful in the service of his master will receive the eternal inheritance as his recompense (Rom 8:15–17). The recompense spoken of in the scripture just cited comes not from merit but from sheer grace, because the inheritance belongs only to children, by their birth or adoption. Faithful servants will be turned from servants into sons, and so enjoy the heavenly inheritance. Christ is so noble a master that He will not allow any service done to men out of conscience towards His command to go unrewarded. Oh, how much more will He recompense pious servants for the spiritual services that they perform for His sake, for His glory! God is so liberal a paymaster that no man will so much as shut the door, or kindle a fire on His altar, or give a cup of cold water—one of the least, readiest, and humblest refreshments there are—without being rewarded (Mal 1:10; Matt 10:42).

It is an excellent observation of Calvin on God’s rewarding of the Rechabites’ obedience (Jer 35:19): “God,” he says, “often recompenses the shadows and seeming appearances of virtue, to show the pleasure He takes in the ample rewards that He has reserved for true and sincere piety.” Nebuchadnezzar, though a tyrant, was employed in God’s service against Tyre, and so he is to have Egypt as his pay for his pains at Tyre (Ezek 29:18–20). It is an ancient slur and slander cast upon God, as if He were a harsh master, an ungenerous lord, and as if there were nothing to be got in His service but knocks, blows, wounds, afflictions, losses, etc., whereas He is a rewarder not only “of those who earnestly seek Him” but even of the very worst of men who do any service for Him (Heb 11:6). I have read of Herod Agrippa, the same Agrippa who was struck by the angel and eaten by worms because he did not give glory to God (Acts 12:23), that when he was bound in chains and sent to prison by Tiberius for wishing Caius were emperor, one Thaumastus, a servant of Caius, carrying a pitcher of water, met him. Agrippa, being very thirsty, asked him for some of his water to drink, which he willingly gave; and Agrippa said, “This service you have done in giving me drink shall do you good another day.” And he was as big and as good as his word. For afterwards, when Caius was emperor and Agrippa was made king of Judea, Agrippa first got Thaumastus his liberty, then made him chief officer of his household, and at his own death arranged that Thaumastus should continue in the same office under his son. Now how much more will the King of kings reward all those poor pious servants of His who not only give Him cups of cold water in His members, but also redeem time from their very rest, meals, and recreations, so that they may have some time to seek the face of God in a corner!

Certainly, there will not be a sigh, a groan, a prayer, a tear let fall by a poor servant in a corner that the great God will not at last regard and reward. Lyra says that Mordecai waited six years before his good service was rewarded by King Ahasuerus. Perhaps God will reward you sooner for all your closet services; but if He does not reward you sooner, He will certainly reward you better. He will reward you with higher honors, with greater dignities, with more glorious robes, and with a more royal crown, even an incorruptible crown, a crown of righteousness, a crown of life, a crown of glory (1 Cor 9:25; 2 Tim 4:8; Rev 2:10; Jas 1:12; 1 Pet 5:4). And therefore hold on and hold out in your+ secret retreats. Though some may deride you+, and others revile you+, and your+ carnal masters discourage you+, yet God is faithful and will certainly reward you+. Indeed, He will openly reward you+ for all the secret pourings out of your+ souls in His bosom.

Objection 3: We Lack Those Gifts and Endowments That Others Have

Some may further object and say, “Oh, but we cannot pray alone; we lack those gifts and endowments that others have. We are tongue-tied and do not know how to pour out our souls before God in a corner. We would willingly pray, but we lack the ability to pour out our souls before the Lord in secret.”

1. God’s Dearest Children May Sometimes Be Tongue-Tied

First, God’s dearest children may sometimes be tongue-tied; they may, like Zechariah, be struck dumb for a time and unable to speak (Luke 1:20). “I am too troubled to speak…O Lord, my every desire is before You; my groaning is not hidden from You” (Ps 77:4; 38:9). God’s dearest children have sometimes been so tongue-tied that they could say nothing, nor do anything but groan. A child of God may sometimes meet with such a blow—from God, from conscience, from Scripture, from Satan, from the world—that it stuns him for a time, so that he is not able to speak to God, nor speak to others, nor speak to his own heart. Just as the Holy Spirit is not always a teaching Spirit, nor always a leading Spirit, nor always a comforting Spirit, nor always a sealing Spirit, nor always a witnessing Spirit, nor always an assuring Spirit to any of the saints, so He is not always a supplicating Spirit in any of the saints. When He is grieved, vexed, quenched, provoked, He may suspend His gracious influences and deny the soul His assistance. And what can a Christian say or do then?

2. There May Be the Spirit of Adoption in Sighs and Groans

Secondly, I answer, you cannot pray; but can you not sigh, nor groan either? There may be the Spirit of adoption in sighs and groans, as well as in spoken prayer (Rom 8:26). The force, the power, the efficacy, the excellence of prayer does not consist in the number and flourish of words, but in the supernatural stirrings of the Spirit—in sighs, and groans, and pangs, and strong affections of heart that are unspeakable and unutterable. Certainly, the very soul of prayer lies in the pouring out of a man’s soul before the Lord, though it be only in sighs, groans, and tears (1 Sam 1:13–19). One sigh and groan from a broken heart is more pleasing to God than all human eloquence.

3. Beg God to Teach You to Pray

Thirdly, I answer, beg God to teach you to pray. Oh, beg for the Holy Spirit, Who is a Spirit of prayer. God has promised His Holy Spirit to those who ask for it. “If you+ then, who are evil, know how to give good gifts to your+ children, how much more will your+ Father in heaven give the Holy Spirit to those who ask Him!” (Luke 11:13). “I will give you+ a new heart and put a new spirit within you+; I will remove your+ heart of stone and give you+ a heart of flesh. And I will put My Spirit within you+ and cause you+ to walk in My statutes and to carefully observe My ordinances…And I will give them singleness of heart and put a new spirit within them; I will remove their heart of stone and give them a heart of flesh…I will pour out on the house of David and on the people of Jerusalem a spirit of grace and supplication” (Ezek 36:26–27; 11:19; Zech 12:10).

Now gracious promises are God’s bonds, and He loves to see His people sue on them. God expects us to put Him in remembrance, and to pray over His promises (Isa 62:6–7; Isa 43:25–26). When He had promised great things to His people concerning justification, sanctification, and preservation, He adds, “For this, too, I will be sought by the house of Israel, to do it for them” (Ezek 36:37).

God expects us to spread His gracious promises before Him, as Hezekiah did Sennacherib’s letter (Isa 37:14). God is never better pleased than when His people importune Him in His own words and urge Him with arguments taken from His own promises. Though God is a very affectionate father, and a very generous father, yet He is not a prodigal father, for He will never throw away His blessings on those who will not boldly and humbly plead His promises with Him. God loves to keep His royal state, and will be sought, both for His bestowing of blessings and for His making good of precious promises. You say you cannot pray. Why, can you not go into a corner and spread the promises just cited before the Lord, and tell Him how much it concerns His honor and glory, as well as your own internal and eternal good, to make good those gracious promises that He has made: His giving of His Spirit to those who ask Him, and His putting His Spirit within them, and His pouring out a Spirit of grace and supplication on them?

We read of Tamar that, when Judah her father-in-law lay with her, she took his signet, bracelets, and staff as a pledge. Afterward, when she was in great distress and ready to be burned as a harlot, she brought out her staff, and signet, and bracelets, and said, “I am pregnant by the man to whom these items belong,” and so she saved her life (Gen 38:18, 25). The promises are like so many rich mines; they are like so many choice flowers of paradise; they are the food, life, and strength of the soul. They are like a staff to support the soul, and they are like a signet and bracelets to adorn the soul and to enrich the soul. And therefore poor sinners should bring them out, and lay them before the Lord, and urge God with them, for there is no way on earth like this to save a man’s soul and to prevent a burning in hell. Concerning precious promises, let me give you+ these eight hints:

First, that they are truly set forth and settled by God (Mark 10:30).

Secondly, that they will certainly be fulfilled, since they are all made in and through Christ (2 Cor 1:20). They are made first to Christ, and then to all who have union and communion with Him.

Sertorius, says Plutarch, paid what he promised with fair words, but God does not do so. Men many times say and unsay; they often eat their words as soon as they have spoken them. But God will never eat the words that have gone out of His mouth. “My purpose will stand, and all My good pleasure I will accomplish…Truly I have spoken, and truly I will bring it to pass. I have planned it, and I will surely do it” (Isa 46:10–11).

Thirdly, that they all flow from free grace, from special love, from divine goodness (Hos 14:4).

Fourthly, that they are all as unchangeable as He who made them (Jer 31:3).

Fifthly, that they are all grounded and founded on the truth, faithfulness, and all-sufficiency of God (Mal 3:6).

Sixthly, that they are pledges and guarantees of great things that God will do for His people in time (Heb 13:5).

Seventhly, that they are most sure and certain evidences of divine favor, and a declaration of the heart and goodwill of God to His poor people (Heb 6:12; Num 23:19).

Eighthly, that they are the price of Christ’s blood.

Now, how all these things should encourage poor souls to be always pressing God with His promises!

4. If You Cannot Pray as You Would, Pray as Well as You Can

Fourthly, you+ say you+ cannot pray, etc. Oh, that you+ would stop objecting and start praying! If you+ cannot pray as you+ would, nor as you+ should, pray as well as you+ can. Joseph’s brothers stood so long dallying, and delaying, and trifling away the time that, having a journey to make to buy grain, they might have bought and returned twice before they went and bought once. When Elijah called Elisha, he beat around the bush, and had to go and bid his father and mother farewell before he could follow the prophet (1 Kgs 19:20). O friends! Beware of dallying, delaying, trifling, and beating around the bush when you+ should be getting down to the work of prayer. What if, with Hannah, you can only weep out a prayer, or with Moses stammer out a prayer, or with Hezekiah chatter out a prayer? Yet do as well as you can, and you will find acceptance with God. “For if the eagerness is there, the gift is acceptable according to what one has, not according to what he does not have” (2 Cor 8:12). The tax collector’s prayer had not much rhetoric or eloquence in it—“God, have mercy on me, a sinner!” (Luke 18:13)—and yet God accepted it. He prayed much, though he spoke little, and God did not turn a deaf ear to him. The God who once accepted a handful of flour for a sacrifice, and a handful of goats’ hair for an offering, and the poor widow’s two mites as if they had been two millions, will certainly accept what you are able to do, though you fall short, yes, far short, of what you ought to do (Lev 2:1–2; 6:15; Luke 21:3). “Lord,” says Luther, “You command me to pray. I cannot pray as I would, yet I will obey; for though my prayer is not acceptable, yet Your own commandment is acceptable to You.” If weak Christians would only put forth in prayer the little strength they have, God would quickly renew their spiritual strength. He would certainly carry them on from strength to strength. He would always, by secret assistance and secret influences, help them on in their heavenly trade (Isa 40:29–31; Ps 84:7). As a loving, indulgent father will take his little child in his arms and carry him on in his way homeward when his strength begins to fail him, and he can walk no further, and the way proves dirty, slippery, or uneven, so God does with His own: “It was I who taught Ephraim to walk (as a nurse does the infant), taking them in My arms” (Hos 11:3). When God’s poor children come to a foul road or a rough place, He takes them up in His own arms, and helps them over the quagmire of trials and the difficulties of duties, and over all that constraint, and narrowness, and weakness of spirit that attends them in their closet duties.

It is worth noting that when the king of Israel was to shoot the arrow, he put his hand on the bow, and Elisha put his hand on the king’s hand (2 Kgs 13:16). So when we go into our closets, we are to put up our hand, and then the Spirit of God likewise will put His hand on our hand. He will put His strength to our strength, or rather to our weakness. “In the same way, the Spirit helps us in our infirmities” (Rom 8:26): lifts with us, or helps together. The Greek word συναντιλαμβάνεται properly signifies such help as when another man of strength and ability steps in to sustain the burden that lies on our shoulders, be it a log or a piece of timber, setting his shoulders under it to lift it up and bear part of it with us; or to help us as the nurse helps her little child, holding it up by the sleeve. When a poor Christian sets himself to closet prayer, to mourn, to believe, or to obey, etc., then the Spirit comes in with new help, and new influences, and new assistance, and so carries him on in all these noble services. The child that only stammers at first will in time speak plainly and fluently. Oh, how many Christians there are who now can pray with much freedom, liberty, and fluency, who at first could only sigh out a prayer, or stammer out a prayer, or weep out a prayer!

You say you cannot pray; but if you would only stir yourself up to obey that command (Matt 6:6) as well as you can, for all you know, a power may go forth with the command that may enable you to act in keeping with the command. In Matthew 9:1–9 Christ told the paralyzed man to rise and walk: “Get up, pick up your mat, and go home.” The paralyzed man might have objected, “Alas! I am carried by four. I am not able to stir a limb, much less to rise, and least of all to pick up my mat and walk.” Oh, but he roused himself up as well as he could, and a power went forth with the command that enabled him to do what was commanded. So there was a poor man who had a withered hand, and Christ commanded him to stretch out his hand (Matt 12:10–14). He might have replied, “My hand is withered, and if I might have as many worlds as there are men in the world for stretching it out, I could not stretch it out. Yes, if my very life—if my very salvation—depended on stretching out my withered arm, I could not stretch it out.” Oh! But he throws aside all such pleas and complies with Christ’s command as well as he could, and a power went forth and healed his hand.

O sirs! If you+ would only pray in your+ closets as well as you+ can, for all you+ know such power and force might flow from Christ into your+ hearts that it would carry you+ on in your+ closet duties beyond all expectation, to the point of wonder. Others have found it so, and why not you+? Why not you+? Well! Remember that God is no exacting or critical observer of the awkward expressions that fall from His poor children when they are at their closet duties. He is a Father who is very well pleased with the broken expressions and divine stammerings of His people when they are in a corner. What catches the ear and delights the heart of God, what opens the gates of glory and brings down the best of blessings on the soul, is not a flood of words, nor studied notions, nor seraphic expressions, nor elegant phrases in prayer, but uprightness, holiness, heavenliness, spiritual-mindedness, and brokenness of heart. These are the things that make a conquest of God and that turn most to the soul’s profit.

5. The Spirit of God Is a Spirit of Prayer and Supplication

Fifthly, you say you cannot pray; but if you are a child of God, you have the Spirit of God, and the Spirit of God is a Spirit of prayer and supplication. That all the children of God have the Spirit of God is most evident in the blessed Scriptures. Take these for a taste: “I will pour out on the house of David and on the people of Jerusalem a spirit of grace and supplication” (Zech 12:10). “Take not Your Holy Spirit from me” (Ps 51:11). “You+ received the Spirit of adoption, by whom we cry, ‘Abba! Father!’” (Rom 8:15). “We have not received the spirit of the world, but the Spirit who is from God, that we may understand what God has freely given us” (1 Cor 2:12). “…the very One who gives you+ His Holy Spirit” (1 Thess 4:8). “And by this we know that He remains in us: by the Spirit He has given us…By this we know that we remain in Him, and He in us: He has given us of His Spirit” (1 John 3:24; 4:13).

That all the children of God have the Spirit of God may be made further evident by an induction of these seven particulars.

[1] First, they are all sanctified by the Spirit. “You+ were sanctified…by the Spirit of our God” (1 Cor 6:11). I do not say that they are all equally sanctified by the Spirit, but I say they are all really sanctified by the Spirit. All the servants of Christ have their talents, but not all have ten talents, nor all five talents, nor all two talents; some have only one talent (Matt 25:15). Benjamin’s portion was five times as large as his brothers’ portions, yet every one of his brothers had his portion (Gen 43:32–34). So, although some Christians have five times more measures of the Spirit, and more measures of light, of love, of holiness, of heavenly-mindedness, than others have, every Christian has some measure of the Spirit and some measure of grace and holiness. Some are babes in Christ and others are children in Christ, some are young men in Christ and others old men in Christ, yet every one of them is born of the Spirit of Christ (1 Pet 2:2; 1 John 2:12–14; John 3:8). None of the people of God in this life has the Spirit in perfection, yet every one of them has enough of the Spirit to bring him to salvation. Every Christian has enough of the Spirit to bring Christ and his soul together; and therefore, beyond all doubt, every Christian has enough of the Spirit to bring heaven and his soul together at last.

[2] Secondly, they are all led by the Spirit. “All who are led by the Spirit of God are sons of God” (Rom 8:14). Every child of God has a twofold guide: the Word outside him and the Spirit within him (Isa 30:20–21). I will not undertake now to show how the Spirit leads by the rule of the Word, and how He leads to God, and leads to Christ, and leads to truth, and leads to righteousness, and leads to holiness, and leads to happiness (Prov 6:22; Eph 5:9).

[3] Thirdly, they are all upheld and strengthened by the Spirit. “Uphold me with Your free Spirit” (Ps 51:12), or, as the Hebrew has it, prop me up or sustain me with Your free, voluntary Spirit; or, as the Greek renders it, with Your noble, princely Spirit. “To be strengthened with power through His Spirit in the inner man” (Eph 3:16). By “the inner man” some understand the regenerate part of man; others understand “the inner man” to mean the soul with all its noble faculties and stirrings. Whichever way you+ take the words, it is certain that a Christian has all his spiritual might and strength from the Holy Spirit. The Spirit strengthens every Christian in the inner man, but I do not say that He strengthens every Christian alike in the inward man. Some have stronger corruptions to subdue than others, and more violent temptations to withstand than others, and greater difficulties to wrestle with than others, and choicer blessings to make use of than others, and higher and harder duties of religion to manage than others; and accordingly they are more strengthened in the inner man than others.

[4] Fourthly, they are all partakers of the firstfruits of the Spirit. “We ourselves, who have the firstfruits of the Spirit” (Rom 8:23), which are only like a handful of grain compared with the whole crop. All the grace and all the holiness that we have from the regenerating Spirit at our first conversion is only a drop to that sea, a mite to those talents, which we shall receive in the life to come (2 Cor 1:22).

[5] Fifthly, they are all taught by the Spirit. “The Holy Spirit, whom the Father will send in My name, will teach you+ all things” (John 14:26; Isa 59:21).

This promise belongs primarily to the apostles, and secondarily to all believers.

Though these words were spoken at first to the apostles only, yet they were not spoken about the apostles only (Isa 54:13). In these words there are three things promised to the apostles:

First, immediate illumination by the Spirit of God.

Secondly, a full knowledge of all those truths that belonged to their apostolic office and that were necessary for them at that particular time.

Thirdly, absolute infallibility in matters of doctrine.

There are also three things promised to all believers:

First, mediate illumination: truths taught by the Spirit of truth in the use of the means of grace.

Secondly, knowledge of all truth necessary to salvation.

Thirdly, infallibility too, so far as they adhere and keep close to the Spirit’s teaching in the Word.

Philo says that the early Christians were called tillers, because, as farmers till their fields and manure their land, so they taught their families and nurtured their children and servants with good instruction. Oh, what choice teachings of the Spirit these early Christians were under, who made it so much their business, their work, to teach those who were under their charge (1 Thess 4:9; 2 Cor 3:8)! “But the anointing you+ received from Him remains in you+, and you+ do not need anyone to teach you+. But as His anointing teaches you+ about all things and is true” (1 John 2:27). Not that we know all things absolutely, or that we do not need a ministry to teach and instruct us; but he speaks comparatively: you+ will not be so helped by any instruction without the Spirit as with the Spirit. The Spirit will declare the truth as it is in Jesus more clearly, more freely, more particularly, more certainly, more universally, more effectually, than any other is able to do. The Spirit, this holy anointing, will teach the saints all things; not all things that can be known, for finite beings cannot possibly attain to that. Who knows the movements of the heavens, the influences of the stars, the nature of created things, or how the bones grow in the womb of her who is with child? Who knows the reason why the river Nile overflows in the summer, when waters are at their lowest? Or why the lodestone draws iron to it, or turns toward the pole star?

Pliny tells us of one who spent fifty-eight years in learning the nature of the bee, and yet had not fully attained it. How is it possible, then, for the wisest naturalist to enter into the deep things of God?

Paul, who learned his theology among the angels, and who had the Holy Spirit for his immediate teacher, tells us plainly that he knew only in part (1 Cor 13:9–11); and oh, then, how little a part of that part do we know! But the Spirit teaches the saints all things; that is,

First, He teaches them all things needed for the salvation of their souls, all things necessary to bring them to heaven (John 17:3).

Secondly, all things needed for life and godliness (2 Pet 1:3).

Thirdly, all things needed for their stations, callings, sexes, ages, and conditions.

Fourthly, all things you+ need to know to preserve you+ in the truth, and to preserve you+ from being deluded and seduced by those false teachers of whom he speaks (1 John 2:18, 19, 22–23, 26). And certainly this is the main thing that John hints at in that expression. The “all things” spoken of in verse 27 must necessarily be understood, in Scripture’s usual way of speaking, to mean only those things that are spoken of there.

[6] Sixthly, they are all comforted by the Spirit. “…living in the fear of the Lord and the comfort of the Holy Spirit…For the kingdom of God is not a matter of eating and drinking, but of righteousness, peace, and joy in the Holy Spirit…And you+ became imitators of us and of the Lord when you+ welcomed the message with the joy of the Holy Spirit, in spite of your+ great suffering” (Acts 9:31; Rom 14:17; 1 Thess 1:6). Not that all Christians always have actual comfort, actual joy. Oh no! For as the air is sometimes clear and sometimes cloudy, and as the sea is sometimes ebbing and sometimes flowing, so the comforts and joys of the people of God are sometimes ebbing and sometimes flowing, sometimes clear and sometimes cloudy.

Hudson the martyr, being deserted by God at the stake, went out from under his chain, and having prayed earnestly, was comforted immediately and suffered valiantly.

So Mr. Glover the martyr was deserted by God in prison; but as he was going to the stake he looked back and cried out to his friend, “He has come, He has come,” meaning the Comforter, and so he laid down his life with joy.

Rachel wept and would not be comforted. She gave so much way to weeping that she would not give the least way to comfort; and so it is many times with the choicest saints. “My soul refused to be comforted” (Ps 77:2). It is not my purpose at present to dwell on the various ways in which the people of God refuse comfort and fall short of those strong consolations which God is willing for them to receive. The sun may work where it does not shine, and a man may be in a state of salvation and yet lack consolation. A man may fear the Lord and obey the voice of His servant, and yet walk in darkness and see no light (Isa 50:10). Any Christian may at times have trouble in his conscience, grief in his heart, tears in his eyes, and fears and questionings in his soul whether God is his Father, and whether Christ is his redeemer, and whether mercy belongs to him, and even whether any promise in the book of God belongs to him.

Joy and comfort are those delicacies, those sweetmeats of heaven, that God does not feast His people with every day (Ps 30:6–7). Not every day is a wedding day, not every day is a harvest day, and not every day is a summer’s day. The fatted calf is not killed every day, nor are the robe and the ring put on every day. Not every day is a festival day or a dancing day (Luke 15:22–23; Eccl 3:4; Rom 12:15). As there is a time to sing, so there is a time to sigh. As there is a time to laugh, so there is a time to weep. And as there is a time to dance, so there is a time to mourn. Not until all sin is wholly taken out of our hearts will all tears be wiped clean from our eyes.

But for all this, gracious souls always have sure and choice grounds of consolation. They have the promises. They have the “firstfruits of the Spirit” (Rom 8:23). They have union with Christ. And they have a right to eternal life, though they do not always have felt comforts. The children of God always have cause to exercise faith and hope in God in their darkest condition, though they do not always have actual joy and consolation (Job 13:15; Ps 42:5). The Comforter always abides with the saints, though He does not always actually comfort the saints (John 14:16). The Spirit many times carries on His sanctifying work in the soul when He does not carry on His comforting work in the soul. The Spirit many times works to humble when He does not work to console. The Spirit many times fills the soul with godly sorrow when He does not fill the soul with holy joy. In His comforting work, the Spirit’s actions all depend on His own sovereign will and pleasure; and therefore He may abide in the soul when He does not actually comfort the soul.

[7] Seventhly, the people of God are sooner or later sealed by the Spirit. “In Him … after … you+ believed, you+ were sealed with the Holy Spirit of promise” (Eph 1:13). The nature of sealing consists in the imparting of the image or character of the seal to the thing sealed. To seal a thing is to stamp the character of the seal on it. Now, the Spirit of God really and effectually imparts the image of God to us, and that image consists in righteousness and true holiness. We are truly sealed by the Spirit of God when the Holy Spirit stamps the image of grace and holiness so obviously, so evidently, on the soul that the soul sees it, feels it, and can read it at a glance. Then the soul is sealed by the Holy Spirit.

“And do not grieve the Holy Spirit of God, in whom you+ were sealed for the day of redemption” (Eph 4:30). The person of the Holy Spirit is set forth here in the Greek with a very great force, such as our tongue is not able fully to express. Here are three words that have three articles, every word its own article by itself, τὸ πνεῦμα τὸ ἅγιον, τοῦ θεοῦ: “The Spirit,” not “a Spirit.” And not “holy,” but “the holy.” Nor “of God,” but “of that God.” “Who also placed His seal on us, and put His Spirit in our hearts as a pledge of what is to come” (2 Cor 1:22).

In these scriptures, you+ see that the Spirit is a seal. Now, a seal among men is,

First, for secrecy.

Secondly, for distinction.

Thirdly, for authority.

Fourthly, for certainty. A sealed writing is authentic, and serves to make things sure.

In the three texts last cited, if you+ compare them together, you+ may observe these six things:

First, the person sealing, and that is the Father.

Secondly, in whom, in Christ.

Thirdly, with what seal, the Spirit of promise. Here are all the persons of the Trinity making us sure of our inheritance.

Fourthly, when, after you+ believed.

Fifthly, the end, which is twofold:

(1) Subordinate, and that is the certainty of our salvation;

(2) Ultimate, and that is the praise of His glory.

Sixthly, the time, how long this seal and pledge shall assure us, and that is, until we have complete possession of that of which it is a pledge.

To prevent mistakes and disputes about the sealings of the Spirit on the one hand, and to support, comfort, and encourage the poor people of God on the other hand, let me briefly touch on the Spirit’s special sealing times.

[1] First, conversion times are often the Spirit’s sealing times. On the prodigal’s return, the fattened calf is killed, the best robe is put on his back, the ring is put on his hand, and shoes on his feet (Luke 15:22–23). Some understand by the robe the royalty of Adam, others the righteousness of Christ. And by the ring some understand the pledges of God’s love, rings being given as pledges of love; others understand by the ring the seal of God’s Holy Spirit, since men used their rings as seals. Among the Romans the ring was a badge of virtue, honor, and nobility, by which those who wore them were distinguished from the common people. I think the main thing intended by the robe and the ring is to show us that God sometimes, on the sinner’s conversion and return to Him, is graciously pleased to give him some choice manifestations of His gracious pleasure and goodwill, and to seal up to him His everlasting love and favor. And this is why some who are only babes in Christ (1 Pet 2:2–3; 1 John 2:12–14) are so diligent and active in religious duties, and so conscientious and skillful in the exercise of their graces. At their first conversion, God helps some of His people to read their own names written in legible letters in the Book of Life (Acts 9:3–6). No sooner are some converted than the Spirit stamps His seal on them.

[2] Secondly, believing times are sealing times (Eph 1:13). When they were in the very exercise of their faith, when they were exercising their faith—for that is what the original implies—the Spirit came and sealed them up for the day of redemption (Rom 15:13; 1 Pet 1:8). If a man honors Christ by frequent acts of faith in Him, Christ will honor him by setting His seal and mark on him.

[3] Thirdly, humbling times, mourning times, are sealing times. When a holy man was asked which were the most joyful days, the most comforting days, that he had ever enjoyed, he answered, his mourning days. His mourning days were his most joyful days, and therefore he cried out, “Oh, give me my mourning days, give me my mourning days, for they were my most joyful days!” Those were days in which God sealed up His everlasting love to his soul (Job 22:29; Isa 29:19). When the prodigal had greatly humbled himself before his father, then the best robe and the ring were put on him (Luke 15:17–24). None long for the sealings of the Spirit like humble souls; none set so high a price on the sealings of the Spirit as humble souls; and none make such choice use of the sealings of the Spirit as humble souls. And therefore, when men’s hearts are humble and low, the Spirit comes and sets the privy seal of heaven on them.

[4] Fourthly, sin-killing, sin-mortifying, sin-subduing times are the Spirit’s sealing times. “To the one who overcomes, I will give the hidden manna. I will also give him a white stone inscribed with a new name, known only to the one who receives it” (Rev 2:17).

God will give the victorious Christian a secret love-token by which his soul may rest assured of the unspeakable love of God and of its freedom from condemnation. White stones were widely used among the Romans and among the Athenians, and served to acquit the accused in courts of justice. When wrongdoers were accused, tried, and condemned in their courts, they were given a black stone as a token of condemnation; but when they were acquitted, they were given white stones as a token of acquittal. The Holy Spirit seems to allude to this practice. He who is victorious over his lusts will have a new name that is better than the names of sons and daughters (Isa 56:5); and he will have the pardon of his sins written in fair letters on the white stone, so that he may read his acquittal at a glance. The victorious Christian will have assurance of the full discharge of all his sins. He will have clear evidence of his justification and blessed assurance of his eternal election, all of which are hidden and mysterious things to all but those who have experienced and tasted what these sweetmeats of heaven mean (1 John 1:7).

Among the Romans, solemn feasts were held in honor of those who were victorious in their sacred games. Now those who were to be admitted to those feasts used to have their names written on white shells and white stones, and by these tickets they were admitted. Now some think the Holy Spirit alludes to this practice, and so would point us to a secret mark by which victorious Christians may be known and admitted as invited guests to the heavenly banquet of the hidden manna, according to Revelation 19:9. O sirs! When predominant lusts are brought under, when bosom sins lie slain in the soul, then the Spirit comes and seals up love, and life, and glory to the soul.

[5] Fifthly, suffering times are sealing times (Acts 7:55–56, 59–60; Rev 1:9–10; 2 Cor 4:15–17). The early Christians found them so, and the suffering saints in Queen Mary’s days found them so.

When the furnace is seven times hotter than usual, the Spirit of the Lord comes and seals up a man’s pardon in his bosom, and his peace with God, and his title to heaven. When the world frowns most, then God smiles most. When the world puts its iron chains on the saints’ legs, then God puts His golden chains about the saints’ necks. When the world puts a bitter cup into one hand, then the Lord puts a cup of consolation into the other hand. When the world cries out, “Crucify them, crucify them!” then commonly they hear that sweet voice from heaven, “These are My beloved ones, in whom I am well pleased.”

Blessed Bradford looked on his sufferings as evidence to him that he was on the right way to heaven. And Ignatius says, “It is better for me to be a martyr than to be a monarch.”

[6] Sixthly, self-denying times are the Spirit’s sealing times (Matt 19:27–29).

First, there is sinful self, which takes in a man’s lusts.

Secondly, there is natural self, which takes in a man’s skills, talents, gifts, and reason.

Thirdly, there is religious self, which takes in all a man’s religious duties and services, whether ordinary or extraordinary.

Fourthly, there is moral self, which includes freedom from gross, heinous, enormous wickedness, and a fair, sweet, harmless behavior toward men.

Fifthly, there is relative self, which takes in our nearest and dearest relations in the flesh, such as wife, children, father, mother, brothers, sisters, etc. (Ps 45:7–11).

Now when a man comes in this way to deny himself in all these things for Christ’s sake, and the gospel’s sake, and religion’s sake, then the Spirit of the Lord comes and seals him up for the day of redemption. This is a truth confirmed by the experiences of many martyrs now in heaven, and by the testimony of many Christians still alive.

[7] Seventhly, sacrament times are sealing times. In that “feast of rich food” (Isa 25:6), God by His Spirit seals up His love to His people, and His covenant to His people, and pardon of sin to His people, and heaven and happiness to His people. There are many precious souls who have found Christ in this ordinance when they could not find Him in other ordinances, though they had sought Him with sorrow. In this ordinance, many a distressed soul has been strengthened, comforted, and sealed.

I might give you+ many instances. Take one for all. There was a gracious woman who, after God had filled her soul with comfort and sealed up His everlasting love to her, fell back into her former fears and trouble of spirit. While she was at the Lord’s Supper, a little before the bread was given to her, Satan seemed to appear to her and told her that she must not presume to eat. But in the very nick of time, the Lord was pleased to bring into her mind that passage in the Song of Songs, “Eat, O friends” (Song 5:1). But even so, Satan went on terrifying her, and when she had eaten, he told her that she must not drink. But then the Lord brought the second clause of the verse to her remembrance: “drink; drink freely” (or, “be drunk,” as the Hebrew has it), “O beloved” (or, “my loves,” as the Hebrew has it—all faithful souls are Christ’s loves). And so she drank also, and at once was filled with such unspeakable joys that she hardly knew how she got home. These soul-enrapturing joys continued for two weeks afterward and filled her mouth with songs of praise, so that she could neither sleep nor eat more than she forced herself to out of a conscientious sense of duty. At the end of the two weeks, when God was pleased to lessen her measure of joy, she came to a settled peace of conscience and assurance of the love of God, so that for twenty years afterward she had not so much as a cloud on her spirit, or the least questioning of her share in Christ.

[8] Eighthly, when God calls His people to some great and noble work, when He sets them on some high services, some difficult duties, some holy and eminent employments, then His Spirit comes and sets His seal on them. “Before I formed you in the womb I knew you, and before you were born I set you apart and appointed you as a prophet to the nations” (Jer 1:5). When the Lord sent the prophet Jeremiah to pronounce most dreadful judgments against a rebellious people, a shameless, brazen-faced nation, He assured him of his eternal election, and of His choice presence and singular help in the work that He set him to (vv. 8, 17-19). This is how the Lord dealt with Peter, James, and John (Matt 17:1–6), and this is how He dealt with Paul (Acts 9:1–23).

[9] Ninthly, when they are taken up into more than ordinary communion with God, that is the Spirit’s sealing time. When was it that the bride cried out, “My beloved is mine, and I am his!” if not when Christ brought her to His banqueting house, and His banner over her was love? (Compare Song 2:16, 3–6, etc.)

[10] Tenthly and lastly, when Christians give themselves up to private prayer, when Christians are more than ordinarily exercised in secret prayer, in closet duties, then the Spirit comes and seals up the covenant and the love of the Father to them. When Daniel had been wrestling and weeping, and weeping and wrestling all day long with God in his closet, then the angel told him that he was a man “greatly beloved” of God, or a man of great desires, as the original has it (Dan 9:20–23). There was a gracious woman who, after attending many sermons and walking in the ways of the Lord, fell into great spiritual desertions; but while she was in secret prayer, God came in with abundance of light and comfort, sealing up to her soul this part of His covenant: “I will remove their heart of stone and give them a heart of flesh, so that they may follow My statutes, keep My ordinances, and practice them. Then they will be My people, and I will be their God” (Ezek 11:19–20).

And so I have given you+ a brief account of the Spirit’s special sealing times. Now note this: God sets this seal on all His wares, on all His adopted children; for sooner or later there are none of His who are not sealed with this seal. God sets His seal of regeneration, He stamps His image of holiness on all His people, to mark them off and distinguish them from all profane, merely moral, and hypocritical persons in the world (John 3:3; 2 Thess 2:13; Heb 12:14). Doubtless the sanctifying work of the Holy Spirit, imprinting the outlines and features of God’s image of righteousness and holiness on man, as a seal or signet leaves an impression and stamp of its likeness on the thing sealed, is the seal of the Spirit spoken of in Scripture. “God’s firm foundation stands, bearing this seal: ‘The Lord knows those who are His,’ and, ‘Everyone who calls on the name of the Lord must turn away from iniquity’” (2 Tim 2:19).

But to prevent mistakes, you+ must remember that though the Spirit of the Lord will sooner or later set His seal on every real saint, the impression of that seal is not equally visible in all; for some bear this impression as babes, others as men grown up to some maturity. All God’s adopted children bear this impression truly, but none of them bear it perfectly in this life. Sometimes this seal of regeneration, this seal of holiness, is so plain and obvious that a man may read it at a glance in himself and others. And at other times it is so obscure and dark that he can hardly discern it, either in himself or in others. This seal is so vividly stamped on some of God’s people that it reveals itself very visibly, eminently, gloriously; but on others it is not equally visible. And so I have made it evident by these seven particulars that all the children of God have the Spirit of God.

Now note this: the Spirit of God that is in all the saints is a Spirit of prayer and supplication. “You+ received the Spirit of adoption, by whom we cry, ‘Abba! Father!’” (Rom 8:15). While the child is in the womb, it cannot cry; but as soon as it is born, it cries. While Paul lay in the womb of his natural state, he could not pray; but no sooner was he born of the Spirit than the next news is, “Behold, he is praying” (Acts 9:11). Prayer is nothing but the turning of a man’s inside out before the Lord. The very soul of prayer lies in the pouring out of a man’s soul into the bosom of God. Prayer is nothing but the breathing out before the Lord of what was first breathed into us by the Spirit of the Lord. Prayer is nothing but a choice, a free, a sweet, and intimate fellowship of the soul with God. Certainly, it is a great work of the Spirit to help the saints to pray.

“Because you+ are sons, God sent the Spirit of His Son into your+ hearts, crying out, ‘Abba, Father!’” (Gal 4:6). God has no stillborn children. The doubling, “Abba, Father,” shows trusting, childlike, and vehement affection. The first is a Hebrew or Syriac word, the second a Greek one, and by this is signified the union of the Hebrews and the Greeks, or the Jews and Gentiles, in one church. “Abba, Father.” What is Abba? Others say it is Father in Hebrew; and “Father” is added because in Christ the cornerstone both peoples are joined, alike becoming sons, wherever they come from: the circumcision from one place, hence Abba; the uncircumcision from another, hence Father is named. The harmony of the walls is the glory of the cornerstone.

The word Abba, others say, means father in the Syriac tongue, which the apostle keeps here because it is a word full of affection, which young children use in almost all languages when they begin to speak. And he adds the word Father, not only to explain it, but also to express better the eager stirrings, the earnest and vehement desires, and the singular affections of believers in their crying to God, just as Christ Himself repeated the word Father for the same purpose when He was in His greatest distress (Mark 14:36). This little word Father, says Luther, lisped out in prayer by a child of God, exceeds the eloquence of Demosthenes, Cicero, and all the other famous orators in the world.

It is certain that the Spirit of God helps the saints in all their communion with God, in their meditations on God, in their reading and hearing of the Word of God, in their communion with one another, and in all their solemn addresses to God. And for this the apostle gives us a most special instance in Romans 8:26: “In the same way, the Spirit helps us in our infirmities. For we do not know what we should pray for as we ought, but the Spirit Himself intercedes for us with groans too deep for words.”

When we are to pray, there is in us sometimes an infirmity of ignorance, so that we do not know what to pray for, either in matter or in manner. And at other times there is in us an infirmity of pride and conceit, so that we cannot pray with the humility and lowliness of spirit that we should, spiritual pride having fly-blown our prayers. Sometimes there is in us an infirmity of deadness, dullness, drowsiness, etc., so that we cannot pray with the warmth, heat, life, spirit, and fervency that we should or that we would. And at other times there is in us an infirmity of unbelief and slavish fears, so that we cannot pray with the faith and holy boldness that become children who draw near to a throne of grace and mercy. But now the Spirit helps these infirmities by way of instruction, prompting and teaching us what to pray for, and how we should spell our lesson. He tells us, as it were within, what we should say and how we should sigh and groan. He rouses and enlivens and stirs us up to prayer. By His singular influence and choice assistance He opens and enlarges our hearts in prayer. And by tuning the strings of our affections, He prepares us and fits us for the work of supplication. And therefore everyone who mocks the spirit of prayer in the saints, saying, “These are the men and the women who pray by the Spirit!” blasphemes against the Holy Spirit, since it is a main work of the Spirit to teach the saints to pray and to help them in prayer. Now, since all the saints have the Spirit, and the Spirit is a Spirit of prayer and supplication, there is no reason in the world why a saint should say, “I would pray in secret, but I cannot pray; I cannot pour out my soul or my complaint before the Lord in a corner.”

6. You Can Manage Your Worldly Business as Well as Others

Sixthly and lastly, you say you cannot pray; you do not have the gifts and talents that others have.
