But you can manage your callings, your worldly business, as well as others; why then can you not pray as well as others? Ah, friends! If you+ only loved private prayer as well as you+ love the world, and delighted in private prayer as much as you+ delight in the world, and if your+ hearts were as much set on closet prayer as they are set on the world, you+ would never say you+ could not pray; indeed, you+ would soon pray as well as others. It is not so much from lack of ability to pray in secret that you+ do not pray in secret, as it is from lack of a will, a heart, to pray in secret, that you+ do not pray in secret. Jacob’s love for Rachel, and Shechem’s love for Dinah, carried them through the greatest difficulties (Gen 29; 34). If men’s affections were only strongly set on private prayer, they would quickly find abilities to pray. He who sets his affections on a young woman, though he is neither learned nor eloquent, will find words enough to let her know how his heart is taken with her. The application is easy. The man in Seneca complained of a thorn in his foot when his lungs were rotten. So many complain of a lack of ability to pray in their closets when their hearts are rotten. Sirs! Only get better hearts, and then you+ will never say you+ can’t pray. It is one of the saddest sights in all the world to see men with strong talents and gifts for all worldly business crying out that they can’t pray, that they have no ability to pour out their souls before the Lord in secret. You+ have sufficient talents and gifts to tell men of your+ sins, your+ needs, your+ dangers, your+ difficulties, your+ blessings, your+ deliverances, your+ duties, your+ trials, your+ losses, your+ enjoyments, your+ friends, your+ foes. Why then are you+ not ashamed to complain of your+ lack of talents and gifts to tell those very things to God in a corner, which you+ can tell to men even on the housetops?

Objection 4: God Is Well Acquainted with All Our Needs

Fourthly, some may further object and say, “God is very well acquainted with all our needs, necessities, straits, and trials; and there is no moving Him to give us any favors that He does not intend to give us, whether we pray in our closets or not. So to what purpose do you press secret prayer so hard on us?”

To this objection I shall give these answers:

1. This Objection Lies as Strong Against Family and Public Prayer

First, this objection lies as strong against family prayer and public prayer as it does against private prayer. God knows all your needs and necessities, all your straits and trials; so why need you pray in your family, why need you attend public prayers in the communion of saints? There is no wringing any mercy out of the hands of heaven which God does not intend to give. This objection confronts every kind of prayer and fights against every kind of prayer.

2. Private Prayer Is Part of That Homage We Owe to God

Secondly, I answer that private prayer is a piece of divine worship and adoration; it is a part of the homage which we owe to God on account of a divine command, as I have already proved. Now, all objections must bow before the face of divine commands, as Joseph’s brothers bowed before him (Gen 42:6), or as King Ahasuerus’s servants bowed before Haman (Esth 3:2). Indeed, every objection that is raised against a divine command should fall before it, as Dagon fell before the ark, or as Goliath fell before David. He who casts off private prayer under any pretense whatever casts off the dominion of God, the authority of God, and this may be as much as a man’s life and soul are worth.

3. Prayer Is the Silver Channel, the Golden Pipe of Mercy

Thirdly, I answer: though prayer is not the ground, the cause, of obtaining favors and blessings from God, yet it is the means. It is the silver channel, it is the golden pipe, through which the Lord is pleased to convey to His people all temporal, spiritual, and eternal favors (Ezek 36:26–37). God promises to give them the cream, the choicest, the sweetest of all spiritual, eternal, and temporal blessings. But note verse 37: “For this, too, I will be sought by the house of Israel, to do it for them.” Though God is very prompt and ready to give His people the best and the greatest of blessings, yet He will be sought by prayer for the actual enjoyment of them. He who has no heart to pray for a mercy he needs has no ground to believe that God will ever give him the mercy he needs. There is no receiving without asking, no finding without seeking, no opening without knocking. The threefold promise attached to the threefold command in Matthew 7:7 should encourage all Christians to be urgent, fervent, and constant in prayer. The proud beggar gets nothing from men, and the silent sinner gets nothing from God. As there is no mercy too great for God to give, so there is no mercy too little for us to ask. Certainly the man who thinks any mercy not worth seeking has little worth in him.

4. Labor to Have Your Blessings Blessed to You

Fourthly and lastly, I answer: every Christian should labor to enjoy his mercies in mercy. He should labor to have his blessings blessed to him; he should labor to have “the favor of Him who dwelt in the burning bush” with all he has (Deut 33:16; Gen 22:17). Now this is an everlasting truth, a maxim to live and die with: whatever mercy does not come in on the wing of prayer is not given in mercy. Oh, how sweet is the mercy that comes flying in on the wing of prayer! How sweet to Samson was the water which streamed to him in the channel of private prayer (Judg 15:19); he called it En-hakkore, the well of him who prayed. Samson prayed as if for his life, and the water that was handed to him was as sweet as life. Every mercy that is gathered by the hand of prayer is as sweet as the rose of Sharon (Song 2:1). But the mercy that does not come in at the door of prayer does not come in at the right door; and the mercy that does not come in at the right door will do a man no good. Such mercies will make themselves wings and fly from us (Prov 23:5).

Every Christian should look closely to see that all his mercies are sanctified mercies. Now, every mercy is sanctified by the Word and prayer (1 Tim 4:4–5). Prayer prepares and fits us for mercy, and mercy for us. It is prayer that gives us a right and holy use of all our mercies. Mercies that do not come in on the wing of prayer are only great miseries. Prayerless men’s mercies are all given in wrath; indeed, their blessings are cursed to them (Prov 3:33; Mal 2:2). Just as every sacrifice was to be seasoned with salt, so every mercy is to be sanctified by prayer. Just as gold is sometimes laid not only on cloth and silks but also on silver itself, so prayer is the golden duty that must be laid not only on all our natural and everyday actions, such as eating, drinking, buying, selling, etc., but also on all our silver duties, on all our most religious and spiritual exercises, such as hearing, reading, meditating, spiritual conversation, church fellowship, breaking of bread, etc.

Certainly, prayer is very necessary to make every providence, and every ordinance, and every mercy a blessing to us. Every mercy that comes in on the wing of private prayer is a double mercy. It is a great-bellied mercy; it is a mercy that has many mercies in its womb. Happy is the Christian who can lay his hand on every mercy that he enjoys and say of them all, as Hannah once said of her Samuel, “I prayed for this boy, and the Lord has granted me what I asked of Him” (1 Sam 1:27).

Objection 5: I Lack a Suitable Place to Retire Into

Fifthly, some may further object and say, “I would carry on a private trade with God, I would exercise myself in secret prayer, but I lack a suitable place to retire into. I lack a private corner in which to unburden my soul to my Father.”

To this objection, I shall give these three short answers:

1. This Objection Concerns Only a Few Christians in Our Days

First, I suppose this objection concerns only a few Christians in our days. The God who has given a Christ to believers commonly gives them a suitable corner in which to enjoy private communion with Himself (Rom 8:32). Most Christians, I am afraid, lack a heart for private prayer rather than a suitable place for private prayer. What men set their hearts on, they will find time and place to carry out, whether it is good or evil, whether it concerns temporal or spiritual things, whether it concerns this world or another world, this life or a better life. If most men would only get better hearts, they would quickly find or make suitable places for private prayer. He who has an inflamed love for God will certainly find a corner in which to enjoy secret communion with God. True lovers will find corners in which to enjoy one another. How many men there are who can easily find private places for their dogs to lie in, and their swine to sleep in, and their horses to stand in, and their oxen to feed in, who cannot find a private place to seek the face of God in! But if these men only loved their God, or their souls, or private prayer, or eternity, as well as or better than their beasts, they would not be such brutes; they would quickly find a hole, a corner, to wait on the Lord in.

2. Peter on the House-Top, Isaac in the Field, Christ on the Mountain

Secondly, I answer: if a Christian is on the top of a house with Peter, he may pray there; or if he is walking in the field with Isaac, he may pray there; or if he is on the mountain with Christ, he may pray there; or if he is behind the door with Paul, he may pray there; or if he is waiting at table with Nehemiah, he may secretly pray there; or if he is in a wood, he may pray there, as the early Christians did in times of persecution; or if he is behind a tree, he may pray there; or if he is by the seaside, he may pray there, as the apostles did. It was a choice saying of Augustine: “Every saint is God’s temple,” he says, “and he who carries his temple about with him may go to prayer when he pleases.” Some saints have never had so much of heaven brought down into their hearts as when they have been with God in a corner. Oh, the secret manifestations of divine love, the secret kisses, the secret embraces, the secret influences, the secret communion with God, that many a precious Christian has had in the most solitary places! It may be behind the door, or behind the wall, or behind the hedge, or behind the arbor, or behind the tree, or behind the rock, or behind the bush.

3. You Once Found Secret Corners Enough to Sin In

Thirdly and lastly, did you never, in your unregenerate state, make use of all your wits, and talents, and utmost efforts, to find fitting times, and secret corners, and solitary places to sin in, and to dishonor your God in, and to ruin your own and others’ souls in? Yes! I remember with shame and blushing that it was so with me when I was dead in trespasses and sins and walked according to the course of this world (Eph 2:1–3). Oh, how much then does it concern you, in your renewed, sanctified, and raised state, to make use of all your wits, and talents, and utmost efforts to find the fittest times, and the most secret corners, and the most solitary places you can, to honor your God in, and to seek the welfare of your own and others’ souls in! Oh, that men were only as serious, studious, and industrious to find fitting times and secret places to please and serve and glorify the Lord in as they have been serious, studious, and industrious to find fitting times and secret places to displease and grieve the Spirit of the Lord in!

Objection 6: Our Infirmities and Weaknesses Hold Us Back

Sixthly, and lastly, others may further object and say, “We would like to be often in private with God; we would like to give ourselves up to closet prayer. But we can no sooner shut our closet doors than a multitude of infirmities, weaknesses, and vanities face us and rise up against us. Our hearts are full of disorders and follies. Our bodies, some say, are greatly indisposed; and our souls, others say, are at present indisposed. How then can we seek the face of God in a corner? How can we wrestle with God in our closets?”

Now, to this objection I shall give these six answers.

1. By This Reasoning, Where Is the Man or Woman Who Could Pray?

If these kinds of reasonings or arguments were enough to shut private prayer out of doors, where is that man or woman, that husband or wife, that father or child, that master or servant, who would ever be found in the practice of that duty? Where is there a person under heaven whose heart is not full of infirmities, weaknesses, follies, and vanities, and whose body and soul are not too often indisposed to closet duties? “When they sin against You—for there is no one who does not sin—” (1 Kgs 8:46). “Surely there is no righteous man on earth who does good and never sins” (Eccl 7:20). “Who can say, ‘I have kept my heart pure; I am cleansed from my sin’?” (Prov 20:9). “Who can bring out clean from unclean? No one!” (Job 14:4). “If I should wash myself with snow and cleanse my hands with lye, then You would plunge me into the pit, and even my own clothes would despise me” (Job 9:30–31). “Even if I were righteous, my mouth would condemn me; if I were perfect, it would declare me guilty” (Job 9:20). “Do not bring Your servant into judgment, for no one alive is righteous before You” (Ps 143:2). “We all stumble in many ways” (Jas 3:2). “If we say we have no sin, we deceive ourselves, and the truth is not in us” (1 John 1:8).

Those who affirm that men may be fully perfect in this life, or without sin in this life, affirm what is expressly contrary to the Scriptures just cited, and to the universal experience of all saints, who daily feel and lament over that body of sin and death that they carry about with them. Indeed, they affirm what is quite contrary to the very state or constitution of all the saints in this life. In every saint “the flesh craves what is contrary to the Spirit, and the Spirit what is contrary to the flesh. They are opposed to each other, so that you+ cannot do what you+ want” (Gal 5:17). In every good man there are two men, the old man and the new. The new must be put on daily, and the old put off daily (Eph 4:22–24). All saints have a law in their members rebelling against the law of their minds, so that the good that they would do, they do not, and the evil that they would not do, that they do (Rom 7:23, 25). They have two contrary principles in them, from which come two kinds of actions, stirrings, and inclinations, continually opposed to one another. That is why there is a continual combat in them, like the struggling of the twins in Rebekah’s womb.

An absolute perfection belongs only to the triumphant state of God’s elect in heaven. Heaven is the only privileged place where no unclean thing can enter (Rev 21:27); it is the only place where neither sin nor Satan will ever get a footing. Those who dream of an absolute perfection in this life confuse and jumble heaven and earth together—the state of the church militant with the state of the church triumphant, which are certainly distinct both in time and place, and in order, measure, and accompaniments (Heb 12:22–23).

This dangerous opinion of absolute perfection in this life shakes the very foundation of religion and overthrows the gospel of grace. It renders the satisfaction of Christ and all His great transactions null and void. It tells the world that there is no need of faith, of repentance, of ordinances, of watchfulness. Those who say they have no sin say they have no need of the blood of Christ to cleanse them from sin (1 John 1:7). Those who say they have no sin say they have no need of faith to rest on Christ for imputed righteousness to justify their persons. Those who say they have no sin say they have no need of Christ as king to subdue their lusts, nor as a priest to atone for offenses, nor as prophet to teach and instruct them, nor as a Savior to save them from their sins or from the wrath to come (Matt 1:21; 1 Thess 1:10). Those who have a perfect righteousness of their own need not be indebted to Christ for His pure, perfect, spotless, matchless righteousness. Those who are without sin have no cause to repent of sin, nor yet to watch against sin. Those who are perfect cannot say, We are unprofitable servants. But are they indeed just? Then they must live by faith (Hab 2:4). Are they men, and not angels? Then they must repent. “He now commands all men everywhere to repent” (Acts 17:30). Surely the best of men are only men at best. Oh, how bad those men must be who make God Himself a liar (1 John 1:10). But if these men are absolutely perfect, how is it that they are afflicted and diseased like other men? How is it that they eat, and drink, and sleep, and buy, and sell, and die like other men? Are these things consistent with an absolute perfection? Surely not. An absolute perfection is not a step short of heaven; it is heaven on this side of heaven, and those who would obtain it must step into heaven before they have it.

2. This Objection Lies Against All Other Kinds of Prayer

Secondly, I answer that this objection lies as strongly against family prayer, and against every other kind of prayer, as it does against closet prayer. He who on any grounds makes this objection a great bugbear to scare his soul from closet prayer may on the same grounds make it a great bugbear to scare his soul not only from every other kind of prayer, but from all other duties of religion as well, whether private or public. The spirit of this objection fights against all religion at once. Therefore you+ should say to it, as Christ said to Peter, “Get behind Me, Satan!” (Matt 16:23).

3. Infirmities Seen, Lamented, and Resisted Cannot Obstruct Prayer

Thirdly, I answer: the infirmities and weaknesses of a Christian that are seen, lamented, bewailed, and resisted cannot obstruct or hinder the efficacy and success of his prayers. Let me make this clear in a few instances. Jonah, you+ know, was a man full of sinful passions and other weaknesses, and yet his prayer prevailed greatly with God (Jonah 2:1–2, 7, 10).

So Elijah’s prayers prevailed mightily with God. He could open and shut heaven at his pleasure, and yet he was “subject to the same passions as we are” (Jas 5:17). Elijah was a man of extraordinary sanctity and holiness, a man who lived in heaven while he dwelt on earth. Like Enoch, he walked with God, and yet he was “subject to the same passions as we are” (1 Kgs 19:8; Rom 11:2–3). God imparted His counsel and secrets to him in a remarkable way. He lay in the bosom of the Father, and yet he “was a man subject to the same passions as we are.” He was a very powerful and prevailing prophet. His very name means as much: Elijah signifies “my strong God.” In 1 Kings 17:1 it is Eli-jahu, that is, the Lord, He is my strong God; and yet he was “subject to the same passions as we are.” He was a man much given to fasting and prayer; he was a lesser mediator between God and His people; and yet he was “subject to the same passions as we are.”

Now some might object from this and say, “No wonder such a man as he was could open and shut heaven at his pleasure by his prayers. But I am a poor, weak, low, sinful, and unworthy creature. I am full of infirmities, weaknesses, and passions. Will my prayers ever find access to God and acceptance with God, or gracious answers and replies from God?” Now to answer this objection, and to remove this discouragement from the thoughts and hearts of poor sinners, the Holy Spirit adds this clause: that he was not a god, nor an angel, but a man, and such a man as was not exempt from common infirmities, for he had his passions, frailties, and weaknesses as well as other saints. This shows us that infirmities in the lowliest saints should no more harm the acceptance and success of their prayers with God than they did in Elijah himself.

The word passion sometimes signifies, first, a stirring of the sensory appetite, arising from imagining good or ill, with some commotion of the body. Secondly, passions sometimes signify sinful infirmities, sinful disturbances of the mind. And thirdly, passion is sometimes used more strictly for the particular emotion of sinful anger and wrath, which Chrysostom calls brevis daemon, a short devil. It makes a man speak without knowing what he says, as you+ may see in Jonah, and act without knowing what he does, as you+ may see in Saul. Now in these last two senses, Elijah was a man subject to the same passions as we are, and yet a man so mighty with God that by private prayer he could do even what he pleased in the court of heaven.

In 1 Samuel 21 you+ may read of David’s outright lies, and of his other failings, infirmities, and unseemly behavior before Achish, king of Gath, for which he was turned out of the king’s presence as a madman. And yet at that very time he prays and prevails with God for favor, mercy, and deliverance. “I sought the Lord, and He answered me; He delivered me from all my fears” (Ps 34:4). But when was this? Read the title of the psalm, and you+ will find it: “Of David, when he pretended to be insane before Abimelech, who drove him away, and he departed.”

In Numbers 20:10–12, Moses’ infirmities are pointed out. First, you have there his immoderate anger. (2) His speaking to the people when he should have spoken to the rock (v. 8). (3) His striking it when he should only have spoken to it with the rod in his hand, and striking it twice, as in a fit of passion and impatience. (4) His distrust of the Lord’s word (v. 12). (5) His reviling the people when he should have reasoned with them and shown them their fault: “Listen now, you+ rebels” (v. 10). (6) He seems so offended at his commission that he can hardly keep from grumbling: “Must we bring…water out of this rock?” (v. 10). Notice that word, “must we.” Oh, how the meekest man in all the world (Num 12:3) is carried away by passion, and anger, and unbelief, and hurried into sad improprieties! And yet there was not a man on earth whose prayers were so powerful and prevailing with God as Moses’ were (Ps 106:23; Exod 32:9–15; 33:11–17; 14:13–16).

So King Asa was a man full of infirmities and weaknesses. He relies on the king of Syria and not on the Lord (2 Chr 16:7–13). He is very impatient and in a great rage at the seer’s rebuke. He imprisons the seer. He oppressed some of the people at the same time; or, as the Hebrew has it, “he crushed,” or trampled on, some of the people. And being greatly diseased in his feet, he sought the physicians and not the Lord. And yet this man’s prayer prevailed wonderfully with God (2 Chr 14:11–15).

The saints’ infirmities can never nullify those gracious promises by which God stands bound to listen to the prayers of His people (Ps 50:15; Isa 30:19; 65:24). Whether God hears our prayers does not depend on sanctification, but on Christ’s intercession; not on what we are in ourselves, but on what we are in the Lord Jesus. Both our persons and our prayers are acceptable in the beloved (Eph 1:6; 1 Pet 2:5). When God hears our prayers, it is not for our own sakes, nor for our prayers’ sake, but for His own sake, and His Son’s sake, and His glory’s sake, and His promise’s sake.

Certainly, God will never cast off His people for their infirmities.

First, it is the glory of a man to pass by infirmities (Prov 19:11). Oh, how much more, then, must it be the glory of God to pass by the infirmities of His people!

Secondly, saints are children; and what father will cast off his children for their infirmities and weaknesses (Ps 103:13–14)?

Thirdly, saints are members of Christ’s body (1 Cor 12:27); and what man will cut off a member because of a scab or wart on it? “What man will cut off his nose,” says Luther, “because there is some filth in it?”

Fourthly, saints are Christ’s purchase; they are His possession, His inheritance. Now what man is there who will cast away or cast off his purchase, his possession, his inheritance, because of thorns, bushes, or briars that grow on it?

Fifthly, saints are in a marriage covenant with God (Hos 2:19–20). Now what husband is there who will cast off his wife for her failings and infirmities? So long as a man is in covenant with God, his infirmities cannot cut him off from God’s mercy and grace. Now it is certain that a man may have very many infirmities on him, and yet not break his covenant with God; for no sin breaks a man’s covenant with God except one that unties the marriage knot. In other marriages, not every offense or infirmity annuls the marriage union; only the breach of the marriage vow, adultery, unties the marriage knot. So here, only those sins that break the covenant untie the marriage knot between God and the soul: (1) When men freely submit to any lust as a new master; or (2) When men take another husband. Men do this when they enter into a league with sin or the world, when they make a new covenant with hell and death (Isa 28:15, 18). Now God guards His chosen ones from these harms. In a word, if God cast off His people for their infirmities, then none of the sons or daughters of Adam could be saved. “Surely there is no righteous man on earth who does good and never sins” (Eccl 7:20). Now if God will not cast off His people for their infirmities, then certainly He will not cast off the prayers of His people because of those invincible infirmities that hang on them. Therefore our infirmities should not discourage us, or keep us from closet prayer, or from any other duties of religion.

4. The More Infirmities Hang upon Us, the More Cause to Keep Close

Fourthly, I answer, The more infirmities and weaknesses hang on us, the more cause we have to keep close and constant to our closet duties. If grace is weak, the omission of private prayer will make it weaker. Just as he who will not eat will certainly grow weaker and weaker, so he who will not pray in his closet will certainly grow weaker and weaker. If corruptions are strong, the neglect of private prayer will make them stronger. The more the remedy is neglected, the more the disease is strengthened. Whatever the disorders of a man’s heart may be, they will never be lessened, but only increased, by the omission of private prayer. The more bodily infirmities hang on us, the more need we have of the physician. And so, the more sinful infirmities hang on our souls, the more need we have of private prayer. All sinful omissions will make work for repentance, for hell, or for the physician of souls. Sinful omissions lead to sinful commissions, as you+ may see in the angels who fell from heaven to hell, and in Adam’s fall in paradise.

Origen, going to comfort and encourage a martyr who was to be tortured, was himself arrested by the officers and forced either to offer to the idols or to have his body abused by a blackamoor who was ready for that purpose. Given that hard choice, to save his life he bowed to the idol. But afterward, making a sad confession of his foul deed, he said that he had gone out that morning before he had been with God in his closet; and so he firmly concluded that his neglect of prayer was the cause of his falling into that great sin.

The neglect of one day, of one duty, of one hour, would undo us forever if we did not have “an advocate with the Father” (1 John 2:1–2). Those years, those months, those weeks, those days, those hours that are not filled up with God, with Christ, with grace, with duty, will certainly be filled up with vanity and folly. All omissions of duty will make the soul less and less fit for duty. A key thrown aside gathers rust; a pump not used is hard to get going; and armor not used is hard to make bright. Just as sinful commissions will stab the soul, so sinful omissions will starve the soul. Those who live in the neglect of private prayer may well cry out, “Our leanness, our leanness!” (Isa 24:16; Job 16:8). Therefore away with all these pleas and reasonings about infirmities, and weaknesses, and indispositions, and give yourselves+ to closet prayer.

5. Your Illness Does Not Hinder Your Buying, Selling, and Getting Gain

Fifthly, I answer, It may be that your illness and indisposition of body is not so great that you cannot buy, sell, and get gain. In spite of your aching head, and the shooting pains in your back, and your painful sides, and your feeble knees, you can still, like Martha, burden yourself with your worldly affairs.

In Song of Solomon 5:2, Christ calls on His spouse to open the door and let Him in. But sin and excuse-making came into the world together; and see how poorly and unworthily she tries to put Christ off: “I have taken off my robe—must I put it back on? I have washed my feet—must I soil them again?” (v. 3). Rather than make no excuse for herself, she will make a foolish excuse, a worthless excuse. She was no longer a child, and what great trouble would it have been for her to rise and let in such a guest, who brings with Him everything the heart can wish or need can require (Rev 3:17–18)? She had not grown so frail with old age that she was unable to get herself ready. At the least, she might easily have slipped on her morning robe and stepped to the door without any danger of catching cold or of being soaked to the skin, and so have let in Him who never comes empty-handed (Rev 22:12)—indeed, Who had now come full of the dew of divine blessings to enrich her, for so some interpret those words, “My head is drenched with dew, my hair with the dampness of the night” (Song 5:2). Oh, the frivolous pretenses and idle excuses that even gracious persons are apt at times to take up to gloss over their neglect of duty!

But some may say, It may be that the spouse of Christ was asleep. Oh, no! For she says, “I sleep, but my heart is awake” (v. 2). She slept with open eyes, as the lion does. She was only half asleep. Though her outward man was drowsy, her inward man was wakeful. Though the flesh took a nap, her spirit did not nod.

Oh, but it may be that Christ made no noise. He gave no notice that He was at the door. Oh, yes! He knocked; He knocked and pounded with the hammer of His Word and the hand of His Spirit. He knocked by outward chastisements and inward admonitions. He knocked by providences, and He knocked by blessings. His importunity and urgency to be let in were very great.

Oh, but it may be that He only knocked. He should have called as well as knocked; for only madmen would open their doors in the night unless they knew the voice of Him who knocks. Oh, yes! He not only knocked but also called.

Oh, but it may be that she did not know His voice, and therefore she would not open. No chaste wife will rise at unseasonable hours and open her doors to a stranger, especially in her husband’s absence. Oh, yes, she knew His voice: “The voice of my beloved! He is knocking” (v. 2). She was not so fast asleep that she could not tell the voice of her beloved from all other voices and make out every word that He said. The calls of Christ were so strong, so loud, and His knocks so mighty, that she could not help but know and confess that it was the voice of her beloved, though she was not respectful and dutiful enough to obey that voice.

Oh, but it may be that Christ knocked and called like a friend on his journey, only to ask how it was with her, or to speak to her at the window. Oh, no! He speaks plainly; He speaks with authority: “Open to me” (v. 2).

Oh, but it may be that she had no power to open the door. Oh, yes, she had; for when He commands His people to open, He lends them a key to open the door so that He may enter (Phil 1:6; 2:13; 1 Cor 15:10). Infused grace is a living principle that will enable the soul to open to Christ. If a man is not a free agent, able to work and act by the help of the grace he has received, why are counsels, commands, exhortations, and directions given to perform this, and that, and the other work? And certainly it is our greatest honor and happiness in this world to cooperate with God in those things that concern His own glory and our own internal and eternal good.

Oh, but it may be that Christ had given His spouse some offense, or had let fall some hard words or unkind speeches, which made her a little contrary and peevish. Oh, no! For He acknowledges her as His beloved, and courts her highly with the most winning and friendly terms of love: “My sister, my darling, my dove, my undefiled,” or “my flawless one.” He calls her so for her dovelike simplicity, purity, and integrity. All these endearing and honoring titles are the rhetoric of divine love, and should have been so many sacred bonds on her to open to her beloved.

Oh, but it may be that Christ was too quick for her; it may be that He gave only a knock and a call and was gone before she could rise and open the door. Oh, no! Christ stayed until His head was drenched with dew, and His hair with the dampness of the night. This most passionate expression shows the tender goodness, patience, and gentleness of our Lord Jesus, Who endures far greater and harder things for His spouse’s sake than Jacob ever did for his Rachel’s sake. After Christ had suffered much for her sake and waited a long while for her to be ready, she very unkindly, very rudely, and unworthily turns her back on all His sweet and comforting names and His blessed and bleeding embraces, and sends Him away to look [for] His lodging in some other place. So He might well have said, Is this your kindness to your friend, your husband, your Lord, to let Him stand bareheaded, and that in foul weather, indeed in the nighttime, wooing, entreating, and begging to be let in, and yet to send Him away as one in Whom your soul could take no pleasure?

Now, if you+ will only seriously weigh all these circumstances in the balance of the sanctuary, you+ may read at a glance the fault and folly, the weakness and madness, the carelessness and laziness of the spouse. And by her you+ may judge of those sad and sinful disorders that may seize on the best of saints, and see how ready the flesh is to frame excuses, and all to keep the soul from duty and the doors bolted fast against the Lord Jesus.

It is sad when men are well enough to sit, chat, and trade in their shops, but not well enough to pray in their closets. Certainly, if a man can maintain and keep up his public trade with men under all his bodily ailments, but is not well enough to maintain his private trade with heaven, that man’s heart is not right with God, at least at this time.

Our bodies are only dirt, handsomely kneaded and skillfully formed; we derive our pedigree from the dirt and are akin to clay. One calls the body “the blot of nature”; another calls it “the soul’s beast,” “a sack of dung,” “worms’ food”; another calls it “a prison,” “a tomb”; and Paul calls it “a body of vileness.” Now for a man to make so much fuss about the ailments of his body to excuse the neglect of his soul is an evil made up of many evils. But really, sir, I am so ill, and my body is so ailing and indisposed, that I am not able to attend to or deal with the least things of the world! Well! If this is so, then know that God has knocked you off from the things of this world on purpose, so that you may look more effectually after the things of another world. God’s design in all the ailments that are on your body is to wind you further off from your worldly trade, and to bring you to follow your heavenly trade more closely. Many a man would never have found the way to his closet if God had not turned him out of his shop, his trade, his business, his all, etc., by bodily ailments.

Well, Christians, remember this once for all: if your+ indisposition to closet prayer really arises from bodily ailments, then you+ may be confident that the Lord will pity you+ much, and bear with you+ much, and kindly accept a little. You+ know how affectionately parents and noble-minded masters behave toward their children and servants when they are under bodily ailments and indisposition; and you+ may be confident that God will never deal worse with you+ than they do with them. Ponder Ezek 34:4, 16, 21–22 often.

6. A Distinction: Contracted and Involuntary Indisposition

Sixthly, and lastly, I shall answer this objection by way of distinction, as follows:

First, there is a contracted indisposition to private prayer, and there is an involuntary indisposition to private prayer. There is a contracted indisposition, and that is when a man, by his willful sinning against light, knowledge, conviction, etc., contracts guilt that lies as a load on his conscience. Now guilt makes the soul shy of God; and the greater the guilt is, the more shy the soul is of drawing near to God in a corner. The child who feels himself under guilt hides himself from his father’s eye in the day, as Adam did, and at night slips off to bed to avoid either a scolding or a whipping from his father (Gen 3:7–8). Guilt makes a man flee from God and flee from prayer. It is a hard thing to look God in the face when guilt stares a man in the face (Job 11:14–15). Guilt makes a man a terror to himself (Jer 20:3–4). Now when a man is a terror to himself, he is neither fit to live, nor fit to die, nor fit to pray. When poison gets into the body, it works on the spirits; it weakens the spirits, endangers life, and unfits and indisposes a man for all natural actions. So it is here. When guilt lies heavy on the conscience, it works on the soul, it weakens the soul, it endangers the soul, and it greatly unfits and indisposes the soul for all holy actions. Guilt fights against our souls, our consciences, our comforts, our duties, and indeed our very graces also (1 Pet 2:11).

There is nothing that wounds and lames our graces like guilt. There is nothing that weakens and wastes our graces like guilt. There is nothing that hinders the activity of our graces like guilt; nor is there anything that clouds our evidences of grace like guilt. What water is to fire, our sins are to our graces, evidences, and duties. Guilt is like Prometheus’ vulture, which always lies gnawing. It is better, with Evagrius, to lie on a bed of straw with a good conscience than to lie on a bed of down with a guilty conscience. What the would-be disciple said to our Savior—“Teacher, I will follow You wherever You go” (Matt 8:19)—a guilty conscience says to the sinner: “Wherever you go, I will follow you. If you go to a fast, I will follow you, and fill your mind with black and dismal thoughts of God. If you go to a feast, I will follow you, and show you the handwriting on the wall (Dan 5:5). If you go away from home, I will follow you, and make you afraid of every leaf that stirs. You will look on every bush as an armed man, and on every man as a devil. If you stay at home, I will follow you from room to room, and fill you with horror and terror. If you lie down to rest, I will follow you with fearful dreams and tormenting apparitions. If you go into your closet, I will follow you, and make your very closet a hell to hold you.”

It is told of King Richard the Third that after he had murdered his two nephews in the Tower, guilt lay so hard on his conscience that his sleep was very unquiet. He would often leap out of his bed in the dark, catch up in his hand his sword, which hung by his bedside, and go distractedly about his room seeking the traitor.

So Charles the Ninth of France, after he had made the streets of Paris run with the blood of the Protestants, could seldom get any sound sleep, nor could he bear to be awakened out of his sleep without music.

Judge Morgan, who passed the sentence of condemnation on Jane Grey, a virtuous lady, shortly afterward went mad, and in his raving cried out continually, “Take away the Lady Jane from me, take away the Lady Jane from me,” and in that horror ended his wretched life.

James Abyes, going to execution for Christ’s sake, gave his money and his clothes to one and another as he went along, until he had given everything away down to his shirt. At this one of the sheriff’s men began to scoff at him and deride him, and told him that he was a madman and a heretic and not to be believed. But as soon as the good man was executed, this wretch was struck mad, threw away his clothes, and cried out that James Abyes was a good man and gone to heaven, but that he himself was a wicked man and was damned; and he went on crying out like this until his death. Certainly, if a man derides or strikes another for walking according to the Word of the Lord, the Lord will, sooner or later, so strike and wound his own conscience that all the physicians in the world will not heal it.

Now if your indisposition to private prayer springs from guilt contracted on your conscience, then your best way is to renew your repentance speedily, to judge and humble your own soul greatly, and so to exercise faith afresh on the blood of Christ, both for pardoning mercy and for purging grace. When a man is stung with guilt, it is his highest wisdom in the world to look up to the bronze serpent, and not to spend his time or create torments for his own soul by endlessly poring over his guilt. When guilt on the conscience brings a man to water the earth with tears and to make heaven ring with his groans, then it works as it should. When the sense of guilt drives a man to God, to duty, to the throne of grace, then the night will not last long for that man. He who thinks to evade private prayer under the pretense of guilt only increases his own guilt by it. Neglect of duty will never get guilt off the conscience.

But then there is an involuntary indisposition to private prayer, as in a sick man who wants to work and walk but cannot, because his disease hinders him; or as with a man who has a great chain on his leg: he would very much like to walk or get away, but his chain hinders him. Now if your+ indisposition to private prayer is an involuntary indisposition, then God will in mercy, in due course, both pardon it and remove it.

Secondly, there is a total indisposition to private prayer, and there is a partial indisposition to private prayer. A total indisposition to private prayer is when a man has no mind at all for private prayer, nor any will at all for private prayer, nor any love at all for private prayer, nor any delight, nor any heart at all for private prayer (Jer 4:22; 44:17–19). Now where this frame of heart is, there all is wicked, very wicked, utterly wicked. A partial indisposition to private prayer is when a man has some will for private prayer, though not such a will as he once had; and some mind for private prayer, though not such a mind as he once had; and some affection for private prayer, though not such warm and burning affections as he once had. Now if your+ indisposition to private prayer is total, then you+ must wait on the Lord in all His appointed means for a changed nature and for union with Christ. But if your+ indisposition to private prayer is only partial, then the Lord will certainly pardon it, and in time remove it in the very use of holy means.

Thirdly and lastly, there is a transient, accidental, occasional, or fleeting indisposition to private prayer; and there is a customary, a constant, or permanent indisposition to private prayer.

Now a transient, accidental, occasional, or fleeting indisposition to what is good may be found on the best of saints, as you+ may see in Moses (Exod 4:10–14), and in Jeremiah (Jer 1:5–8, 17–19; 20:9), and in Jonah (Jon 1), and in David (Ps 39:2–3). Now if this is the indisposition you are under, then you may be confident that it will certainly wear off by degrees, as theirs did whom I have just cited.

But then there is a customary, a constant, or permanent indisposition to private prayer, and to all other holy duties of religion (Isa 65:2). Now if this is the indisposition you are under, then I may safely conclude that you are truly “poisoned by bitterness and captive to iniquity” (Acts 8:21–23). And your work lies not in complaining of your indisposition but in repenting and believing, and in laboring for a change of your heart and state; for until your heart and your state are changed, you will remain forever indisposed both to closet prayer and to all other duties of religion and godliness. To see a sinner sailing hellward with wind and tide on his side alter his course and tack about for heaven; to see the earthly man become heavenly, the carnal man become spiritual, the proud man become humble, the frivolous man become serious; to see a sinner move contrary to himself in the ways of Christ and holiness, is as strange as to see the earth fly upward, or the bowl run against its own bias. And yet a divine power of God on the soul can bring it about; and this must be brought about before the sinner will be graciously inclined and sincerely disposed to closet prayer. And let this much suffice by way of answer to this objection also.

3. Advice and Counsel in Eleven Particulars

Now, for the better management of this great duty, closet prayer, I beg you+ to take my advice and counsel in these eleven particulars that follow:

a. Be Frequent in Closet Prayer, and Not Now and Then Only

First, be frequent in closet prayer, and not now and then only. He who is not frequent in closet prayer will never make any earnings from closet prayer. Now, so that this counsel may stick, consider:

[1] First, other eminent servants of the Lord have been frequent in this blessed work. “Let Your eyes be open and Your ears attentive to hear the prayer that I, Your servant, now pray before You day and night” (Neh 1:6). So Daniel: “three times a day he got down on his knees, prayed, and gave thanks to his God, just as he had done before” (Dan 6:10). So David: “In the morning, O Lord, You hear my voice; in the morning I will direct my prayer to You and will look up” (Ps 5:3). “But to You, O Lord, I cry for help; in the morning my prayer comes before You” (Ps 88:13). “I rise before dawn and cry for help” (Ps 119:147). “Evening, morning, and noon I pray and cry aloud” (Ps 55:17). Indeed, he was vir orationis, “a man of prayer,” for his frequency in it. “In return for my love they accuse me, but I give myself to prayer” (Ps 109:4), or, as the Hebrew may be read, “But I am a man of prayer.”

It was said of Carolus Magnus, Carolus plus cum Deo quam hominibus loquitur, that he spoke more with God than with men.

[2] Secondly, consider that the blessed Scripture not only commands this duty but requires frequency in it as well (Luke 18:1; 1 Thess 5:17; Col 4:2). In the former part of this discourse, I have shed light on these scriptures, and therefore the bare citing of them must now suffice.

[3] Thirdly, Christ was frequent in private prayer, as you+ may easily see by comparing these scriptures together (Mark 1:35; Matt 14:23; Luke 22:39; John 18:2). In my second argument for private prayer, you+ may see these scriptures explained and expanded.

[4] Fourthly, consider that you+ have the examples of the very worst of men in this case. Papists are frequent in their private devotions. And the Muslims, whatever occasion they have to divert them, either by profit or pleasure, will still pray five times every day. Indeed, the very heathens sacrificed to Hercules morning and evening on the great altar at Rome. Now, shall blind nature do more than grace?

[5] Fifthly, consider that you+ cannot have too frequent communion with God. You+ cannot have too frequent fellowship with Jesus. You+ cannot have your+ hearts too frequently filled with joy unspeakable and full of glory, and with that peace that passes understanding. You+ cannot have heaven too frequently brought down into your+ hearts, nor can you+ have your+ hearts too frequently carried up to heaven. And therefore you+ cannot be too frequent in closet prayer.

[6] Sixthly, consider that you+ are under frequent needs, and frequent sins, and frequent snares, and frequent temptations, and frequent allurements, and frequent trials, and frequent cares, and frequent fears, and frequent favors (1 Pet 5:8; Job 1:7). And therefore you+ need to be frequent with God in your+ closets.

[7] Seventhly, consider that you+ are the favorites of heaven. You+ are greatly beloved, you+ are highly honored, you+ are exceedingly esteemed and valued in the court of the Most High. And remember that the petitions of many weak Christians, and of many benighted Christians, and of many tempted Christians, and of many clouded Christians, and of many staggering Christians, and of many doubting Christians, and of many bewildered Christians, and of many fainting Christians, etc., are put into your+ hands for a quick and speedy dispatch to the throne of grace. So you+ need to be frequent in your+ closets and make use of your+ influence in heaven, or else many of these poor hearts may be wronged, betrayed, and harmed by your+ neglect. Those who are favorites in princes’ courts, if they are active, diligent, careful, and watchful, may do much good for others. They may come into their prince’s presence as often as they please, and, like Queen Esther, have whatever they please for the asking, both for themselves and others (Est 7). Oh, what a world of good may those who are God’s favorites do for others, if they would only be frequent with God in their closets!

O sirs! If you+ do not have that love, that regard, that pity, that compassion for your+ own souls that you+ should have, yet, oh, do not let others suffer by your+ neglect of private prayer! Oh, do not let Zion suffer! Oh, do not let any particular saint suffer by your+ being seldom found in your+ closets.

Certainly, it might have gone better with the churches of Christ, and with the concerns of Christ, and with many of the poor people of Christ, if most Christians had been more frequent with God in their closets.

[8] Eighthly and lastly, consider that this liberty to draw near to God in your+ closets cost Christ His dearest blood (Eph 2:13; Heb 10:20). Now, he who is not frequent with God in his closet tells everyone around him that he sets no great value on the liberty that Christ has purchased with His blood. The incomparable, the unparalleled price that Christ paid down on the spot more than sixteen hundred years ago, so that we might have liberty and free access to His Father in our closets, shows very strongly, indeed irrefutably, the superlative excellence of that liberty (1 Pet 1:19). Oh, therefore let us make effectual use of this blessed purchase of our Lord Jesus by being frequent with God in our closets.

Some dispute whether one drop of Christ’s blood was sufficient for the pardon of our sins and the redemption of our souls. My intention is not to dispute but to offer a few things for your+ consideration:

First, it must be granted that, because of the hypostatic union, a drop of Christ’s blood was of inestimable worth and excellence; and the value of His passion is to be measured by the dignity of His person.

Secondly, a proportion was to be observed between the punishment due to men and what was suffered for man. For His sufferings to make satisfaction, two things were necessary: poenae gravitas, the gravity of the punishment, as well as personae dignitas, the dignity of the person.

That the least drop of Christ’s blood was not sufficient for the redemption of our souls may be shown in this way:

First, if it were, then the circumcision of Christ was enough, for there a drop of blood was shed, if not many drops.

Secondly, then His being crowned with a crown of thorns was sufficient, for it is most probable that they drew blood from Him.

Thirdly, then all Christ’s other sufferings were superfluous and vain.

Fourthly, then God was unjust and unrighteous to take more than was due to His justice. But for any man to affirm that God has taken more than His just due is high blasphemy.

Fifthly, then Christ was weak and imprudent to pay more than He needed, for what need was there of His dearest heart’s blood if a drop from His hand would have saved our souls? Let the schoolmen imagine what they please, it is certain that not one dram of that bitter cup that Christ drank down could be abated, if His Father was to be fully satisfied and man eternally redeemed. Christ has declared under His own hand that it was necessary for Him to suffer many things (Luke 24:26; 17:25). O sirs! Shall Christ shed not only a few drops of blood, but His very heart’s blood, to purchase for you+ a freedom and liberty to be in your+ closets with His Father as often as you+ please, and will you+ give God a visit in private only now and then? The Lord forbid.

b. Take the Fittest Seasons and Opportunities for Closet Prayer

My second advice and counsel is this: take the fittest seasons and opportunities that you+ possibly can for closet prayer. Many take unfit seasons for private prayer, which obstruct the importunity of the soul in prayer more than all the suggestions and instigations of Satan. For example:

First, when the body is drowsy and sleepy, this is a very unfit season for closet prayer (Song 3:1). Beware of laying cushions of sloth under your+ knees, or pillows of idleness under your+ elbows, or of mixing nods with your+ petitions, or of being drowsily devout when you+ draw near to God in your+ closets.

Secondly, when a man’s head and heart are filled with worldly cares and distractions, this is a very unfit season for closet prayer (1 Cor 7:35; Ezek 33:31).

When Dinah must go gadding about to see the fashions, Shechem, the prince of that country, meets with her and violates her. So when our hearts, like Dinah, must go roving and gadding about after the things of the world, then Satan, the prince of the air, usually seizes on us and commits a rape upon our souls. He either leads us away from prayer, or else he so distracts us from prayer that it would be better not to have prayed at all than to have offered the sacrifice of foolish and distracted prayer.

I have read a story of how one man offered to give his horse to his companion on condition that he would only say the Lord’s Prayer and think of nothing but God. The offer was accepted, and he began, “‘Our Father in heaven, hallowed be Your name’ (Matt 6:9)…But I must have the bridle too,” he said. “No, nor the horse either,” said the other, “for you have lost both already.” The application is easy.

Certainly the freest and liveliest season for closet prayer is the morning, before a man’s spirit is blunted or cooled, deadened, damped, or dulled by worldly business. A man should speak with God in his closet before he speaks with his worldly affairs and dealings. A man should say to all his worldly business what Abraham said to his young men when he went to offer up his only Isaac: “Stay here…I…will go over there to worship, and then…return to you+” (Gen 22:5). He who would attend to closet prayer without distraction or disturbance must not go out into the world first and only then slip from it into his closet, but he must first slip into his closet before he is surrounded by a crowd of worldly employments.

It was a precept of Pythagoras that when we enter the temple to worship God, we must not so much as speak or think of any worldly business, so that we do not make God’s service an idle, perfunctory, and lazy recreation. I may say the same of closet prayer.

Jerome complains very much of his distractions, dullness, and indisposition to prayer, and chides himself in this way: “What! Do you think that Jonah prayed like this when he was in the whale’s belly, or Daniel when he was among the lions, or the thief when he was on the cross?”

Thirdly, when men or women are in rash and angry disorders (1 Tim 2:8), this is a very unfit season for closet prayer, for when passions are up, holy affections are down. For prayers that do not first warm our own hearts will never reach God’s ear. In the Muscovite churches, if the minister makes a mistake in reading, or stammers in pronouncing his words, or speaks any word that is not heard well, the hearers blame him very much and are ready to take the book from him, as unworthy to read from it. And certainly God is no less offended with the giddy, rash, heated, precipitate, and inconsiderate prayers of those who, without deliberate understanding, send their petitions to heaven in great haste. Solomon’s advice is worthy of all praise and acceptance: “Do not be rash with your mouth, and do not be hasty in your heart to utter a word before God” (Eccl 5:2). Or, as the Hebrew may be read, “Do not let your heart, through haste, be so troubled or disturbed as to tumble over and throw out words without wisdom or forethought.” Good men are often apt to be too hasty, rash, and unadvised in their prayers, complaints, and pleas against evil. Witness David, Job, Jeremiah, Jonah, and the disciples.

There is no Christian like the one who wisely and seriously weighs his prayers and praises before he pours out his soul before the Lord. He who first duly deliberates what to request never repents of his requests. But he who blurts out whatever lies uppermost, and who brings into the presence of God his rash, raw, tumultuous, and undigested petitions, confessions, complaints, etc., only provokes God. He only brawls with God, instead of praying to Him or wrestling with Him. Suitors at court watch for their fittest times and seasons for begging. They commonly take the very nick of time when they have the king in a good mood, and so seldom or never come away without good success.

Sometimes God strongly inclines the heart to closet prayer. Sometimes He brings the heart beforehand into a praying frame; sometimes both body and soul are more enlivened, revived, raised, and divinely inflamed than at other times. Sometimes conscience is more stirring, working, and tender. Oh, now strike while the iron is hot! Oh, now lay hold on all such blessed opportunities by applying yourself to private prayer. O sirs! Can you+ take your+ fittest times, seasons, and opportunities for plowing, and sowing, and reaping, and buying and selling, and eating, and drinking, and marrying, and can you+ not just as well take your+ fittest times and seasons to seek the Lord in your+ closets? Must the best God be put off with the least and worst of your+ time? The Lord forbid. Do not neglect the seasons of grace; do not let your+ opportunities for closet prayer slip. Thousands have lost their seasons and their souls together.

c. Do Not Perform Closet Duties Merely to Still Your Consciences

My third advice and counsel is this: be exceedingly careful that you+ do not perform closet duties merely to still your+ consciences. You+ must perform them out of conscience, but you+ must not perform them only to quiet conscience. Some have such a light set up in their understandings that they cannot omit closet prayer without conscience being on their backs, always upbraiding and disquieting them. And therefore they are afraid to neglect closet prayer, for fear that conscience will question, arraign, and condemn them for their neglect. Sometimes, when men have greatly sinned against the Lord, conscience becomes impatient and is always accusing, condemning, and terrifying them. And now, in these agonies, they will run to their closets, and cry, and pray, and mourn, and confess, and bitterly bewail their transgressions. But all this is only to quiet their consciences. And sometimes they find, on performing closet duties, that their consciences are a little calmed and quieted; and for this very end and purpose they take up closet prayer as a charm to calm their consciences. And when the storm is over and their consciences are quieted, they lay closet prayer aside—as the monk did the net when the fish was caught—and are ready to transgress again. O sirs! Beware of this, for this is nothing but plain hypocrisy, and it will be bitterness in the end. He who performs closet prayer only to bribe his conscience so that it will not be clamorous, or to stop the mouth of conscience so that it will not accuse him for sin, will in the end venture on such a trade, such a course of sinning against conscience, as will certainly turn his troubled conscience into a seared conscience (1 Tim 4:2). And a seared conscience is like a sleeping lion: when he awakes, he roars and tears his prey in pieces. So a seared conscience, when it is awakened, will roar and tear the complacent sinner in pieces.

When Dionysius’s conscience was awakened, he was so troubled with fear and horror of conscience that, not daring to trust his best friends with a razor, he used to singe his beard with burning coals, as Cicero reports. All the mercy that a seared, a benumbed, conscience affords the sinner, when it most befriends him, when it seems to deal most kindly with him, is this: it will not cut, so that it may kill; it will not convict, so that it may confound; it will not accuse, so that it may condemn. It will spare the sinner a while so that it may torment him forever; it will spare him here so that it may gnaw him hereafter. It will not strike until it is too late for the sinner to ward off the blow. Oh, cruel mercy, to see the sin and leave the sinner alone until the gates of mercy are shut on him and hell stands gaping to devour him! “Sin lies at the door” (Gen 4:7). The Hebrew word robets signifies to lie down, or couch, like some wild beast at the mouth of its cave, as if it were asleep, but in fact it watches and wakes, and is ready to fly at all who come near it. O sirs! Sin is couchant (lying down) rather than dormant (sleeping). It sleeps a dog’s sleep so that it may take the sinner at a greater advantage and fly the more furiously in his face.

d. Beware of Resting and Trusting upon Closet Duties

My fourth advice and counsel is this: beware of resting on closet duties; beware of trusting in closet duties. Noah’s dove made use of her wings, but she did not trust in her wings; she trusted in the ark. So you+ must make use of closet duties, but you+ must not trust in your+ closet duties but in Jesus, of Whom the ark was only a type.

There are many who go a round of duties as mill horses go their round in a mill, and rest on them when they have finished, using the means as mediators, and so fall short of Christ and heaven at once. Closet duties rested in will undo a man as eternally as the greatest and foulest enormities do. Open wickedness slays her thousands, but a secret resting on duties slays her ten thousands. Multitudes bleed inwardly from this disease and die forever. Open profaneness is the broad, dirty way that leads to hell, but closet duties rested in are a sure way to hell, though a cleaner way. Profane persons and professing Christians of mere outward form will meet at last in one hell. Ah, Christians! Do not make closet duties your+ money, or you+ and your+ money will perish together.

The phoenix gathers sweet, fragrant sticks together in Arabia, and then fans them with her wings and burns herself with them. So many shining professing Christians burn themselves by resting in their duties and services. You+ know that in Noah’s Flood all who were not in the ark, though they climbed up the tallest trees and the highest mountains and hills, were really drowned all the same. So let men climb up to this duty and that; yet if they do not get into Christ, they will really be damned. It is not your closet, but your Christ, that must save you. If a man has no share in Christ, he may perish with “Our Father” in his mouth. It is as natural for a man to rest in his duties as it is for him to rest in his bed.

This was Bernard’s temptation: when he had been helped a little in duty, he could stroke his own head with bene fecisti Bernard, “O Bernard, this was gallantly done; now cheer yourself up.” Ah, how apt man is, when he has been a little helped, heated, melted, set free in a way of duty, to go away and stroke himself, bless himself, hug himself, and warm himself with the sparks, with the fire of his own kindling (Isa 50:11)!

Adam was to win life and wear it; he was to be saved by his doings (Gen 2:16–17). Do this and live. That is why all his descendants are so prone to seek salvation by doing (Acts 2:37). “What must I do to be saved?” (Acts 16:30). “Good Teacher, what must I do to inherit eternal life?” (Mark 10:17–20). Like father, like son. But if our own duties or doings were sufficient to save us, to what purpose did Christ leave His Father’s bosom and lay down His dearest life?

Closet duties rested in may pacify conscience for a time, but this will not always hold. “When Ephraim saw his sickness and Judah his wound, then Ephraim turned to Assyria and sent to King Jareb. But he cannot cure you+ or heal your+ wound” (Hos 5:13).

If we rest on closet duties, or on anything else this side of Christ, we shall find them as weak as Assyria, or as Jareb. We shall find to our cost that they cannot help us or heal us; they cannot comfort us or cure us of our wounds. Just as created things were never true to anyone who trusted in them, neither were duties. When the Israelites were in great distress, the Lord tells them, “Go and cry out to the gods you+ have chosen. Let them save you+,” says God, “in your+ time of trouble” (Judg 10:14). O sirs! If, when you+ are in distress of conscience, or lying on a deathbed, God should say to you+, Go to your+ closet prayers and performances that you+ have made and rested in; go to your+ closet tears that you+ have shed and rested in, and let them save you+ and deliver you+—oh, what miserable saviors and comforters they would be to you+! What the ark of God was to the Philistines (1 Sam 5), closet duties are to Satan. He trembles every time he sees a poor sinner go into his closet and come out of his closet resting and glorying in Jesus, and not in his duties. But when he sees a poor creature confide in his closet duties and rest on his closet duties, then he rejoices; then he claps his hands and sings, “Aha! That is how I would have it.”

Oh, do not rest on anything this side of Jesus Christ. Say to your+ graces, say to your+ duties, say to your+ holiness, You+ are not my savior, you+ are not my mediator; and therefore you+ are not to be relied on, you+ are not to be rested in. It is my duty to perform closet duties, but it is my sin to rely on them, or to put confidence in them. Do them I must, but glory in them I must not. He who rests in his closet duties makes a savior of his closet duties. Let all your+ closet duties lead you+ to Jesus and leave you+ more in communion with Him and in dependence on Him, and then you+ will be thrice happy (Heb 7:25). Let all your closet prayers and tears, your closet fastings and meltings, be a star to guide you to Jesus, a Jacob’s ladder by which you may ascend into the bosom of eternal loves, and then you are safe forever.

Ah! It is sad to think how most men have forgotten their resting place, as the Lord complains: “My people are lost sheep; their shepherds have led them astray, causing them to roam the mountains. They have wandered from mountain to hill; they have forgotten their resting place” (Jer 50:6). Ah, how many poor souls there are who wander from mountain to hill, from one duty to another, and here they will rest, and there they will rest, and all this side of their resting place! O sirs! It is God Himself who is your+ resting place. It is His free grace, it is His singular mercy, it is His infinite love that is your+ resting place. It is the bosom of Christ, the favor of Christ, the satisfaction of Christ, and the pure, perfect, spotless, matchless, and glorious righteousness of Christ that is your+ resting place. And therefore say to all your+ closet duties and performances, Farewell! Prayer, farewell! Reading, farewell! Fasting, farewell! Tears, farewell! Sighs and groans, farewell! Meltings and humblings, I will never trust you+ again. I will never rest on you+ again, but I will now return to my resting place. I will now rest only in God and Christ. I will now rest wholly in God and Christ. I will now rest forever in God and Christ.

It was the saying of a precious saint that he was more afraid of his duties than of his sins, for the one often made him proud, the other always made him humble.

e. Labor to Bring Your Hearts into All Your Closet Prayers

My fifth advice and counsel is this: labor to bring your+ hearts into all your+ closet prayers and performances. See that your+ tongues and your+ hearts keep time and tune. “Give ear to my prayer—it does not come from feigned lips” (Ps 17:1), or, as it is in the Hebrew, “without lips of deceit.” Heart and tongue must go together; word and work, lip and life, prayer and practice, must echo one another, or else your prayers and your soul will be lost together. The labor of the lips and the travail of the heart must go together.

The Egyptians, of all fruits, chose the peach to consecrate to their goddess, for no other reason than that its fruit is like a heart and its leaf like a tongue. These very heathens, in the worship of their gods, thought it necessary that men’s hearts and tongues should go together. Ah, Christians! When your+ hearts and your+ tongues go together in your+ closet duties, then you+ make that sweet and delightful melody that is most winning and pleasing to the King of kings. The very soul of prayer lies in the pouring out of the soul before God (1 Sam 1:15). “These things come to mind as I pour out my soul” (Ps 42:4). So the Israelites poured out their souls like water before the Lord. So the church: “Your name and renown are the desire of our souls. My soul longs for You in the night; indeed, my spirit seeks You at dawn” (Isa 26:8–9). “Let us lift up our hearts and hands to God in heaven” (Lam 3:41). “Let us draw near with a sincere heart” (Heb 10:22). “God, whom I serve with my spirit … is my witness” (Rom 1:9). “I will pray with my spirit, … I will sing with my spirit” (1 Cor 14:15). “It is we who are the circumcision, we who worship God in the spirit” (Phil 3:3).

Under the law only the inward parts were to be offered to God in sacrifice; the skin belonged to the priests. From this we may easily gather that truth in the inward parts is what is most pleasing in a sacrifice. When the Athenians asked the oracle the cause of their frequent lack of success in battle against the Lacedaemonians, seeing that they offered in sacrifice to the gods the choicest things they could get, which their enemies did not, the oracle gave them this answer: the gods were better pleased with their enemies’ inward supplication without ambition than with all the Athenians’ outward pomp in costly sacrifices.

Ah, sirs! The reason why so many are so unsuccessful in their closet duties and services is that there is no more of their hearts in them. No man can make sure work or happy work of prayer unless he makes heart work of it. When a man’s heart is in his prayers, his returns from heaven will be great and sweet. A prayer in which the person’s heart bears no part is no prayer. When the soul is separated from the body, the man is dead; and so, when the heart is separated from the lip in prayer, the prayer is dead.

The Jews in our day write these words on the walls of their synagogues, Tephillah belo cavannah ceguph belo neshamah; that is, a prayer without the heart, or without the affections intent on it, is like a body without a soul.

In the Law of Moses, the priest was commanded to wash the inward parts and the feet of the sacrifices in water; and this was done, says Philo, “not without a mystery, to teach us to keep our hearts and affections clean when we draw near to God.” In all your+ closet duties God looks first and most to your+ hearts: “My son, give me your heart” (Prov 23:26). It is not a piece, it is not a corner of the heart, that will satisfy the Maker of the heart. The heart is a treasure, a bed of spices, a royal throne in which He delights. God does not look at the elegance of your+ prayers to see how neat they are, nor at the geometry of your+ prayers to see how long they are, nor at the arithmetic of your+ prayers to see how many they are, nor at the music of your+ prayers, nor at the sweetness of your+ voice, nor at the logic of your+ prayers, but at the sincerity of your+ prayers, how heartfelt they are. No prayer is acknowledged, approved, accepted, recorded, or rewarded by God except one in which the heart is present, sincerely and wholly. The true mother would not have the child divided. As God loves “a broken and a contrite heart,” so He loathes a divided heart (Ps 51:17; Jas 1:8).

God loves neither halting nor halving. He will be served truly and totally. The royal law is, “You shall love and serve the Lord your God with all your heart and with all your soul.” Among the heathens, when the beasts were cut up for sacrifice, the first thing the priest looked at was the heart, and if the heart was bad, the sacrifice was rejected. Truly, God rejects all those services and sacrifices in which the heart is absent, as you+ may see by comparing these scriptures together (Prov 21:27; Isa 1:11–12; 29:13; Matt 15:7–9; Ezek 33:30–32; Zech 7:4–6; 2 Chr 25:1–2; Ps 78:36–37). Prayer without the heart is no more than sounding brass or a clanging cymbal. Prayer is lovely and weighty only so far as the heart is in it, and not otherwise. In prayer God does not look at the lifting up of the voice, the wringing of the hands, the beating of the breast, an affected tone, studied notions, or seraphic expressions, but at the stirrings of the heart. God hears no more than the heart speaks. If the heart is mute, God will certainly be deaf. No prayer finds favor with God except one that is the travail of the heart.

On the same day that Julius Caesar came to the imperial dignity, sitting in his golden chair, he offered a beast in sacrifice to the gods. But when the beast was opened, it was without a heart, which the soothsayers looked on as an ill omen. It is a sad omen that you, who are habitually without heart in your closet duties, will rather provoke the Lord than prevail with Him. Of the heart, God seems to say to us, as Joseph did to his brothers concerning Benjamin, “You+ will not see my face without it.”

It was the saying of blessed Bradford that he would never leave a duty until he had brought his heart into the frame of the duty. He would not leave confession of sin until his heart was broken for sin. He would not leave petitioning for grace until his heart was revived and enlivened in a hopeful expectation of more grace. He would not leave thanksgiving until his heart was set free with a sense of the blessings he enjoyed and revived to return praise.

f. Be Fervent, Be Warm, Be Importunate with God

My sixth advice and counsel is this: Be fervent, be warm, be importunate with God in all your+ closet duties and exercises. “The effectual, fervent prayer of a righteous man has great power” (Jas 5:16)—or, as the Greek has it, “the working prayer.” That is, such working prayer as sets the whole man to work, as sets all the faculties of the soul and all the graces in the soul at work. The word signifies such a working as denotes the liveliest activity there can be. Certainly, all those common phrases of crying, wrestling, and striving with God that are scattered up and down in Scripture strongly show the holy importunity and sacred violence that the saints of old expressed in their addresses to God.

Fervency feathers the wings of prayer and makes them fly the swifter to heaven. An arrow drawn back only a little way does not fly far; but if it is drawn back to the head, it will fly far and pierce deeply. So fervent prayer flies as high as heaven and will certainly bring down blessings from there. Cold prayers call for a denial, but fervent prayers offer a sacred violence both to heaven and earth. Just as in a painted fire there is no heat, so in a cold prayer there is no heat, no warmth, no omnipotence, no devotion, no blessing. Cold prayers are like arrows without heads, like swords without edges, like birds without wings. They do not pierce, they do not cut, they do not fly up to heaven. Prayers that have no heavenly fire in them always freeze before they reach as high as heaven. But fervent prayer has great prevailing power with God.

“So Peter was kept in prison, but the church was fervently praying” (Acts 12:5). The Greek word ἐκτενής signifies urgent prayer, earnest prayer, stretched-out prayer—prayer stretched out on the tenter frame, as it were. These gracious souls strained and stretched themselves in prayer as men do who are running in a race. They prayed with all the strength of their souls and with all the fervency of their spirits; and accordingly they carried the day with God, as you+ may see in the verses that follow.

“… our twelve tribes … earnestly serve God day and night” (Acts 26:7), or rather, as the Greek has it, ἐν ἐκτενείᾳ, “in a stretched-out manner, serving God day and night.” These twelve tribes, or the godly Jews of the twelve tribes of Israel, stretched out their hearts, their affections, their graces, to the utmost in prayer. In all your+ private retreats, do as the twelve tribes did.

“Fervent in spirit, serving the Lord” (Rom 12:11). The Greek word ζέοντες signifies “seething hot.” God loves to see His people zealous and warm in His service. Without fervency of spirit no service finds acceptance in heaven. God is a pure act, and He loves His people to be lively and active in His service.

“Persistent in prayer” (Rom 12:12); προσκαρτεροῦντες, continuing with all your+ might in prayer. It is a metaphor from hunting dogs that will never give up the chase until they have caught their game.

“to strive together with me in your+ prayers to God for me” (Rom 15:30); συναγωνίσασθαι, strive mightily, strive as champions strive, even to an agony, as the word implies. It is a military word, and denotes the kind of fervent wrestling or striving that is for life and death.

“Always wrestling in prayer for you+” (Col 4:12). The Greek word used here, ἀγωνιζόμενος, signifies to strive or wrestle as those do who strive for mastery. It denotes the vehemence and fervor of Epaphras’ prayers for the Colossians. Just as wrestlers bend, and writhe, and stretch, and strain every joint of their bodies so that they may be victorious, so Epaphras bent, and writhed, and stretched, and strained every joint of his soul—if I may speak so—so that he might be victorious with God on the Colossians’ behalf.

So, when Jacob was alone with God, ah, how earnest and fervent he was in his wrestlings with God (Gen 32:24–27; Hos 12:4–5)! He wrestles and weeps, and weeps and wrestles. He tugs hard with God, he keeps his hold, and he will not let God go, until, as a prince, he had prevailed with Him.

Fervent prayer is the soul’s contention, the soul struggling with God. It is sweating work; it is the sweat and blood of the soul; it is the spending of all the strength and powers of the soul to the utmost. He who would gain the victory over God in private prayer must strain every string of his heart. In beseeching God, he must besiege Him, and so get the better of Him. He must be like importunate beggars who will not be put off with frowns, or silence, or sad answers. Those who would be masters of their requests must, like the importunate widow, press God so far as to put Him to a holy blush, if I may say so with reverence. With a holy impudence, as Basil puts it, they must make God ashamed to look them in the face if He should deny the importunity of their souls.

Had Abraham had a little more of this impudence, says one, when he pleaded for Sodom, it might have gone well. Abraham brought the price down to ten righteous, and there his modesty stopped him.
