Had he gone lower, God only knows what might have been done; for God did not go away, says the text, until “He had left off speaking with Abraham” (Gen 18:33). That is, until Abraham had no more to say to God. Abraham left off asking before God left off granting. He left off praying before God left off lowering the price; and so Sodom was lost.

Oh, the heavenly fire, the holy fervency that was in Daniel’s closet prayer! “O Lord, listen! O Lord, forgive! O Lord, hear and act! For Your sake … do not delay” (Dan 9:19). Just as there are two kinds of antidotes against poison, hot and cold, so there are two kinds of antidotes against all the troubles of this life, fervent prayers and holy patience—the one hot, the other cold; the one enlivening, and the other quenching. And holy Daniel made use of them both. Fervency is to prayer as the fire was to the spices in the censer, or as wings to the bird, or as oil to the wheels; and Daniel found this by experience.

God does not look for any James with calloused knees from constant prayer, nor for any Bartholomew with a hundred prayers for the morning and as many for the evening, but for fervency of spirit in prayer, which alone carries all with God. Feeble prayers, like weak labor pains, pass off and never bring a mercy to birth. Cold prayers are stillborn children in whom the Father of spirits can take no pleasure. Just as a painted man is no man, and painted fire is no fire, so a cold prayer is no prayer. Prayers that do not first warm our own hearts never win the heart of God. As a body without a soul, much wood without a fire, a bullet in a gun without powder, so are all prayers without fervency of spirit.

Luther calls prayer bombarda Christianorum, the gun or cannon of Christians, or the Christian’s gunshot.

The hottest springs send out their waters by boiling up. Cold prayers make a smoke, a choking smoke, in the eyes of God. Lazy prayers never obtain noble answers. Lazy beggars may perish for all their begging (Isa 1:15; 65:5).

Those who have a male in their flock and offer the Lord a female; those who offer the Lord the torn and the lame and the sick; those who put God off with their cold, lazy, sleepy, and merely outward devotions are condemned, convicted, and cursed by God (Mal 1:13–14). David compares his prayers to incense, and no incense was offered without fire (Ps 141:2). It was the fire that made its smoke ascend. It is only fervent prayer that hits the mark and pierces the walls of heaven, though they are made of brass and iron like those of Gaza (Isa 45:2). While the child only whimpers and whines in the cradle, the mother lets it alone; but once it raises its voice and cries outright, then she runs and picks it up. So it is with a Christian: “This poor man cried out” (Ps 34:6). There is his fervency: he cried out. But it was silently and secretly, in the presence of King Achish, as Moses did at the Red Sea, and as Nehemiah did in the presence of the king of Persia. “And the Lord heard him; He saved him from all his troubles” (Ps 34:6). Here is his prevailing power. So Latimer plied the throne of grace with great fervency, crying out, “Once again, Lord, once again restore the gospel to England,” and God heard him.

Hudson the martyr, deserted by God at the stake, slipped out from under his chain; and having prayed fervently, he was comforted at once and suffered valiantly.

I have read of one Giles of Brussels, a Dutch martyr, who was so fervent in his prayer, kneeling by himself in some secret place of the prison where he was, that he seemed to forget himself. When he was called to his meal, he neither heard nor saw who stood by him until he was lifted up by the arms; and then he would speak gently to them, like one awakened from a trance.

So Gregory Nazianzen, speaking of his sister Gorgonia, says that in the vehemence of her prayer she came to a religious impudence with God, so that she threatened heaven and told God that she would never leave His altar until her petition was granted.

Let us make it our business to follow these noble examples, if ever we would play the prince in prayer and so prevail with God. An importunate soul in prayer is like the poor beggar who prays and knocks, who prays and waits, who prays and works, who knocks and knits, who begs and patches, and will not stir from the door until he has received alms. Well, friends, remember this: God regards lukewarm prayers no more than He regards lukewarm persons, and those He has threatened to spit out of His mouth. Prayers that are only lip-labor are lost labor; and therefore, in all your+ closet prayers, look to the fervency of your+ spirits.

g. Be Constant, as Well as Fervent, in Closet Prayer

My seventh advice and counsel is this: Be constant, as well as fervent, in closet prayer. See that you+ hold on and hold out, and that you+ persevere to the end in private prayer. “Pray without ceasing” (1 Thess 5:17). A man must always pray in habit, though not in act. He must have his heart in a praying disposition in all states and conditions. Though closet prayer may have an intermission, yet it must never have a cessation. “Then Jesus told them a parable about their need to pray always and not lose heart” (Luke 18:1), or, as the Greek has it, “not to shrink back, as sluggards in work or cowards in war.” Closet prayer is a fire like the one on the altar, which was never to go out day or night. “Night and day we pray most earnestly” (1 Thess 3:10). Paul speaks like a man made up entirely of prayer, like a man who minded nothing so much as prayer. “Pray in the Spirit always, with every kind of prayer and supplication. To this end, stay alert with all perseverance in your+ prayers” (Eph 6:18). Calvin makes this difference between “praying always” at the beginning of this verse and “praying with perseverance” at the end of it: “By ‘praying always,’” he says, “he exhorts us to pray in prosperity as well as in adversity, and not to quit the duty of prayer in a prosperous state because we are not driven to it by outward pressing necessities and miseries. And by ‘praying with perseverance,’ he admonishes us not to be weary of the work, but to continue urgent and constant in its performance, though we do not at once receive what we pray for.” So “praying always” is opposed to a neglect of the duty in its proper times and seasons, and “praying with perseverance” is opposed to a losing of heart in our spirits over this or that particular request that we offer up to God. When God turns a deaf ear to our prayers, we must not fret or faint. We must not be dismayed or discouraged, but we must hold up and hold on in the duty of prayer with invincible patience, courage, and constancy, as the church did (Lam 3:8, 44, 55–57). “Continue steadfastly in prayer, being watchful and thankful” (Col 4:2). We must be constant and urgent in closet prayer; we must wait on it and lay everything aside for it. He who is in his closet only by fits and starts will neither glorify God nor benefit his own soul. If we do not make a trade of closet prayer, we will never make any earnings from closet prayer. Just as those who make money by their ironworks keep a continual fire in their ironworks, so those who would get any good for their souls by closet duties must keep close and constant to closet duties. The hypocrite is constant only in inconstancy; he is in his closet only by fits and starts. Now and then, when he is in a good mood, you will find him stepping into his closet, but he never keeps at it. “Will he always call upon God?” (Job 27:10), or, as the Hebrew has it, “Will he call upon God at every time?” When they are under the smarting rod, or upon the tormenting rack, or under grievous needs, or struck with panic fears, etc., then you will see them run to their closets, as Joab ran to the horns of the altar when he was in danger of death. But they never persevere; they never hold out to the end. And therefore, in the end they lose both their closet prayers and their souls together (Isa 26:16; Ps 78:34; Zech 7:5).

It was a most profane and blasphemous speech of that atheistic wretch who told God “that he was no common beggar, and that he had never troubled Him with prayer before, and if He would only hear him this time, he would never trouble Him again.”

Closet prayer is hard work; and a man must tug hard at it and stick close to it, as Jacob did, if ever he intends to gain any internal or eternal advantage by it (Gen 32). Daniel chose to risk his life rather than give up praying in his room (Dan 6). The one who will be saved and crowned is not he who begins in the Spirit and ends in the flesh (Gal 3:3), nor he who puts his hand to the plow and looks back (Luke 9:62), but he who perseveres to the end in prayer (Matt 24:13). It is he who perseveres in doing good who will eat of the hidden manna and have the white stone, “inscribed with a new name, known only to the one who receives it” (Rev 2:17). Those precious, praying, mourning souls in Ezekiel 9:4, 6 who were marked to be preserved in Jerusalem were distinguished, some of the learned say, by the letter ת, tau, the last of all the Hebrew letters, to teach them that they must hold out and hold on to the end in doing good. It is constancy in closet duty that crowns the Christian and commends the duty.

But would God have His people cast off their callings, and cast off all care of their families, and shut themselves up in their closets, and there spend their whole time in secret prayer? Oh, no! Every duty must have its time and place. And as one friend must not shut out another, so one duty must not shut out another (Eccl 3:1). The duties of my particular calling as a man must not shut out the duties of my general calling as a Christian. And the duties of my general calling as a Christian must not shut out the duties of my particular calling as a man. But so that you+ may be fully satisfied on this point, you+ must remember that a man may be said to pray always,

[1] First, when his heart is always in a praying frame. A man whose heart is always in a giving frame may truly be said to give always; a man whose heart is always in a suffering frame, to suffer always—“For Your sake we face death all day long” (Ps 44:22)—and a man whose heart is always in a sinning frame, to sin always (2 Pet 2:14; Jer 9:3). Just so, a man whose heart is always in a praying frame may as truly be said to pray always.

[2] Secondly, a man prays always when he takes hold of every fitting season and opportunity for pouring out his soul before the Lord in his closet. To pray always is ἐν παντὶ καιρῷ, to pray at every opportunity; but of this I have spoken before.

Some have observed of Proteus that he used to give certain oracles, but it was hard to make him speak and deliver them, for he would turn himself into various shapes and forms. Yet if they would hold out and press him hard without fear, whatever form or shape he appeared in, they were sure to have satisfying oracles. So if we continue constant in our closet wrestlings with God, if we hold on in private prayer, though God should appear to us in the form or shape of a judge, an enemy, a stranger, we will certainly succeed at last. “O woman, your faith is great! Let it be done for you as you desire.” And her daughter was healed from that very hour (Matt 15:28).

When the philosopher was asked in his old age why he did not give up his practice and take his ease, he answered, “When a man has a race of forty furlongs to run, would you have him sit down at the thirty-ninth, and so lose the prize, the crown for which he ran?” O sirs! If you+ do not hold out to the end in closet prayer, you+ will certainly lose the heavenly prize, the crown of life, the crown of righteousness, the crown of glory.

Continuing to give glory to God in this way of duty is as necessary and requisite as beginning to give glory to God in this way of duty; for though the beginning is more than half, yet the end is more than all. The finish crowns the work. The God of all perfections expects that our ultimum vitae, our life’s end, should be His optimum gloria, His best glory; that our last works should be our best works; and that we should persevere in closet prayer to the end (Rev 2:10).

h. Thirst and Long After Communion with God

My eighth advice and counsel is this: In all your+ closet prayers, thirst and long after communion with God. In all your+ private retreats, settle for nothing below fellowship with God, nothing below a sweet and spiritual enjoyment of God (Song 3:1–3; Ps 73:28). “One thing I have asked of the Lord this is what I desire: to dwell in the house of the Lord all the days of my life, to gaze on the beauty of the Lord and seek Him in His temple” (Ps 27:4). The temple of the Lord without communion with the Lord of the temple will not satisfy David’s soul.

“As the deer pants for streams of water, so my soul longs after You, O God. My soul thirsts for God, the living God. When shall I come and appear in God’s presence?” (Ps 42:1–2). The deer, as Aristotle and others observe, is by nature the hottest and driest of all creatures; but especially when it is chased and hunted, it is extremely thirsty. The female is meant here, as the Greek article, ἡ ἔλαφος, he elaphos, shows. Now, in the females the pangs of thirst are stronger, as the naturalists observe. By this David reveals what a vehement and inflamed thirst there was in his soul after communion with God; and as nothing could satisfy the hunted deer but the streams of water, so nothing could satisfy his soul but the enjoyment of God.

“Then I will go to the altar of God, to God, my greatest joy” (Ps 43:4). The altar of God is put here for the worship of God. Now, it is not merely the worship of God, but communion with God in His worship, that was David’s greatest joy. “O God, You are my God. Earnestly I seek You; my soul thirsts for You. My body yearns for You in a dry and weary land without water, to see Your power and Your glory, as I have seen You in the sanctuary” (Ps 63:1–2). David’s soul did not thirst after a crown, a kingdom, or any worldly greatness or glory, but after a choice and sweet enjoyment of God in his wilderness condition. Never did any woman with child long more after this or that than David’s soul longed to enjoy felt communion with God in the midst of all his sorrows and sufferings. “My soul longs, even faints, for the courts of the Lord my heart and my flesh cry out for the living God” (Ps 84:2). By “the courts of the Lord” we are to understand the ordinances. Now, these without communion with God would never have satisfied David’s soul.

I commend that saying of Bernard, Nunquam abs te, absque te recedo: I never come to You but by You; I never go from You without You.

Whenever you+ go into your+ closets, press hard after real and felt communion with God, so that you+ may come out of your+ closets with some shining of God upon your+ spirits, as Moses came down from the mountain with his face shining (Exod 34:29–35). Oh, do not settle for your+ closet prayers, or tears, or joys, or freedom of heart; but labor and long to enjoy such inward and close fellowship with God in your+ closets as may leave so choice and sweet a savor of God upon both your+ hearts and lives that others may be forced to say, Surely these have been with Jesus (Acts 4:13). It is sad when Christians return from their closets to their shops, their trades, their families, their business without the least visible rays of divine glory upon them.

O sirs! Closet prayer will prove to be nothing but a dry, sapless, lifeless, heartless, comfortless thing if you+ do not enjoy communion with God in it. Communion with God is the very life, soul, and crown of all your+ closet duties; and therefore, press after it as for life. When you+ go into your+ closets, let go of everything that may hinder your+ enjoying Christ, and embrace everything that makes way for your+ enjoyment of Christ. Oh, let closet prayer be a golden bridge, a ferryboat, a chariot to carry your+ souls over to God, and to bring you+ into a more intimate communion with God. Let no closet duty satisfy you+ or content you+ if you+ have not conversed with God in it as a child converses with his father, or as a wife converses with her husband, or as a friend converses with his friend, even face to face. Nothing reveals more unsoundness, falseness, and baseness of heart than this: when men make duty the end of duty, and prayer the end of prayer; when men can begin a duty, and go on in a duty, and close up a duty, and bless and stroke themselves after a duty, and yet never enjoy the least communion with God in the duty.

Question: But how can a man know whether he has real communion with God in a duty or not? This is a very noble and necessary question, and accordingly it calls for a clear and satisfying answer; and therefore, I answer thus:

1. A Man May Have Communion with God in Sorrow and Tears

First, A man may have communion with God in sorrow and tears when he does not have communion with God in joy and delight (Ps 51:17). A man may have communion with God in a heart-humbling, a heart-melting, and a heart-abasing way, when he does not have communion with God in a heart-reviving, a heart-cheering, and a heart-comforting way.

It is a very great mistake among many tender-spirited Christians to think that they have no communion with God in their closets unless they meet with God embracing and kissing (Song 2:4–6), cheering and comforting their souls. When they find God raising the springs of joy and comfort in their souls; when they find God speaking peace to them; when they find the special, felt presence of God cheering, refreshing, and setting them free in their closets, oh, then they are willing to grant that they have had sweet communion with God in their closets. But if God meets with them in their closets and only breaks their hearts for sin and from sin, if He meets with them and only makes His power and His presence known in humbling and casting down their souls, at the sight and sense of their strong corruptions and many imperfections, how unwilling they are to believe that they have had any communion with God!

Well, friends, remember this once and for all: a Christian may have as real communion with God in a heart-humbling way as he can have in a heart-comforting way. A Christian may have as choice communion with God when his eyes are full of tears as he can have when his heart is full of joy (John 20:11–19). Sometimes God meets with a poor Christian in his closet and greatly breaks him and humbles him, and at other times He meets with the same Christian in his closet and mightily cheers him and comforts him. Sometimes God meets with a poor soul in his closet, and there He sweetly quiets him and stills him. And at other times He meets with the same soul in his closet, and then He greatly revives him and enlivens him.

God does not always come upon the soul in one way. He does not always come in at one and the same door (John 3:8). We sometimes look for a friend to come in at the front door, and then he comes in at the back door. And at other times, when we look for him at the back door, then he comes in at the front door. And just so it is with God’s coming into His people’s souls. Sometimes they go into their closets and expect God to come in at the front door of joy and comfort, and then God comes in at the back door of sorrow and grief. And at other times, when they expect God to come in at the back door of humbling, breaking, and melting their hearts, then God comes in at the front door of joy and consolation, cheering and rejoicing their souls.

2. Not All Christians Enjoy the Same Communion in Their Closets

Secondly, I answer that not all Christians enjoy the same communion with God in their closets. Some enjoy much communion with God in their closets, and others enjoy only a little communion with God in their closets.

Moses had a clearer, more glorious, and more constant communion with God in his day than any others had in the times in which he lived (Exod 33:11; Deut 5:4; Num 12:7–8). God spoke to none “face to face” as He did to Moses. And Abraham (Gen 18), in his time, had a closer, friendlier, and more intimate communion with God than holy Lot or any others had in that day. And though all the disciples, Judas excepted, had sweet communion with Christ in the days of His flesh, yet Peter, James, and John had a clearer, choicer, and fuller communion with Him than the rest had (Matt 17:1–4). Among all the disciples, John had the closest bosom communion with Christ. He was the greatest favorite in Christ’s court. He leaned on Christ’s bosom; he could say anything to Christ, and he could know anything of Christ, and he could have anything from Christ (John 13:23; 20:2; 21:20). Now, it may be shown in this way that not all Christians enjoy communion with God alike in their closets:

First, not all Christians prepare alike to enjoy closet communion with God; and therefore, not all Christians enjoy communion with God alike in their closets (Eccl 5:1; Ps 10:17). Commonly, he who prepares and fits himself most for closet communion with God is the man who enjoys the most closet communion with God (2 Chr 30:17–20).

Secondly, not all Christians prize communion with God in their closets alike. Some prize communion with God in their closets before all and above all other things, as that noble marquis said, “Cursed is he who prefers all the world to one hour’s communion with God.” They look on it as the pearl of great price, and to enjoy it they are ready to sell all and part with all. Others prize it at a lower rate, and so enjoy less of it than those who set a higher price and value on it (Job 23:12; Ps 119:127; Matt 13:45–46).

Thirdly, not all Christians press after communion with God in their closets alike. Some press after communion with God in their closets as a condemned man presses after a pardon, or as a prisoner in close confinement presses after release, or as a poor beggar presses after alms (Ps 63:8; Isa 26:8–9). Now, you+ know that these press on with the greatest earnestness, the greatest fervency, and the greatest importunity imaginable. But others press after communion with God in their closets more coldly, more carelessly, more superficially, more lazily: “I have taken off my robe—must I put it back on? I have washed my feet—must I soil them again?” (Song 5:3). Now, those who press hardest after communion with God in their closets are usually blessed with the highest degrees of closet communion with God.

Fourthly, not all Christians make use of their communion with God in their closets alike; and therefore, not all Christians enjoy communion with God alike in their closets. Some Christians make a wiser, a humbler, a holier, a more faithful, a more fruitful, and a more constant use of their closet communion with God than others do; and therefore, they are blessed with higher degrees of communion with God than others are. Some Christians make more use of their closet communion with God against the world, the flesh, and the devil than others do; and therefore, no wonder if they enjoy more communion with God in their closets than others do.

Fifthly, not all Christians need communion with God in their closets alike; and therefore, not all Christians have the same communion with God in their closets. Not all Christians have the same place in the mystical body of Christ (1 Cor 12:14ff): some rule, and others are ruled. Now, every man stands in more or less need of communion with God according to the place that he holds in the body of Christ. Again, not all Christians have the same burdens to bear, nor the same difficulties to contend with, nor the same dangers to escape, nor the same temptations to wrestle with, nor the same passions and corruptions to mortify, nor the same blessings and experiences to make use of; and therefore, not all Christians need communion with God alike in their closets. Now, commonly God imparts Himself more or less in ways of communion as the various necessities and conditions of His people require.

Sixthly and lastly, not all Christians alike meet with outward interruptions, or inward interruptions; and therefore, not all Christians have communion with God alike in their closets. Some Christians meet with a world of outward and inward interruptions, more than others do. Some Christians’ outward callings, relations, conditions, and stations afford more plentiful matter and occasions to interrupt them in their closet communion with God than other Christians’ callings, relations, conditions, and stations do. Besides, Satan is busier with some Christians than he is with other Christians; and corruptions work more strongly and violently in some Christians than they do in other Christians. And let me add this to all the rest: the very natural dispositions of some Christians are more averse to closet duties than the natural dispositions of other Christians are. And therefore, not all Christians have communion with God alike in their closets, but some have more and some have less, as God in His infinite wisdom sees best.

Now, let no Christian say that he has no communion with God in closet prayer because he does not have such a full, such a choice, such a sweet, such a felt, and such a constant communion with God in closet prayer as such and such saints have had, or as such and such saints now have; for not all saints enjoy communion with God alike in their closets. Some have more, some have less. Some have a higher degree, others a lower. Some are caught up into the third heaven, when others are caught up only into the clouds. What man is so childish and babyish as to argue like this: that he has no wisdom because he does not have the wisdom of Solomon; or that he has no strength because he does not have the strength of Samson; or that he has no life because he does not have the swiftness of Ahimaaz (2 Sam 18:23); or that he has no wealth because he does not have the riches of the rich man? And yet many weak Christians are so childish and babyish as to argue like this: that they have no communion with God in their closets because they do not have such high, such comforting, and such constant communion with God in their closets as such and such saints have had, or as such and such saints now have. They should seriously consider instead that though some saints have a great communion with God, yet other saints have only a small communion with God; and though some Christians have a strong communion with God, yet other Christians have only a weak communion with God; and though some of the people of God have a very close and near communion with God, yet others of the people of God have only a more remote communion with God; and though some of God’s servants have a daily, constant, and uninterrupted communion with God, yet others of His servants have only a more passing and inconstant communion with God.

3. When a Man Exercises Grace in Closet Duties

Thirdly, I answer, When a man exercises grace in closet duties, then certainly he has communion with God in closet duties (2 Tim 1:17; 1 Tim 2:8). When a man in closet duties exercises faith in God, or faith in the promises, or faith in the blood of Christ; or when a man in private duties exercises repentance for sin, or love for Jesus Christ, or sets up God as the object of his fear, or as the object of his joy, then he has communion with God, then he has fellowship with the Father and with the Son (1 John 1:3). An unregenerate man may exercise gifts and talents in a duty, but he cannot exercise grace in a duty. For no man can exercise grace in a duty except he who has grace in his soul. And that is why unsanctified persons, under the highest activity of their skills, talents, and gifts in religious duties, enjoy no communion with God at all. Witness the scribes and Pharisees, Demas, Judas, Simon Magus, etc. (Isa 1:11–13). If ever you+ would have evidence of your+ communion with God in closet duties, look carefully to the activity of your+ graces; carefully stir up the grace of God that is in you+ (2 Tim 1:6).

4. When a Man Gives God the Glory of All His Own Actions

Fourthly, I answer, When a man has communion with God in his closet, then he gives God the glory of all his actions and activities (Ps 115:1). Communion with God always helps a man to set the crown of praise and honor on the head of God. Witness that gracious and grateful doxology of David and his people in 1 Chronicles 29:13, “Now therefore, our God, we give You thanks, and we praise Your glorious name.”

Men who enjoy no communion with God in religious duties are always sacrificing to their own net and burning incense to their own dragnet (Hab 1:16). They are always blessing themselves, and stroking themselves, and applauding themselves. They think the garland of praise, the crown of honor, suits no head but their own (Luke 18:11–12). But now, men who enjoy communion with God in religious duties will uncrown themselves to crown God; they will uncrown their duties to crown the God of their duties; they will uncrown their skills, talents, gifts, and freedom in prayer to set the crown of praise on the head of God alone (Acts 3:11–13, 16; Rev 4:10–11; 5:11–12). You think that you have communion with God in closet duties. Yes, you say that you have communion with God in closet duties; but on whose head do you put the garland of praise (Ps 148:13)? If on God’s head, you have communion with God; if on your own head, you have no communion with God. As all the rivers run into the sea, and all the lines meet in the center, so when all our closet duties end and center in the advancement of God’s glory, then we have communion with God in them.

Constantine used to write the name of Christ over his door. When a man has communion with Christ in a duty, then he will write the name of Christ, the honor of Christ, upon his duty. Some say that the name of Jesus was engraved on the heart of Ignatius. Sure I am that when a man has communion with God in a duty, then you+ will find the honor and glory of Jesus engraved on that duty.

5. When Closet Duties Leave the Soul in a Better Frame

Fifthly, I answer, When the performance of closet duties leaves the soul in a better frame, then a man has communion with God in them. When a man comes away from closet duties in a more holy frame, or in a more humble frame, or in a more spiritual frame, or in a more watchful frame, or in a more heavenly frame, or in a more broken frame, or in a more revived and enlivened frame, then certainly he has had communion with God in those duties. When a man comes out of his closet and finds the frame of his heart more strongly set against sin than ever, and more highly resolved to walk with God than ever, and more eminently crucified to the world than ever, and more divinely fixed against temptations than ever, then beyond all doubt he has had communion with God in his closet.

6. When Closet Duties Fit a Man for the Duties Next at Hand

Sixthly, I answer, When closet duties fit a man for the other duties that lie next at hand, then doubtless he has had communion with God in them. When private duties fit a man for public duties, or when private duties fit a man for the duties of his place, calling, and condition, in which God has set him, then certainly he has had fellowship with God in them (Eccl 9:10). When a man in closet duties finds more spiritual strength and power to perform the duties that are next required of him, then assuredly he has met with God. When private prayer fits me better for family prayer or public prayer, then I may safely conclude that God has drawn near to my soul in private prayer. Or when one closet duty fits me for another closet duty, as when praying fits me for reading, or reading for praying; or when the more external duties in my closet (reading or praying) fit me for the more spiritual and internal duties (self-examination, holy meditation, soul-humbling), then I may rest satisfied that there has been some choice fellowship between God and my soul. When I find that the more I pray in my closet, the fitter I am to pray in my closet; and the more I read in my closet, the fitter I am to read in my closet; and the more I meditate in my closet, the fitter I am to meditate in my closet; and the more I search and examine my heart in my closet, the fitter I am to search and examine my heart in my closet; and the more I humble and abase my soul in my closet, the fitter I am to humble and abase my soul in my closet, then I may be confident that I have had communion with God in my closet.

7. Private Communion with God Is Soul-Humbling and Soul-Abasing

Seventhly, I answer that all private communion with God is very soul-humbling and soul-abasing. Abraham was a man who had much private communion with God, and a man who was very unworthy and low in his own eyes. “Then Abraham answered, ‘Behold, I have ventured to speak to the Lord, though I am but dust and ashes’” (Gen 18:27). “In respect of my origin,” says Abraham, “I am but base dust and ashes. And in respect of my deserts, I deserve to be burned to ashes.” There are none so humble as those who have the nearest communion with God (Gen 28:10–18). Jacob was a man who had much private communion with God, and a man who was very little in his own eyes. “I am unworthy of the least of all the mercies and all the faithfulness You have shown Your servant” (Gen 32:10), or, as the Hebrew has it, “I am less than all Your mercies.” When Jacob had to deal with Laban, he pleads his merit; but when he has to do with God, he abases himself below the least of His mercies (Gen 31:38–41). Moses was a man who had much private communion with God, as I have shown before, and a man who was the meekest and humblest person in all the world. “Now Moses was a very humble man, more so than any man on the face of the earth” (Num 12:3).

Josephus, writing of Moses, says, if he may be believed, “that he was so free from passions that he knew no such thing in his own soul. He knew passions only by their names, and saw them in others, but did not feel them in himself.” And so, when the glory of God appeared to him, he falls on his face (Num 16:22) as a sign of humility and self-abasement. David was a man who had much private communion with God, as all agree; and how greatly he abases and belittles himself! “The king of Israel has come out to look for a flea” (1 Sam 26:20); and what is weaker and more contemptible than a flea? “Against whom has the king of Israel come out? Whom are you pursuing? A dead dog? A flea?” (1 Sam 24:14). It is as if David had said, “It is not worth the time and the labor. It is below the dignity and honor of the king of Israel to take such pains, and to pursue so violently, such a poor nothing as I am, who have no more strength or power to bite or hurt than a dead dog or a poor flea has.” “But I am a worm and not a man” (Ps 22:6). Now, what is weaker, what less regarded, what more despicable, what more trampled underfoot than a poor worm? The Hebrew word tolagnath, here rendered “worm,” signifies a very little worm, such as breeds in scarlet, so little that a man can scarcely see it or perceive it. Thus you+ see that holy David lowers himself below a worm, indeed, below the least of worms. No man sets so low a value on himself as he who has the most private communion with God. The twenty-four elders cast down their crowns at the feet of Jesus Christ (Rev 4:10–11). Their crowns signify all their inward and outward dignities, excellencies, and glories. And the casting down of their crowns signifies their great humility and self-abasement. When Christians, in their closets and out of their closets, can cast down their crowns, their duties, their services, their graces, their times of freedom in prayer, their enjoyments at the feet of Jesus Christ, and sit down abasing and belittling themselves, then certainly they have had a very near and sweet communion with God.

Chrysostom has a remarkable saying about humility: “Suppose,” he says, “that one man were defiled with every kind of sin and enormity, yet humble, and another man enriched with gifts, graces, and duties, yet proud. The humble sinner would be in a safer condition than this proud saint.”

When a man can come away from closet duties and say, as Ignatius once said of himself, Non sum dignus dici minimus, I am not worthy to be called the least, then certainly he has had fellowship with God in them. All the communion that a man has with God in his closet is very soul-humbling and soul-abasing. In all a man’s communion with God, some beams, some rays of the glory and majesty of God will shine on his soul. Now all divine manifestations are very humbling and abasing, as you+ may clearly see in those two great instances of Job and Isaiah. “My ears had heard of You, but now my eyes have seen You. Therefore I abhor myself, and I repent in dust and ashes” (Job 42:5–6). “In the year that King Uzziah died, I saw the Lord seated on a throne, high and exalted; and the train of His robe filled the temple.…Then I said: ‘Woe is me, for I am ruined, because I am a man of unclean lips dwelling among a people of unclean lips; for my eyes have seen the King, the Lord of Hosts’” (Isa 6:1, 5). What sweet communion Elijah had with God in the low cave!

There was a gentlewoman of no ordinary rank or upbringing who, being much troubled in mind and grievously deserted by God, could not be persuaded by her husband or any other Christian friends either to hear or to read anything that might work for her spiritual good. At last her husband, by much importunity, prevailed so far with her that she was willing for him to read one chapter of the Bible to her. So he read Isaiah 57, and came to the fifteenth verse: “For thus says the One who is high and lifted up, who inhabits eternity, whose name is Holy: ‘I dwell in a high and holy place, and with the contrite and humble in spirit, to restore the spirit of the lowly and revive the heart of the contrite.’” “Oh,” she said, “is it so, that God dwells with a contrite and humble spirit? Then I am sure He dwells with me, for my heart is broken into a thousand pieces. Oh, happy text and happy time, that I should ever hear such comfort!” And with that she recovered. The more communion any man has with God, the more humble and broken his heart will be.

Holy Bradford was a man who had much private communion with God, and he would often sign his letters, “John the hypocrite, and a very painted sepulcher.” Agur was one of the wisest and holiest men on the earth in his days, and he condemned himself as “more brutish than any man,” and as one who lacks “the understanding of a man” (Prov 30:2). How much sweeter is the smell of the lowly violet that hides its head than all the gaudy tulips in your+ garden! The lowly Christian is the most attractive and the loveliest Christian. When a man can come out of his closet and cry out with Augustine, “I hate what I am, and love and desire what I am not. O wretched man that I am, in whom the cross of Christ has not yet eaten out the poisonous and bitter taste of the first tree”; or, as another says, “Lord, I see, and yet am blind. I will, and yet rebel. I hate, and yet I love. I follow, and yet I fall. I press forward, yet I faint. I wrestle, yet I limp”; then he may be confident that he has had communion with God in his closet. He who comes away from closet duties in a self-abasing way, laying himself low at the foot of God, has certainly had communion with God. But when men come out of their closets with their hearts swelled and lifted up, as the hearts of the Pharisees were (Luke 18:11–12), it is evident that they have had no communion with God. God has not been near to the souls of those who say, “Keep to yourself; do not come near me, for I am holier than you!” (Isa 65:5).

8. When It Wounds and Weakens His Beloved Corruption

Eighthly, and lastly, when a man finds such a secret power and force running through his closet duties that it wounds and weakens his beloved corruption, breaks the strength and power of his special sin, sets his heart more fully, resolutely, and constantly against his darling lust, and stirs up a greater rage, a more bitter hatred, and a fiercer indignation against the toad in the bosom, then certainly he has had communion with God in his closet duties.

Consult this scripture: “In that day men will cast away to the moles and bats their idols of silver and gold—the idols they made to worship” (Isa 2:20). In the day when God would take these poor hearts into communion with Himself, their hearts would be filled with such rage and indignation against their most delightful and desirable idols that they would take not only those made of wood and stone, but even their most precious and costly idols, those made of silver and gold, and cast them to the moles and to the bats, to show their horrible hatred and indignation against them. Idolatry was the darling sin of the Jews. Their hearts were so exceedingly taken with their idols, and delighted in them, that they did not care what they spent on them. “They lavish gold from the bag and weigh out silver on scales; they hire a goldsmith to fashion it into a god, so they can bow down and worship” (Isa 46:6). The word used here in the Hebrew for lavish properly signifies to waste or spend riotously. They valued their treasure so lightly that they did not care what they spent on their idols. God gave them gold and silver as pledges of His favor and bounty, and they lavish it on their idols, as if God had hired them to be wicked. Oh, but when God would come and take these poor wretches into close and near communion with Himself, then you will find their wrath and rage rising against their idols, as you may see in Isaiah 30:19–21. Their communion with God is more than hinted at there; but mark verse 22: “You+ will defile your silver-plated idols and your gold-plated images. You will throw them away like a menstrual cloth, saying to them, ‘Be gone!’” None defile, deface, detest, and disgrace their idols like those who are taken into communion with God. Fellowship with God will make a man throw away, like a menstrual cloth, the very idols in which he has most delighted, and with which he has been most pleased and enamored.

Idols were Ephraim’s bosom sin. “Ephraim is joined,” or glued, as the Hebrew has it, “to idols; leave him alone!” (Hos 4:17). Oh, but when you+ find Ephraim taken into close communion with God, as you+ do in Hosea 14:4–7, then you+ will find another spirit on him. “Ephraim shall say, ‘What have I to do anymore with idols?’” (Hos 14:8). I have had too much to do with them already. I will never have anything to do with them again. Oh, how my soul detests and abhors them and rises up against them! Oh, how I now loathe and abominate them more than ever I loved them or delighted in them before! After the return of the Jews from Babylon, they so hated and abhorred idols that in the time of the Romans they chose to die rather than allow the eagle, the imperial standard, to be set up in their temple.

Though closet duties are weak in themselves, yet when a man has communion with God in them, they prove exceedingly powerful for casting down strongholds, and vain imaginations, and every high thing and thought “set up against the knowledge of God” (2 Cor 10:4–5). When a man comes out of his closet with a heart more fully and steadfastly set against every known sin, but especially against his bosom sin, his darling sin, his Delilah that he played and toyed with most, and that he has hugged in his bosom with pleasure and delight, then certainly he has had private communion with God.

After Moses had enjoyed forty days’ private communion with God on the mountain, how his heart rose and his anger burned against the molten calf that his people had made! “As Moses approached the camp and saw the calf and the dancing, he burned with anger and threw the tablets out of his hands, shattering them at the base of the mountain. Then he took the calf they had made, burned it in the fire, ground it to powder, and scattered the powder over the face of the water. Then he forced the Israelites to drink it” (Exod 32:19–20). Moses never had more intimate fellowship with God than now, and he never showed so much holy zeal, anger, and indignation against sin as now. When a man comes down from the mountain of closet duties with a greater hatred, anger, wrath, and indignation against bosom sins, darling sins, the sins of his own temperament, that were once as dear to him as right hands or right eyes, or as Delilah was to Samson, or Herodias to Herod, or Isaac to Abraham, or Joseph to Jacob, then certainly he has had communion with God in those duties. When a man finds his beloved sins, his Delilahs, which, like the prince of devils, command all other sins, fall before his closet duties, as Dagon fell before the ark, or as Goliath fell before David, then assuredly he has had fellowship with God in them. Pliny writes of some families that had secret marks on their bodies, found only in those of that line. Certainly there are no families, no persons, who do not have some sin or sins, some secret marks on their souls, that may in a special way be called theirs. Now when in private duties they find the bent of their hearts, and the purposes, resolutions, and inclinations of their souls, more raised, inflamed, and set against these, they may safely conclude, to their comfort, that they have had communion with God in them. O sirs! No bosom sin is so sweet or profitable that it is worth burning in hell for, or worth being shut out of heaven for. And therefore, in all your+ private duties and services, strive for a communion with God in them that may break the neck and heart of your+ most bosom sins. When Darius fled before Alexander, he threw his heavy crown from his head, so that he might run out of danger the faster. If ever you+ would be safe from eternal danger, throw away your+ golden and your+ silver idols; throw away your+ bosom sins, your+ darling lusts.

And so I have finished the answers to that noble and necessary question that was last proposed.

i. See That the Glory of God Is Your Ultimate End

My ninth advice and counsel is this: in all your+ closet duties, see that your+ ends are right; see that the glory of God is your+ ultimate end, the mark, the center of the target, that you+ have in your+ eye. There is great truth in that old saying, Quod non actibus, sed finibus pensantur officia, “Duties are valued not by their acts but by their ends.”

As the shining sun puts out the light of the fire, so the glory of God must consume all other ends. There may be malum opus in bona materia (a bad work in a good matter), as in Jehu’s zeal. Two things make a good Christian: good actions and good aims. And though a good aim does not make a bad action good, as in Uzzah, yet a bad aim makes a good action bad, as in Jehu, whose justice was approved but his scheming punished. God writes “nothing” over all those services in which men’s ends are not right. “They did not obey Your voice or walk in Your law. They have done nothing of all that You commanded them to do” (Jer 32:23). “All this disaster has come upon us, yet we have not made our prayer before the Lord our God” (Dan 9:13). The Jews were very much in religious duties and services; witness Isa 1:11–15; Isa 58:1–3; Zech 7:5–6. I might produce a hundred more witnesses to confirm it, were it necessary. But because they did not aim at the glory of God in what they did, the Lord writes “nothing” over all their duties and services. It was Ephraim’s folly that he brought forth fruit for himself (Hos 10:1). And it was the Pharisees’ hypocrisy that in all their duties and services they looked to the praise of men. “Truly I tell you+,” says Christ, “they already have their full reward” (Matt 6:1–5). A poor, a pitiful reward indeed! Men who cannot look above the praises of men will be sure to fall short of divine acceptance and of a glorious recompense. Woe to the man who, like Augustus, is ambitious to go off the stage of duty to applause. Peter was not himself when he denied his Lord and cursed himself to gain credit among a cursed crew. If ever you+ would ask and have, speak and succeed, seek and find, see that the glory of the Lord is engraved on all your+ closet duties.

He will be sure to fare best whose heart is set most on glorifying God in all his secret retreats. When God crowns us, He only crowns His own gifts in us; and when we give God the glory of all we do, we only give Him the glory that is due to His name. For it is He, and He alone, who works all our works in us and for us. All closet duties are good or bad according to the mark at which the soul aims. He who makes God the object of closet prayer, but not the end of closet prayer, only loses his prayer and takes pains to undo himself. God will be Alexander or Nemo; He will be all in all, or He will be nothing at all.

Prayers that are not directed to the glory of God never reach the ear of God, nor delight the heart of God, nor will ever be lodged in the bosom of God. The end must be as noble as the means, or else a man may be undone after all his doings. A man’s most glorious actions will at last be found to be only glorious sins if he has made himself, and not the glory of God, the end of those actions.

j. Offer All Your Closet Prayers in Christ’s Name, and in His Alone

My tenth advice and counsel is this: be sure that you+ offer all your+ closet prayers in Christ’s name, and in His alone. “And I will do whatever you+ ask in My name, so that the Father may be glorified in the Son. If you+ ask Me for anything in My name, I will do it” (John 14:13–14). “So that whatever you+ ask the Father in My name, He will give you+” (John 15:16). “Truly, truly, I tell you+, whatever you+ ask the Father in My name, He will give you+. Until now you+ have not asked for anything in My name. Ask and you+ will receive, so that your+ joy may be complete.…In that day you+ will ask in My name. I am not saying that I will ask the Father on your+ behalf” (John 16:23–24, 26). O sirs! This is your+ privilege as well as your+ comfort, that you+ never deal with God except through a mediator. When you+ appear before God, Jesus Christ appears with you+, and He appears for you+. When you+ do invocare, call on, pray, then He does advocare, call out to, advocate. When you+ put up your+ petitions, then He makes intercession for you+. Christ gives you+ a commission to put His name on all your+ requests; and whatever prayer comes up with this name on it, He will procure an answer for it.

In the state of innocence, man could worship God without a mediator; but since sin has made so wide a breach between God and man, God will accept no worship from man except what is offered up by the hand of a mediator. Now this mediator is Christ alone. “For there is one God, and there is one mediator between God and men, the man Christ Jesus” (1 Tim 2:5). One mediator, not only of redemption, as the papists grant, but also of intercession, which they deny.

The papists make saints and angels co-mediators with Christ; but in this, as in other things, they fight against the clear light of Scripture. The apostle plainly tells us that the office of intercession belongs to Christ as part of His mediation (Heb 7:25). And it is certain that we need no other officer to present our requests in heaven but the man Christ Jesus, who, being so near to the Father, and so dear to the Father, and so much in favor with the Father, can doubtless win anything from the Father that makes for His glory and our good. This was typified in the law. The high priest alone entered the sanctuary and carried the names of the children of Israel before the Lord, while the people all stood outside. This pointed to Christ’s mediation (Exod 28:29). In Leviticus 16:13–14, you+ read of two things: first, the cloud of incense that covered the mercy-seat; secondly, the blood of the bull that was sprinkled before the mercy-seat. Now that blood typified Christ’s satisfaction, and the cloud of incense His intercession.

Some of the learned think that Christ intercedes only by virtue of His merits; others, that He does it only with His mouth. I conjecture it may be done both ways, all the more because Christ has a tongue, as well as a whole body, though glorified, in heaven. And is it likely that the mouth which pleaded so much for us on earth should be altogether silent for us in heaven?

There is no coming to the Father except through the Son (John 14:6). Christ is the true Jacob’s ladder by which we must ascend to heaven. Joseph, you+ know, commanded his brothers, if ever they hoped for any good from him or to see his face with joy, to be sure to bring their brother Benjamin with them. O sirs! If ever you+ would prevail with God, if ever you+ would have sweet, choice, and comforting returns from heaven to all your+ closet prayers, be sure that you+ bring your+ elder brother, the Lord Jesus Christ, in the arms of your+ faith. Be sure that you+ deal and trade with God only in the name of the Lord Jesus.

It is a notable saying that Luther has on the 130th Psalm: “Often and willingly,” he says, “do I drive this home: that you+ should shut your+ eyes and your+ ears, and say you+ know no God outside Christ, none but Him who was in the lap of Mary and nursed at her breasts.” Dulce nomen Christi (Sweet is the name of Christ). He means none outside Him. When you+ go to closet prayer, see that you+ do not pray in your+ own names, but in the name of Christ; and that you+ do not plead in your+ own names, but in the name of Christ; and that you+ do not believe and hope in your+ own names, but in the name of Christ; and that you+ do not look to succeed in your+ own names, but in the name of Christ. “And whatever you+ do, in word or deed, do it all in the name of the Lord Jesus” (Col 3:17). Whatever we do, we are to do it by the authority of Christ, and through the help of Christ, and in the name of Christ, and for the sake and glory of Christ.

Christ’s name is so precious and powerful with the Father that it will win any suit and obtain any request from Him. Jesus, in the Chinese language, means the rising sun. When a man writes the name of Jesus on his closet prayers, he will be sure to succeed. Though God will not give a man a drop, a sip, a crumb, a crust, for his own sake, yet for Jesus’ sake He will give the best, the choicest, and the greatest blessings that heaven affords. That name is always mighty and powerful, prevailing and precious before the Lord. The prayers that were offered up with the incense on the altar were pleasing (Rev 8:3) and rose up and were accepted (v. 4). Joseph’s brothers were treated kindly for Benjamin’s sake.

O sirs! All our duties and services are accepted by the Father, not for their own sakes, nor for our sakes, but for Christ’s sake. No prayers are heard, acknowledged, accepted, regarded, or rewarded except those that Christ puts His hand to. If Christ does not mingle His blood with our sacrifices, our services, they will be lost, and will never ascend as incense before the Lord. No coin will pass that does not have Caesar’s stamp on it; and no prayers will pass in heaven that do not have the stamp of Christ on them. Nothing is more pleasing to our heavenly Father than for us to use the mediation of His Son. Those who always present themselves before the Father with Christ in their arms will be sure to find the most favor and to fare best in the court of heaven.

k. Closely Watch What Becomes of Your Private Prayers

My eleventh and last advice and counsel is this: when you+ come out of your+ closets, closely watch what becomes of your+ private prayers. Look to see at what door, in what way, and by what hand the Lord will be pleased to give you+ an answer to the secret desires of your+ souls in a corner. It has been the custom of the people of God to follow up on their prayers, to see what success they have had, to observe what reception they have found in heaven.

“In the morning, O Lord, You hear my voice; in the morning I will direct my prayer to You and will look up” (Ps 5:3). In these words you+ may observe two things: first, David’s posture in prayer; secondly, his practice after prayer. First, his posture in prayer: I will “direct my prayer to You.” Secondly, his practice after prayer: I “will look up.” The prophet in these words makes use of two military words. First, he would not only pray, but marshal his prayers; he would put them in battle array. That is what the Hebrew word gnarach, translated “direct,” conveys. Secondly, when he had done this, he would be like a spy on his watchtower, to see whether he prevailed, whether he won the day or not. That is what the Hebrew word tsaphah, translated “look up,” conveys. When David had set his prayers, his petitions, in rank and file, in good array, he was resolved to look out, to look about him, to see at what door God would send in an answer to prayer. He is either a fool or a madman, either very weak or very wicked, who prays and prays but never looks to see what becomes of his prayers, who shoots many an arrow toward heaven but never watches where his arrows land.

“I will listen to what God the Lord will say; for He will surely speak peace to His people and His saints” (Ps 85:8). If David would have God listen to his prayers, he must then listen to what God will speak; and on this point it seems he was fully resolved. You+ have the prophet’s prayer in the first seven verses of this psalm, and his gracious resolution in the eighth verse: “I will listen to what God the Lord will say.” As if he had said, “Certainly it will not be long before the Lord gives me a gracious answer, a timely and a fitting return to my present prayers.”

“Out of the depths I cry to You, O Lord! O Lord, hear my voice; let Your ears be attentive to my plea for mercy.…I wait for the Lord my soul does wait, and in His word I put my hope. My soul waits for the Lord more than watchmen wait for the morning—more than watchmen wait for the morning” (Ps 130:1–2, 5–6). Those who keep watch outside in dangerous times and wearisome weather look often for the first light of day. How the weary sentry who is wet with the rain of heaven or with the dew of the night waits and watches, looks and longs, for the morning light! Now this was the frame and disposition of David’s spirit when he came away from praying: he sets himself to wait for a gracious answer. Shall the farmer wait for the precious fruits of the earth, and shall the merchant wait for the return of his ships, and shall the wife wait for the return of her husband who has gone on a long journey, and shall a Christian not wait for the return of his prayers (Jas 5:7–8)? As Noah patiently waited for the return of the dove to the ark with an olive branch in its mouth, so you+ must patiently wait for the return of your+ prayers. When children shoot their arrows, they never watch where they fall; but when prudent archers shoot their arrows up into the air, they stand and watch where they fall. You+ must deal with your+ prayers as prudent archers do with their arrows.

“I will stand at my guard post and station myself on the tower. I will watch to see what He will say to me” (Hab 2:1). The prophet, having been very earnest in his complaints to God and very fervent in his supplications in the chapter before, now gets up on his watchtower to see what becomes of his prayers. He stands as a sentry and watches as vigilantly and as carefully as a spy, a scout, earnestly longing to hear and see the outcome, the issue, and the success of his prayers.

The Christian who in prayer has one eye on a divine precept and another on a gracious promise will be sure to look after his prayers. He who prays and waits, and waits and prays, will be sure to succeed. He will never fail of rich returns (Ps 40:1–4). He who can go without as well as wait, and who can be content that God is glorified though he himself is not gratified; he who dares not run ahead of God’s promises, but patiently waits for their fulfillment, may be confident that he will have timely and fitting answers to all the prayers he has sent off to heaven. Though God seldom comes at our time, yet He never fails to come at His own time. “He who is coming will come and will not delay” (Heb 10:37). The mercies of God are not called “the swift,” but “the sure” mercies of David (Isa 55:3). He who makes it as much a matter of conscience to look after his prayers as to pray will shortly clap his hands for joy and cry out with that blessed martyr, “He has come, Austin, He has come, He has come.”

Certainly there is little worth in the heart or in the prayers of the man who keeps up a trade of prayer but never looks to see what becomes of his prayers. When you+ are in your+ closets, marshal your+ prayers. See that every prayer keeps its place and ground; and when you+ come out of your+ closets, then look up for an answer. Only take care that you+ are not too hasty and impatient with God. Though the mercy in the promise is yours+, yet the time of giving it out is the Lord’s. And therefore you+ must wait as well as pray.

And so much by way of counsel and advice for carrying on closet prayer better.

4. Means, Rules, and Directions

I have now only one thing more to do before I close this discourse, and that is to lay down some means, rules, or directions that may help you+ on in a faithful and conscientious discharge of this great duty of closet prayer. And so, as follows.

a. Beware of an Idle and Slothful Spirit

First, if ever you+ would give yourselves+ up to private prayer, beware of an idle and slothful spirit. If Adam in the state of innocence had to work and tend the garden, and if after his Fall, when he was monarch of all the world, he still had to labor, why should anyone be idle or slothful? Idleness is a sin against the law of creation. God created man to labor, and so the idle person violates this law of creation; for by his idleness he casts off the authority of his Creator, Who made him for labor. Idleness contradicts the principles of our creation. Man in innocence would have been free from weariness, but not from work; he was to tend the garden by divine appointment. “Then the Lord God took the man and placed him in the Garden of Eden to tend and keep it” (Gen 2:15). All weariness in labor, and all vexing, tiring, and tormenting labor, came in by the Fall. “By the sweat of your brow you will eat your bread” (Gen 3:19). The bread of idleness is neither sweet nor sure. “An idle soul will suffer hunger,” says Solomon (Prov 19:15). An idle life and a holy heart are very far apart. “By doing nothing,” says the heathen, “men learn to do evil things.” It is easy to slip out of an idle life into an evil and wicked life. Indeed, an idle life is evil in itself, for man was made to be active, not to be idle. The Cyclops thought man’s happiness consisted in nihil agendo, in doing nothing, but no excellent thing can be the child of idleness. Idleness is a mother-sin, a breeding-sin. It is pulvinar diaboli, the devil’s cushion, on which he sits, and the devil’s anvil, on which he forges very great and very many sins (Eph 4:28; 2 Thess 3:10, 12). Just as toads and serpents breed most in standing waters, so sin thrives most in idle persons. Idleness is what provokes the Lord to forsake men’s bodies and the devil to possess their souls.

No man has fewer means to preserve his body and more temptations to infect his soul than an idle person. Oh, shake off sloth! The sluggish Christian will be sleeping, or idling, or trifling, when he should be in his closet praying. Sloth is the wasting sickness of the soul; get it cured, or it will be your+ eternal ruin. Of all devils, it is the idle devil that keeps men out of their closets most. Nothing gives the devil so much advantage against us as idleness. It was good counsel that Jerome gave to his friend, Facito aliquid operis, ut te semper Diabolus inveniat occupatum, “Do some work so that the devil always finds you busy,” so that when the devil comes with a temptation you may tell him that you are not at leisure.

Mr. Greenham, a famous and painstaking preacher of this nation, told how a poor soul whom the devil tempted came to him for advice on how she might resist the temptation, and he gave her this answer: “Never be idle, but be always well employed. For I have found it in my own experience: when the devil came to tempt me, I told him that I was not at leisure to listen to his temptations; and by this means I resisted all his assaults.” Idleness is the hour of temptation; and an idle person is the devil’s tennis ball, tossed by him as he pleases.

“He who labors,” said the old hermit, “is tempted by only one devil, but he who is idle is assaulted by all.” Cupid complained that he could never fasten on the Muses, because he could never find them idle. The fowler bends his bow and spreads his net for birds when they are perched, not when they are on the wing. So Satan shoots his most fiery darts at men when they are most idle and slothful. And the Sodomites found this by woeful experience (Ezek 16:49), when God rained hell out of heaven on them, both for their idleness and for those other sins of theirs to which their idleness exposed them.

It was said of Rome that during the time of their wars with Carthage and other enemies in Africa, they did not know what vice meant; but no sooner had they won the victory than they came to ruin through idleness. Idleness is a sin not only against the law of grace, but also against the light of nature. You+ cannot look any way without every created thing rebuking and reproaching your+ idleness and sloth. If you+ look up to the heavens, there you+ will find all their glorious lights constant in their motions. The sun is “like a champion rejoicing to run his course” (Ps 19:5; 104:23). The winds blow, the waters run, the earth brings forth her pleasant and delightful fruits. All the fish in the sea, the birds in the air, and the beasts in the fields and on the mountains have their movements and workings, and all of them call aloud to man not to be idle, but active.

Solomon sends the sluggard to the ant to learn industry (Prov 6:6). The ant is a very little creature, but exceedingly hard-working. Nature has put an instinct into her to be very busy and active all the summer; she is at it early and late, and will not lose an hour unless the weather hinders her. And the prophet Jeremiah sends the Jews to school to learn to wait and to keep their times from the stork, the turtledove, the crane, and the swallow (Jer 8:7). And our Savior sends us to the sparrows and lilies to learn to wait on providence (Matt 6:26, 28). And let me send you+ to the busy bee to learn activity and industry. Though the bee is little in size, yet it is great in service. She flies far, searches the fields, hedges, trees, orchards, and gardens, and loads herself with honey and wax, and then returns to her hive.

Now how the activity of these creatures should make the idle person blush! Oh, sirs! Man is the noblest creature, into whom God has put principles of the greatest activity, as being capable of the greatest and highest enjoyments. And therefore idleness is a forgetting of man’s dignity, a forsaking of the rank in which God has set him, and a debasing of himself below the least and lowest creatures, which constantly, in their order, obediently serve the law of their creation. Indeed, if you+ look up to the blessed angels above you+, you+ will always find them active and serviceable. “Are they not all ministering spirits sent to serve those who will inherit salvation?” (Heb 1:14). And if you+ look down to the angels of darkness below you+, oh, how laborious and industrious they are to destroy and damn your+ precious and immortal souls (1 Pet 5:8)!

In closing, remember that idleness is so great an evil that it has been condemned and severely punished by the very worst of men. Among the Egyptians, idleness was a capital crime. Among the Lucans, he who lent money to an idle person had to forfeit it. By Solon’s law, idle persons were to be put to death. And Seneca would rather be sick than idle. The Lacedaemonians called men to account for their idle hours. Among the Corinthians, idle persons were handed over to the carnifex, the executioner.

Antoninus Pius, when he was emperor, had the roofs and coverings taken off all such houses as were known to take in idle people. He declared that nothing was more unseemly, or more absurd to be allowed, than for such idle caterpillars and slow-worms to have their food and nourishment from a commonwealth to whose maintenance their industry and labor added nothing. All of this should steel us and arm us against sloth and idleness. I have dwelt the longer on this because there is no greater hindrance to closet prayer than sloth and idleness. Slothful and idle persons commonly lie in bed so long, and spend so much precious time between the comb and the mirror, and in eating, drinking, sporting, and trifling, etc., that they can find no time for private prayer. Certainly, those who would rather go sleeping to hell than sweating to heaven will never care much for closet prayer. And therefore shun sloth and idleness as you+ would shun a lion in the way, poison in your+ food, or coals in your+ bosom, or else you+ will never find time to wait on God in your+ closets.

b. Beware of Spending Your Precious Time on Side Issues

Secondly, beware of spending too much of your+ precious time on side issues, on the little things of religion, such as “mint, dill, and cumin” (Matt 23:23); or in searching into the circumstances of worship, or in standing stoutly for this or that ceremony, and meanwhile neglecting the study of the covenant of grace; or in inquiring what fruit it was that Adam ate in Paradise, or in inquiring after the authors of such and such books, whose names God in His infinite wisdom has concealed, or in inquiring what God did before the world was made. When someone asked Augustine that question, he answered that God was preparing hell for such busy questioners as he was. It was a saying of Luther, “From a vainglorious teacher, from a contentious pastor, and from unprofitable questions, the good Lord deliver His church.”

It is one of Satan’s great designs to hinder men in the great and weighty duties of religion by keeping them busiest about the lowest and least matters of religion. Satan is never better pleased than when he sees Christians puzzled and perplexed about things in religion that are of no great moment or importance (Col 2:21). Those who deal and trade in religion more for a good name than for a good life, more for a good report than for a good conscience, more to please others than to honor God, etc., will take no pleasure in closet duties. Those who are busier about ceremonies than about substance, about the form of godliness than about the power (2 Tim 3:5), will never make it their business to be much with God in their closets, as is evident in the scribes and Pharisees (Matt 6:1–6). Those who are more taken up with the outward dress and style of religion than with the spirit, power, and life of religion will never care to drive a secret trade heavenward (Luke 11:34–40). There cannot be a surer or a greater mark of a hypocrite than to make a great stir about little things in religion, and meanwhile to neglect the great and main things in religion. Such people as these have all along in Scripture shown an aloofness and a complete carelessness toward closet duties. I never knew any man hot and zealous about side issues, about the little things of religion, who was ever famous for closet prayer.

c. Beware of Prying Curiosity in Searching Dark and Abstruse Truths

Thirdly, beware of prying curiosity, and of spending too much of your+ precious time in searching into the dark, abstruse, mysterious, and hidden truths and things of God and religion that lie furthest from the understanding of the best and wisest of men. Curiosity is the spiritual adultery of the soul. Curiosity is a spiritual drunkenness. For just as the drunkard is never satisfied unless he sees the bottom of the cup, however deep it is, so those who are troubled with the itch of curiosity will say they can never be satisfied until they come to the bottom of the deepest and most profound things of God. They love to pry into God’s secrets, and to examine the mysteries of religion by their weak, shallow reason, and to be wise above what is written. Curious searchers into the deep, mysterious things of God will make all God’s depths into shallows rather than be thought unable to fathom them by the short line of their own reason.

Oh, that men would at last learn to be contentedly ignorant where God would not have them know! Oh, that men were humble enough to count it no disgrace to acknowledge some depths in God and in the blessed Scripture which their shallow reason cannot fathom! They are only a company of utter fools who aim to know more than God would have them know. Did not Adam’s tree of knowledge make him and his descendants sheer fools? He who goes to school to his own reason has a fool for his schoolmaster. The quickest way to go stark blind is to be always prying and gazing at the body of the sun; so the quickest way to spiritual blindness is to be always prying into the most secret and hidden things of God (Deut 29:29). Are there not many who, by prying long into the secrets of nature, have become archenemies of the grace of God (Rom 9:20)? Oh, that we were wise enough to wonder at the deep mysteries which we cannot understand, and to adore the depths and counsels which we cannot reach (Rom 11:33)!

Oh, let us check our curiosity in the things of God, and sit down satisfied and content to trace many of God’s actions back to hidden causes, which lie secret in the abyss of His eternal knowledge and infallible will. Christ, when He was on earth, very frequently, severely, and sharply condemned prying inquirers, as is evident from these scriptures (John 21:22; Acts 1:6–7; Luke 13:23–24). And the great reason our Savior so frequently checked this habit of curiosity was that the great indulgers of it were too often neglecters of the greater, necessary, and important points of religion.

Curiosity is one of Satan’s most dangerous devices, by which he keeps many souls out of their closets, indeed, out of heaven. When many a poor soul begins in good earnest to look toward heaven and to apply himself to closet duties, then Satan begins to stir himself, and to labor with all his might to keep the poor soul so busy with vain inquiries, curious speculations, and unprofitable curiosities that the soul has no time for closet prayer. Ah! How well it might have gone with many a man, had he spent in closet prayer only a quarter of the time that he has spent in curious inquiries after things that have not been essential to his happiness!

The heathen priests aimed at curious subtleties; they had their mythologies and strange jargon about their imaginary, inaccessible deities, to astonish and bewilder their blind, superstitious followers, and so to hold up their popish and apish idolatries in greater veneration. Oh, that there were none of this heathenish spirit among many in these days who have their faces toward heaven! Ah! How many there are who busy themselves more in searching out the reasons why man’s Fall could not be recovered than they do in recovering themselves out of their fallen state! Ah, how many there are who busy themselves more about the apostasy of the angels than they do about making sure of their share in Christ! And what a great deal of precious time some have spent in finding out the natures, distinctions, properties, and orders of angels!

That high-soaring, counterfeit Dionysius describes the hierarchy of angels as exactly as if he had lived among them. He says there are nine orders of them, which he grounds on nine words found partly in the Old Testament and partly in the New: seraphim, cherubim, thrones, powers, hosts, dominions, principalities, archangels, and angels. And he describes at length their separate natures, distinctions, and properties: that the first three orders are for immediate attendance on the Almighty, the next three orders for the general government of created things, and the last three orders for the particular good of God’s elect; that the archangel surpasses the beauty of the angels ten times, the principalities surpass the archangels twenty times, and the powers surpass the principalities forty times, etc. How he came by this learning is not known, and yet this hierarchy in these nine separate orders has passed as genuine through many ages of the church.

The Platonists were the first who divided the angels into three orders: some above heaven, called supercoelestes; others in heaven, called coelestes; and others under heaven, called subcoelestes and they assign them various offices accordingly, as follows:

First, those above heaven (I mean this visible heaven) continually stand before God, as they say, praising, lauding, and magnifying His name.

Secondly, those in heaven are placed there to move, rule, and govern the stars.

Thirdly, of those under heaven, some rule kingdoms, others provinces, others cities, others particular men.

Various Christian writers who have written on the hierarchy of angels follow these opinions. Now, if we were to take these guesses for real truths, it would follow that the highest angels do not minister to the saints, but only and directly to God Himself, which is expressly contrary to various scriptures, as you+ may see from these, among others (Jude 9; Luke 1:19, 26; Zech 4:10; Rev 5:6; Heb 1:14). When I was preaching on the ministry of the blessed angels, I proved then in several sermons, as some of you+ may remember, that all the angels in heaven were commanded and commissioned by God to be serviceable and useful to the heirs of salvation. “Are they not all ministering spirits sent to serve those who will inherit salvation?” (Heb 1:14).

The devil knows he is no loser, and the curious soul only a very little gainer, if he can only persuade him to spend most of his precious time in studying and poring over the darkest, most mysterious, and hidden things of God. He who would rather read the Revelation of John than John’s plain epistles, or Daniel’s prophecies than David’s psalms, and is busier reconciling difficult scriptures than mortifying unruly lusts, or who is set more on vain speculations than on the things that make most for edification, is not the man who is cut out for closet prayer.

Those who aim at lofty notions and obscure expressions, and are men of abstract fancies, are only a company of wise fools who will never take any delight in being with God in a corner. Had many men spent in secret prayer only half the time that they have spent in seeking after the philosopher’s stone, how happy they might have been! Oh, how holy, how happy, how heavenly, how humble, how wise, how knowing many men might have been, had they spent in closet prayer only half the time that they have spent in searching after the things that are “hard to understand” (2 Pet 3:16)!

d. Beware of Engaging in a Crowd of Worldly Affairs

Fourthly, beware of involving yourselves+ in a crowd of worldly affairs. Many have so much to do on earth that they have no time to look up to heaven. As much earth puts out the fire, so much worldly business puts out the fire of heavenly affections.
