Just as the earth swallowed up Korah, Dathan, and Abiram (Num 16:32), so much worldly business swallows up so much precious time that many men have no leisure to be with God in their closets. “This business has to be done, and that business cannot be left undone, and the other necessary matter must be attended to, so that I have no leisure to step out of my shop into my closet,” says the earthly-minded man (Phil 3:19).

So a crowd of worldly affairs crowds closet prayer right out of doors. Many drive so great a trade in their shops that their private trade with heaven is laid aside altogether. Nothing has kept men from Christ and closet prayer more than the shop, the exchange, the farm, and the oxen (Luke 14:16–22). The stars which have the smallest circuit are nearest the pole; and the men who are least entangled with worldly business are commonly nearest to God, to Christ, to heaven, and so the fitter for closet prayer. It is sad when men grasp so much business that they can have no leisure for communion with God in a corner. The noise in a mill is such that it hinders all private conversation between man and man; and so a multitude of worldly affairs makes such a noise that it hinders all private fellowship between God and the soul. If a man of much business should now and then slip into his closet, yet his head and his heart will be so filled and distracted with thoughts of his work that God will have little of him but his bodily presence, or at most only bodily exercise, which profits little (1 Tim 4:8). If Christ blamed Martha for the multitude of her household tasks (Luke 10:40–42), though they were undertaken directly to serve and welcome Him, because they hindered her in the concerns of her soul, oh, how He will one day blame all those who, by running themselves into a crowd of worldly affairs, cut themselves off from all opportunities of pouring out their souls before Him in secret!

e. Beware of Secret Sins

Fifthly, beware of secret sins. There is no greater hindrance to secret prayer in all the world than secret sins; therefore stand on your+ guard, and arm yourselves+ with all your+ might against them. There is an antipathy between secret sinning and secret praying. It comes partly from guilt, which makes the soul shy of coming under God’s secret eye, and partly from the fears, doubts, disputes, and disorders that secret sins raise in the heart. Light is not more opposite to darkness, Christ to Belial, or heaven to hell, than secret prayer is to secret sins. And therefore, whatever you+ do, see that you+ keep clear of secret sins. To that end consider these four things:

1. God Is Privy to Our Most Secret Sins

First, that God is privy to our most secret sins. His eye is as much on secret sins as it is on open sins. “You have set our iniquities before You, our secret sins in the light of Your presence” (Ps 90:8). God has an eye on our inmost evils; He sees all that is done in the dark. “Can a man hide in secret places where I cannot see him? declares the Lord. Do I not fill the heavens and the earth? declares the Lord” (Jer 23:24). “The eyes of the Lord are in every place, observing the evil and the good” (Prov 15:3). To say that God does not see the most secret sins of the children of men detracts not only from His omniscience but also from His mercy; for how can God pardon sins which He does not see to be sins? There is no cloud, nor curtain, nor moment of darkness, that can stand between the eyes of God and the ways of men. “A man’s ways are before the eyes of the Lord, and the Lord examines all his paths” (Prov 5:21). In this scripture Solomon speaks mainly of the ways of the adulterer, which are usually plotted with the most cunning secrecy; yet God sees all those ways. Just as no boldness can exempt the adulterer from the justice of God, so no secrecy can hide him from the eye of God. Though men labor to hide their ways from others and from themselves, yet it is only labor in vain to try to hide them from God. Men who labor to hide God from themselves can never hide themselves from God.

I have read that Paphnutius converted Thais and Ephron, two famous prostitutes, from their uncleanness with only this argument, “That God sees all things in the dark, when the doors are fast, the windows shut, and the curtains drawn.” “Nothing in all creation is hidden from God’s sight; everything is naked and laid open (anatomized) before the eyes of Him to whom we must give account” (Heb 4:13). It is an allusion to the priests under the law: when they killed a beast, all that was within the beast was laid open and naked before the priest, so that he might see what was sound and what was corrupted. Though evil is done out of the sight of all the world, yet it is naked and laid open in the sight of Him to whom we must give account.

The sins which lie closest and lurk most secretly in the heart are as obvious and odious to God as those most plainly written on a man’s forehead. God is πανόφθαλμος, “all eye,” so that He sees all the most secret turnings and windings of our hearts. Our most secret sins are as plainly seen by Him as anything can be by us at noonday. “If I say, ‘Surely the darkness will hide me, and the light become night around me’—even the darkness is not dark to You, but the night shines like the day, for darkness is as light to You” (Ps 139:11–12). Not even the thickest clouds can bar out the observation of Him Whose eyes fill heaven and earth. What is the curtain, or the darkest night, or the double lock, or the secret room, to Him Who clearly observes all things in perfect nakedness? God has an eye on the most inward intentions of the heart and the subtlest stirrings of the spirit. Those philosophers were wrong who held that the eye and care of God reached no lower than the heavens. Certainly there is not a creature, not a thought, not a thing, but lies open to the all-seeing eye of God. The Lord knows our secret sinnings as exactly as our visible sinnings. “He knows the secrets of the heart” (Ps 44:21). Would not a criminal at the bar speak truly, if he knew, if he believed, that the judge had windows that looked into his breast?

Athenodorus, a heathen, could say that all men ought to be careful in the actions of their life, because God was everywhere and saw all that was done.

Zeno, a wise heathen, affirmed that God saw even the thoughts.

It was an excellent saying of Ambrose, “If you cannot hide yourself from the sun, which is God’s minister of light, how impossible it will be to hide yourself from Him Whose eyes are ten thousand times brighter than the sun!” Though a sinner may outwit his conscience, yet he cannot outwit the eye of God’s omniscience. Oh, that poor souls would remember that, as they are never out of the reach of God’s hand, so they are never out from under the view of His eye! God is totus oculus, “all eye.” “For My eyes are on all their ways. They are not hidden from My face, and their guilt is not concealed from My eyes” (Jer 16:17). “For His eyes are on the ways of a man, and He sees his every step. There is no darkness or deep shadow where the workers of iniquity can hide” (Job 34:21–22). “Your eyes are on all the ways of the sons of men, to reward each one according to his ways and according to the fruit of his deeds” (Jer 32:19).

You+ know what Ahasuerus, that great monarch, said concerning Haman, when he came in and found him thrown down on the couch on which the queen sat. “What?” he says, “Would he actually assault the queen … before me in the house?” (Esth 7:8). The deadly emphasis was in the words “before me.” “Would he assault the queen … before me?” What, will he dare to commit such villainy while I stand and look on? Oh, sirs! To sin in the sight of God, to do wickedly under the eye of God, is a thing that He regards as the greatest affront and the highest indignity that can possibly be done to Him. What, He says, will you be drunk before Me? Will you swear and blaspheme before Me? Will you be wanton and unclean before Me? Will you be unjust and unrighteous under My eye? Will you profane My Sabbaths and pollute My ordinances before My face? Will you despise and persecute My servants in My presence?

This, then, is what gives all sin its deadly weight: it is done before the face of God, it is committed in the royal presence of the King of kings—whereas the very consideration of God’s omnipresence should nobly arm us against sin and Satan. The consideration of His all-seeing eye should make us shun all occasions of sin and make us shy of all appearances of sin. Shall the eye of the master keep the schoolboy from blotting his copybook? Shall the eye of the judge keep the criminal from pilfering and stealing? Shall the eye of the master keep the servant from idling and trifling? Shall the eye of the father keep the child from wandering and roaming? Shall the eye of the husband keep the wife from excesses and indecencies? Shall the sharp eye of wise Cato, or the keen eye of a near neighbor, or the severe eye of a bosom friend keep you from many enormities and vanities? And shall the strict, the pure, the jealous eye of an all-seeing God not keep you from sinning in the secret room, when all the curtains are drawn, the doors bolted, and everyone in the house in bed or away from home but you and your Delilah? Oh, what dreadful atheism is bound up in the heart of the man who is more afraid of the eye of his father, his pastor, his child, his servant, than he is of the eye, the presence, of the eternal God! Oh, that all whom this concerns would take such serious notice of it that they would judge themselves severely for it, mourn bitterly over it, and strive mightily in prayer with God both for the pardon of it and for power against it!

The apostle sadly complains of some in his time who wallowed in secret sins. “For it is shameful even to mention what the disobedient do in secret” (Eph 5:12). He speaks of those who lived in secret fornication and uncleanness. There were many who had put on a form of godliness and yet indulged themselves in the secret practice of abominable wickedness and filthiness, as if there were no God to see them, no conscience to accuse them, no Judgment Day to arraign them, no justice to condemn them, and no hell to torment them. Oh! How infinitely odious in the eyes of a holy God must those be who can court and flatter Him highly in public, and yet are so bold as to provoke Him to His face in private! They are like those whores who make a great show of affection and respect for their husbands in public, and yet at home will play the harlot before their husbands’ eyes.

Some perform religious duties only to cloak and color over their secret filthiness, their secret wickedness; some pretend to pay their vows, and yet wait for the twilight (Prov 7:13–15; Job 24:15); some commit wickedness in a corner, and yet with the harlot wipe their mouths and say, “What have we done?” Such people will at last find the rooms, the stone out of the wall, the beam out of the timber, the seats they sit on, and the beds they lie on, witnessing against all their wanton caresses and lewd behavior in secret (Hab 2:11). “God will judge the sexually immoral and adulterers” (Heb 13:4). He will sentence them Himself. And why, but because such sinners carry on so secretly and craftily that often no one but God can find them out? Magistrates often neglect to punish such sinners even when their secret wickedness is made known; and therefore God Himself will sit in judgment on them. Though they may escape the eyes of men, yet they will never escape the judgment of God. Iniquities of the heart do not come under any human sentence. The sexually immoral and adulterers are usually marvelously close, secretive, and cunning in concealing their abominable filthiness; therefore the harlot is said to be subtle of heart (Prov 7:10). Rabbi Solomon translates the Hebrew munito corde, “having her heart fenced.” “For,” he says, “as a city is surrounded with fortifications, so her heart is fortified all around with subtlety.” Or else it may be rendered occlusa corde, “fast shut up in the heart, as close as a besieged city,” that is, “most secret in the subtlety of her heart, however open she may be in the boldness of her outward behavior.” So the prophet Agur counts the way of a man with a maiden, and the way of an adulterous woman, among the things which neither he himself nor any other man could possibly trace and find out, and compares it to the way of three things which no mind or industry of man is able to make out. But yet God sees all, and will bring them to the bar for all (Prov 30:19–20).

2. Secret Sins Shall Be Revealed

Secondly, consider that secret sins will be revealed. The most hidden works of darkness will be openly made manifest, for though sin is done in the dark, yet sin will be judged in the light. “For there is nothing secret that will not be disclosed, and nothing concealed that will not be made known and brought to light” (Luke 8:17). The slanders of the Jews about the magical arts of Christ and His apostles, and the horrible lies of the pagans about the incestuous unions of the Christians and their drinking of man’s blood, were in time exposed for what they were. “For God will bring every work into judgment, along with every secret thing, whether good or evil” (Eccl 12:14). Note that he does not say “some work,” but “every work”; and not only works, but secrets; and not only secrets, but every secret; and not only secret good things but evil too. Whether good works or ill works, whether secret or open, all must be brought to judgment. The books of God’s omniscience and of man’s conscience will then be opened; and then secret sins will be as legible on your forehead as if they were written with the most glittering sunbeams on a wall of crystal. All men’s secret sins are printed in heaven, and God will at last read them aloud in the ears of all the world. “Judge nothing before the appointed time; wait until the Lord comes. He will bring to light what is hidden in darkness and will expose the motives of men’s hearts” (1 Cor 4:5).

Just as there is a world of flies and specks of dust in the air which we never see until the sun shines, so there are many thousands upon thousands of proud thoughts, and unclean thoughts, and worldly thoughts, and malicious thoughts, and envious thoughts, and bloody thoughts, which the world does not see and does not know. But on the Great Day, when the motives of all hearts shall be exposed, then all shall come out, then all shall appear, both to the upper and the lower world. On that great day all masks, visors, and hoods shall be pulled off, and then all shall come out. All that you have ever done in the secret room, in the dark corner, shall be made known to men and angels, yes, to the whole court of heaven and to all the rest of the world. “On the day when God will judge men’s secrets through Christ Jesus” (Rom 2:16). On this Great Day, God will judge not only our words but our works, not only our open works but also our secret works and ways. When Jehoiakim was dead, the characters, superstitious marks, and prints of his sorcery were found on his body (2 Chr 36:8). This shows how deeply idolatry was rooted in his heart, seeing he bore its marks in his flesh during his life. Being a king, he carried it off boldly and kept everything hidden; but when he was dead, then all came out, then the marks of his abominable idolatry appeared on his body. Sinners, even the greatest of sinners, may hide and keep secret their horrid abominations for a time; yet a time will come when all shall come out, when all their secret marks and secret abominations shall be plain to all the world.

But sinners may be ready to object and say, “Just leave us alone in our secret sins until that day, and then we will be well enough off.” And therefore,

3. God Many Times Reveals Secret Sins Even in This Life

In the third place, consider that God many times, even in this life, reveals and makes known to the world men’s secret sins (Isa 41:21–23).

God loves to act in keeping with His own names. Now, to be “a revealer of secrets” is one of His names (Dan 2:47); and accordingly, even in this world, He often brings to light the most hidden things of darkness.

Of all the glorious attributes of God, there is none that suffers so deeply by secret sins as the attribute of His omniscience. And therefore, in this world God often stands up to vindicate the honor of that attribute by unmasking sinners and by bringing to light all those secret paths and ways of wickedness in which they have long walked undetected. It was for the honor of this blessed attribute of God that the secretly plotted sin of Ananias and Sapphira was so openly revealed (Acts 5:1–12). “And great fear came over the whole church and all who heard about these events” (Acts 5:11).

For a long time Joseph’s brothers hid their malice, their cunning, their cruelty, their envy, their treachery in selling their brother into Egypt; but at last, by amazing and bewildering providences, all was brought to light (Gen 42:21–22; 50:15–22). Conscience may seem to be asleep for a time, yet in time it will awake and make the sinner know that it is as faithful in recording as it is terrible in accusing; and Joseph’s brothers learned this by sad experience.

So with Gehazi: he sins secretly, he lies dreadfully, and after all he defends it boldly. But at last it all comes out, and instead of being richly clothed, he and his descendants were clothed with leprosy forever. And instead of two sets of garments, God hangs them up in chains as a monument of His wrath to all generations (2 Kgs 5:20ff).

So Achan secretly and sacrilegiously steals a fine Babylonian garment, and two hundred shekels of silver, and a wedge of gold weighing fifty shekels, and hides them in the ground in the middle of his tent; and because of this, Israel flees before its enemies. But at last Achan is taken, and it all comes out: his golden wedge proved a wedge to split him, and his Babylonian garment a garment to shroud him (Josh 7). Joshua makes a bonfire of all that he had secretly and sinfully stolen, and in it burns him and his children and all that he had. Oh, how openly, how severely, God sometimes punishes men for their most secret iniquity!

You+ may see the same in that great instance of David. “Why then have you despised the command of the Lord by doing evil in His sight? You put Uriah the Hittite to the sword (this was done in a secret letter) and took his wife as your own … Now, therefore, the sword will never depart from your house, because you have despised Me and have taken the wife of Uriah the Hittite to be your own. This is what the Lord says: ‘I will raise up adversity against you from your own house. Before your very eyes I will take your wives and give them to another, and he will lie with them in broad daylight. You have acted in secret, but I will do this thing in broad daylight before all Israel’” (2 Sam 12:9–12; 16:22). David was very intent and very industrious to hide his sin and to save his reputation, but the covering made of Uriah’s blood was too short and too narrow to hide his folly with Bathsheba. And therefore, when he had done all he could, his sin was tossed like a ball from man to man through court, city, and country.

I have read of one Parthenius, a murderer, treasurer to Theodobert king of France, who had treacherously slain a special friend of his called Ausanius, with his wife Papianilla. When no man suspected or accused him of it, he exposed and accused himself in this strange manner. As he slept in his bed, he suddenly roared out most pitifully, crying for help or else he would perish. And being asked what ailed him, he answered, half asleep, that his friend Ausanius and his wife, whom he had slain long ago, were summoning him to judgment before God. Upon this confession he was arrested and, after due examination, stoned to death. Thus the terrors and horrors of his own conscience revealed that secret wickedness which no one could prove against him.

I have read how Mahomet the great Turk, with great rewards, had hired two Turks to undertake to kill Scanderbeg. These traitors came to Scanderbeg making such a show of detesting both Mahomet’s tyrannical government and his vain superstition that they were held, both by Scanderbeg and by others, to be indeed the men they wished to be taken for. And so, after they had learned the principles of the Christian religion, they were both baptized at their own desire. Soon after, by a providence, it so happened that these two traitors fell out with each other, by which means the plot came to be revealed; and after due examination and confession of the deed, they were at once condemned and executed.

Conscience is God’s spy in the bosom. Conscience, like a scribe, a registrar, sits in the closet of your+ hearts with pen in hand and keeps a daily record of all your+ secret ways and secret crimes, which are beyond the knowledge of men. Conscience sets down the time when, the place where, the manner how, and the persons with whom such and such secret wickednesses have been committed—and sets them down so clearly and plainly that, go where you+ will and do what you+ can, the record of them shall never be cancelled or erased until God appears in judgment.

Let a man sin in the most secret seclusion that human cunning can contrive; let him take every way he can to hide his sins, to cloak and cover his sin, as Adam did; yet conscience will so play the judge that it will bring in the evidence, produce the law, press the penalty, and pass the sentence of condemnation upon him. Many a man who makes a fair profession of faith and has a great name in the world is yet αὐτοκατάκριτος, self-condemned for those secret sins which are neither plain to the eyes of man nor punishable by the laws or hands of men. Indeed, many times in this life God raises such a hell of horror and terror in many men’s consciences because of their secret sins that they can have no rest or quiet, neither in bed nor at table, neither lying down nor rising up. They would gladly conceal their sins; they are unwilling that the world should know how vile they have been in secret. But with conscience on the rack, and always gnawing, accusing, and condemning them, they can hold out no longer. Now all must come out, and now those sins that were most secret and concealed come to be proclaimed on the housetop.

Some who have been in anguish of conscience, others who have been struck with delirium, and many in their very sleep, have often been the ones to broadcast and proclaim their own secret filthiness and wickedness. In such cases, God has made many a secret sinner cry out with the leper, “Unclean, unclean!” (Lev 13:45), and with Judas before all present, “I have sinned, I have sinned” (Matt 27:4). Many times in this life God very strangely and wonderfully reveals those secret works of darkness in which people have long lived undetected.

A Pythagorean bought a pair of shoes on credit. The shoemaker dies; he is glad, and thinks he has the shoes for free. But a while after, his conscience flies at him and becomes a continual scold and tormentor to him. At this he goes to the dead man’s house and throws in his money with these words: “There, take your due; you live to me, though dead to all others.” But,

4. Secret Sins Are in Some Respects More Dangerous Than Open Sins

Fourthly, consider that secret sins are in some respects more dangerous than open sins. Many a man bleeds to death inwardly, and no man notices it. The more inward and secret the disease is, the greater the danger that the man will lose his life. There are no fevers so dangerous as those that prey on the vital spirits and inward parts; so there are no sins so dangerous and destructive to the souls of men as those that are most inward and secret. Secret sins often reign most powerfully in the souls of men when they are least visible.

First, consider that he who sins secretly deprives himself of those helps and remedies which, by a divine blessing, might arm him against sin, indeed, make him victorious over sin: namely, the prayers, counsels, rebukes, examples, and encouragements of friends and relatives. A man’s house may be on fire, but while it is all inside, no help comes in. But when the fire flames out, when it catches the outside of the house, then help runs in; then help is ready on every hand. He who sins in secret shuts himself off from all public remedy, and takes great pains to damn his soul in a corner and to go to hell in the dark.

Secondly, secret sins will make way for public sins. He who has no scruple about sinning in the secret room will before long, with Absalom, be ready to spread a tent on the top of the house and to go in to his concubines in the sight of all Israel (2 Sam 16:21–22). Those who have had no scruple about stealing a few pins or pennies or a few shillings in private have in time come to be so bold as to take a purse on the highway at high noon. The cockatrice must be crushed in the egg, or it will soon become a serpent. The very thought of sin, if not looked to, will break out into action, action into custom, custom into habit, and then both body and soul are irrecoverably lost to all eternity.

If Satan can only wound our heel, as the poets imagine of Achilles, he will go to great lengths to send death from the heel to the heart. If this subtle serpent can only wriggle in his tail by an evil thought, he will soon get in his head by a worse action. That is why Christ calls hatred murder, and a lustful eye adultery. Secret hatred often ends in open murder, and secret lustful glances of the eye often end in visible adultery. If Amnon is sick with the sinful conceptions of incestuous lust, how his soul will be in pain and labor until he has brought forth! And how many there are who in secret have taken now and then just one cup more than enough, who now may be seen at high noon reeling against every post! Just as secret diseases in the body, if not cured, will in time openly break out, so secret sins in the soul, if not pardoned and purged, will in time be openly revealed. Covetousness was Judas’s secret sin; and no sooner does an occasion or a temptation present itself than he is very ready and eager to betray and sell his Lord and Master for thirty pieces of silver before all the world. “After lust has conceived, it gives birth to sin” (Jas 1:15)—and that in this way:

First, sin has its conception, and that is delight.

And then its formation, and that is design.

And then its birth, and that is action.

And then its growth, and that is custom.

And then its end, and that is damnation.

Thirdly, secret sinning shows far more respect and fear toward men than toward God. You will be unjust in secret, and lustful in secret, and unclean in secret, and treacherous in secret. And why? Only because you are afraid that this or that man might know it, or that this or that friend might know it, or that this or that relative might know it. Ah, poor wretch, are you afraid of the eye of a man, “of mortal man, of a son of man who withers like grass” (Isa 51:12)? And yet you do not tremble under the eye of Him whose eyes are “like a blazing fire,” sharp and terrible, such as pierce into the inward parts (Rev 1:14; Heb 4:12–13)? Ah, how full of atheism is the heart of that man who silently says, “If only my sins are hidden from the eyes of the world, I do not care though the Lord knows them, though the Lord strictly observes them, though the Lord sets a mark, a memorandum, on them.” What is this, O man, but to defy God, and to tempt Him, and to provoke Him to His very face, who “is light, and in Him there is no darkness at all” (1 John 1:5)? Ah, sinner, sinner, can man damn you? Can man disinherit you? Can man fill your conscience with horrors and terrors? Can man make your life a very hell? Can man bar the gates of glory against you? Can man speak you into the grave by a word of his mouth? And after all, can man cast you into endless, easeless, and remediless torments? Oh, no! Can God do all this? Oh, yes! Why, then, does your heart not stand more in awe of the eye of the great God than of the eye of a poor, weak, mortal man?

I have dwelt longer on this point because there is no one thing in all the world that hinders secret communion with God and secret prayer more than secret sins do. And oh, that you+ would all make it your+ great business to watch against secret sins, and to pray against secret sins, and to mourn over secret sins, and to judge and condemn yourselves+ deeply for secret sins, and carefully and conscientiously to shun and avoid all occasions and incitements that may be fuel for secret sins!

Certainly, there are no men or women so sincere and serious in closet prayer, or so frequent, so fervent, so constant in closet prayer, or so delighted, so resolute, so undaunted, or so unwearied in closet prayer, as those who keep themselves most clear and free from secret sins.

To close, remember this: that though secret sins are in some respects more dangerous than other sins, yet in three respects they are not so bad nor so dangerous as other sins.

First, they do not bring as much scandal on religion as open sins do.

Secondly, they do not shame, grieve, and wound the hearts of the saints as open sins do.

Thirdly, they are not so infectious to others, nor do they provoke others to sin against the Lord as much as open sins do.

And so you+ may see what things you+ must carefully beware of, if ever you+ would devote yourselves+ to closet prayer.

5. Other Things to Apply Yourselves To

And just as you+ must beware of these five things, so there are various other things you+ must carefully and conscientiously apply yourselves+ to, if ever you+ would be found faithful and constant in this great duty of closet prayer.

Now they are these:

a. Lament Greatly and Mourn Bitterly over the Neglect of This Duty

First, lament greatly and mourn bitterly over the neglect of this choice duty. He who does not make it a matter of conscience to mourn over the neglect of this duty will never make it a matter of conscience to perform this duty. Oh, that your+ heads were waters, and your+ eyes a fountain of tears, that you+ might weep day and night for the great neglect of closet prayer (Jer 9:1)! He who mourns most for the neglect of this duty will be found most in the practice of this duty. He who most makes it a matter of conscience to accuse, arraign, and condemn himself for neglecting closet prayer will most make it a matter of conscience to give himself up to closet prayer. It is said of Adam that he turned his face toward the garden of Eden and from his heart bitterly lamented his great fall. Oh, that you+ would turn your+ faces toward your+ closets and bitterly lament how seldom you+ go into them!

b. Make Private Prayer Your Constant Trade

Secondly, habituate yourselves+, accustom yourselves+, to closet prayer. Make private prayer your+ constant trade. Frequency breeds familiarity, and familiarity confidence. We can go freely and boldly into the house of a friend whom we often visit. What we are accustomed to, we do with ease and delight. A man who is habituated or accustomed to writing, reading, riding, running, or playing this or that musical instrument does it all with delight and ease. And so a man who habituates himself to closet prayer will carry it on with delight and ease.

c. Keep a Diary of All Your Closet Experiences

Thirdly, keep a diary of all your+ closet experiences (Deut 7:18–19; Ps 66:12). Oh, carefully record and write down all your+ closet blessings! Oh, be often reading over your+ closet experiences, and be often meditating and pondering on your+ closet experiences! There is no way like this to inflame your+ love for closet prayer and to bind your+ hearts to this secret trade of private prayer.

Oh, remember that at one time you+ went into your+ closets with hard hearts and dry eyes, but before you+ came out of your+ closets, ah, how sweetly, how evangelically, how powerfully you+ were melted and humbled before the Lord (Ps 6:6; 39:12; 56:8)! Oh, remember how at another time you+ went into your+ closets clouded and in darkness, but came out of your+ closets with as glorious a shining of God on your+ souls as Moses had on his face when he came down from the mountain from speaking with God (Exod 34:28–29)! Oh, remember how often you+ have gone into your+ closets with cold, frozen spirits, but before you+ came out of your+ closets, what a fire God has kindled in your+ souls, what a spirit of burning you+ have found in your+ hearts (Luke 24:31–32; Isa 4:4)! Oh, remember how often you+ have gone into your+ closets cramped and tongue-tied, but before you+ came out, how your+ souls have been like the chariots of Amminadib (Song 6:12)! Oh, remember what power God has given you+ against corruptions in your+ closets, and what strength God has given you+ against temptations in your+ closets! Oh, remember the sweet revelations of divine love that you+ have had while in your+ closets! Oh, remember the secret visits, the secret kisses, the secret embraces, the secret whispers, the secret love-tokens that Christ has given you+ in your+ closets! Oh, seriously ponder these things, and then closet duties will be sweet to you+!

It was a sweet saying of Bernard: “O saint, do you not know that your husband Christ is bashful and will not be intimate in company? Withdraw by meditation into your closet, or into the fields, and there you will have Christ’s embraces.” Meditatio nutrix orationis, “Meditation is the nurse of prayer.” Oh, the more any man meditates on his closet experiences, the more he will find his heart bound to closet duties. The more you+ ponder closet experiences, the sweeter closet experiences will be to your+ souls. And the sweeter closet experiences are to your+ souls, the more your+ souls will delight to be with God in your+ closets.

Pliny tells us of one Messala Corvinus, whose memory was so bad that he forgot his own name. And I am afraid that many of your+ memories are so bad that you+ forget your+ closet blessings, your+ closet experiences.

I have read of a plague once at Athens of such a kind that it took away the memories of those who were infected with it, so that they forgot their own names. Oh, that I had no cause to fear that some plague or other has so taken away the memories of many that they have quite forgotten their closet experiences! Well, friends, remember this: though stony hearts are bad, yet iron memories are good. And oh, that you+ would all labor for iron memories, so that you+ may remember and ponder your+ closet experiences! I have read of the heathen, how they made use of white and black stones for these two ends. First, they gave them to persons at their trial before the judges. If any were condemned to death, they gave him a black stone, but if acquitted and set free, a white stone. The Holy Spirit seems to allude to this custom in Revelation 2:17: “To the one who overcomes, I will give … a white stone.” A second use of those stones was this: that by them they might keep an account of all the good days or evil days they had met with in their lives. So Giacopo Senzaro, having been long in love and much thwarted about his marriage, filled a pot full of black stones, putting only one white stone among them; and being asked the reason, he answered, “There will come one white day,” meaning his wedding day, “which will make amends for all my black days.”

Ah, friends! How often God has given you+ the white stone in your+ closets! Certainly, you+ have had more white stones than black stones. Your+ closet blessings and experiences have outnumbered your+ public trials and miseries. O sirs! If you+ only counted your+ good days by the white stones you+ have had in your+ closets, it would make you+ more in love with closet prayer than ever.

d. Do Not Spend So Much Time in Public Duties That None Is Left for Private

Fourthly, be sure that you+ do not spend so much of your+ precious time in public duties and ordinances that you+ can spare none for private duties, for secret services. Though Pharaoh’s cows ate one another up, our duties must not eat one another up (Gen 41:4). Public duties must not eat up family duties; family duties must not eat up public duties; neither of them must eat up closet duties. The wisdom of a Christian sparkles and shines most brightly in giving every duty its proper time and place. I was going to say that he is either no Christian, or at least no excellent Christian, who is all eye to read, or all ear to hear, or all tongue to speak, or all knee to bow, to kneel, to pray (Eccl 8:5). Ah, how many there are who spend so much time hearing this man and that, and running here and there from meeting to meeting, that they have no time to meet with God in their closets! O sirs! Your+ duties are never so lovely and pleasing, they are never so radiant and beautiful, as when they are performed in their proper season and in their proper order.

Oh, how wise the men of this world are in ordering all their worldly affairs so that no one business interferes with another! They set apart a suitable portion of time for each business. They allot an hour for one business, two for another, three for another, etc. Oh, that we were as wise for our souls, as wise for eternity, as they are for this world! Oh, that our hearts would so take counsel with our heads that we might never lack a fit time to seek God in a corner! That devil who loves to set one man against another, and one nation against another, and one Christian against another, that devil loves to set one ordinance against another, and one duty against another. That is why, on the one hand, he stirs up some to exalt public prayers in opposition to secret prayer. And on the other hand, he stirs up others to exalt private duties in opposition to all public duties. Yet all Christians are bound by God to manage one sort of duty so as not to shut out another sort. Every Christian must find time and room for every duty that is incumbent on him.

e. Love Christ with a More Inflamed Love

Fifthly, love Christ with a more inflamed love. Oh, strengthen your+ love for Christ, and your+ love for closet duties! Lovers dearly love to be alone, to be in a corner together (Song 7:10–12). Certainly, the more any man loves the Lord Jesus, the more he will delight to be with Christ in a corner. There was a great deal of love between Jonathan and David (1 Sam 18–20); and their private fellowship, their secret communion with one another, matched their love. They were always at their best when in the field together, or when in a corner together, or when behind the door together, or when locked up together. And it would be just the same with you+ if you+ only loved the Lord Jesus Christ with a higher and a more inflamed love. You+ would always be at your+ best when you+ were most with Christ in a corner.

Divine love is like a rod of myrtle, which, as Pliny reports, makes the traveler who carries it in his hand so lively and cheerful that he never faints or grows weary. Ah, friends, if you+ only loved the Lord Jesus with a stronger, with a higher love, you+ would never faint in closet duties, nor would you+ ever grow weary of closet duties. Just as the Israelites moved their tents from Mithcah to Hashmonah, from sweetness to swiftness, as the names mean (Num 33:29), so the sweetness of divine love will make a man move swiftly on in the way of closet duties. Divine love will make all closet duties easier to the soul, and more pleasant and delightful to the soul. And therefore, do all you+ can to strengthen your+ love for Christ and your+ love for closet work.

It was observed among the early Christians that they were so full of love for one another that they could become as well acquainted with one another in half an hour as in half a year. O sirs, if only your+ hearts were fuller of love for Christ and for closet duties, you+ would quickly be better acquainted with them; you+ would quickly know what secret communion with Christ behind the door means.

f. Be Fixedly Resolved to Keep Close to Closet Duties

Sixthly, be highly, thoroughly, and fixedly resolved, in the strength of Christ, to keep close to closet duties in the face of all the difficulties and discouragements that you+ may meet with (Ps 44:17–20). A man of no resolution, or of weak resolution, will be won with a nut and lost with an apple. Satan, and the world, and carnal relatives, and your+ own hearts will throw in many things to discourage you+ and draw you+ away from closet prayer. But be nobly and firmly resolved to keep close to your+ closets, let the world, the flesh, and the devil do and say what they can. Daniel was a man of invincible resolution. Rather than omit praying in his room, he would be cast into the den of lions. Of all the duties of religion, Satan is the deadliest enemy to this one of secret prayer: partly because secret prayer foils him in his most secret designs, plots, and schemes against the soul; and partly because secret prayer is so musical and delightful to God; and partly because secret prayer is of such rare use and advantage to the soul; and partly because it does not lay the soul so open to pride, vainglory, and worldly applause as prayer in the synagogue does. And therefore, he would rather have a man pray a thousand times in the synagogues, on the street corners, or behind a pillar, than pray once in his closet. And therefore, you+ need to steel your+ hearts with holy courage and resolution, so that whatever suggestions, temptations, oppositions, or objections you+ may encounter, you+ will yet keep close to closet prayer.

There is no better bulwark in the day of battle than a heroic resolution of heart before the day of battle. Sanctified resolutions greatly weaken and discourage Satan in his assaults. They greatly daunt and dishearten him in all his attempts against the soul. The man who is not steadfastly resolved to seek the Lord in a corner, even if all the powers of darkness rise up against him, will never be at peace in his closet for long. O sirs! Divine fortitude, holy resolutions, will make you+ like a wall of brass that no arrows can pierce. They will make you+ like tested armor that no shot can hurt. They will make you+ like that angel who rolled away the stone from the door of the tomb (Matt 28:2). They will either enable you+ to remove the greatest mountains of opposition that lie between you+ and closet prayer, or else they will enable you+ to step over them.

Luther was a man of great resolution and a man who spent much time in closet prayer. Nehemiah was another such man. He met with so much opposition that, had he not been steeled by a strong and unyielding resolution, he could never have rebuilt the temple, but would have sunk in the middle of his work. And he was a man given to private prayer, as I have shown at the beginning of this treatise. Who was more resolute than David? And who was more given to secret prayer than David? I might say the same of Paul, Basil, and many others who have been famous in their generations.

O sirs! Sanctified resolutions for closet prayer will chain you+ faster to closet prayer than Ulysses’ resolutions ever chained him to the mast of the ship. It was a noble resolution that kept Ruth close to her mother-in-law when her sister-in-law Orpah only paid her courtesies, kissed her, and took her leave of her (Ruth 1:10–20). Just be nobly resolved for closet prayer, and then you+ will keep close to it when others only court it and take their leave of it.

In the Salentine country, mention is made of a lake that is always brimful. However much you put in, it never runs over; however much you draw out, it is always full. The resolution of every Christian for closet prayer should be like this lake, always brimful. Come life, come death, come honor or reproach, come loss or gain, come liberty or bonds, come what may, the true-bred Christian must be fully and constantly resolved to keep close to his closet.

g. Labor for a Greater Effusion of the Holy Spirit

Seventhly, labor for a greater effusion of the Holy Spirit; for the greater measure any man has of the Spirit of God, the more that man will delight to be with God in secret. “I will pour out on the house of David and on the people of Jerusalem a spirit of grace and supplication … The land will mourn, each clan apart: the clan of the house of David apart, and their wives apart; the clan of the house of Nathan apart, and their wives apart; the clan of the house of Levi apart, and their wives apart; the clan of Shimei apart, and their wives apart; and all the remaining clans, each clan apart, and their wives apart” (Zech 12:10, 12–14). Note that in the last of the last days, when men shall generally be under a greater effusion of the Holy Spirit than ever (Joel 2:28–29; Isa 44:3), then they shall be more given up to secret prayer than ever. There will never be such praying apart, and such mourning apart, as there will be when the Lord pours out His Spirit most richly, gloriously, abundantly, on His poor people. Then everyone shall pour out his tears and his soul before God in a corner, to show the soundness of their sorrow and to show their sincerity by their secrecy, for Ille dolet vere, qui sine teste dolet, “He grieves in earnest who grieves without a witness.”

Certainly, the more any man is now under the blessed pouring out of the Spirit of Christ, the more that man gives himself up to secret communion with Christ. Every man is with Christ in his closet more or less, just as he is more or less under the anointings of the Spirit of Christ. The more any man has of the Spirit of Christ, the more he loves Christ; and the more any man loves Christ, the more he delights to be with Christ alone. Lovers love to be alone. The more any man has of the Spirit of Christ, the more his heart will be set on pleasing Christ. Now, nothing pleases Christ more than the secret prayers of His people. “O my dove, in the clefts of the rock, in the secret places of the stairs, let me see your face, let me hear your voice; for your voice is sweet, and your countenance is lovely” (Song 2:14). And therefore such a man will spend much time in secret prayer. The more any man has of the Spirit of Christ, the more his heart will be set on glorifying and exalting Christ. Now, nothing glorifies Christ more, or exalts Him more, than secret prayer; and therefore, the more any man has of the Spirit of Christ, the more that man will be found in secret prayer.

There are many persons who say they would be more in their closets than they are, but that they meet with many hindrances, many engagements, many distractions, many temptations, many oppositions, many difficulties, many discouragements which prevent them. Ah, friends! If you+ had a greater measure of the Holy Spirit upon you+, none of these things would ever be able to hinder your+ secret trade heavenward. If you+ had a richer anointing of the Spirit upon you+, you+ would never plead, “A lion is in the road! A fierce lion roams the public square!” (Prov 26:13). But if there were a thousand lions between you+ and your+ closets, you+ would either step over them or make your+ way through them, so that you+ might enjoy communion with Christ in your+ closets.

h. Be Frequent in the Serious Consideration of Eternity

Eighthly and lastly, if ever you+ would keep close to private prayer, be frequent in the serious consideration of eternity. Oh, see eternity standing at the end of every closet prayer, and this will make you+ pray effectually in your+ closets.

O sirs! Every work you+ do is a step toward a blessed or a cursed eternity. Every movement, every action in this life, is a step toward eternity. As every step that a traveler takes brings him forward to his journey’s end, so every step that a man takes in the secret ways of righteousness and holiness, such as closet duties are, brings him nearer to his journey’s end. It brings him nearer to a blessed eternity. Just as every step the sinner takes in a way of wickedness brings him nearer to hell, so every step that a saint takes in a way of holiness brings him nearer to heaven. Just as every step that a wicked man takes in the ways of unrighteousness brings him nearer to a cursed eternity, so every step that a godly man takes in a way of righteousness brings him nearer to a blessed eternity.

Zeuxis, the famous painter, was so exceedingly careful and cautious in drawing all his lines that he would let no piece of his go out into the world to be seen by men until he had turned it over and over, and viewed it from this side and that, again and again, to see if he could spot any fault in it. When he was asked why he was so painstaking and so long in drawing his lines, he answered, Aeternitati pingo, “I paint for eternity.” O sirs! We all pray for eternity, we fast for eternity, we read for eternity, we hear for eternity, we wait for eternity, we weep for eternity. And therefore, oh, how painstaking, how exact, how wise, how faithful, how careful, how diligent, how unwearied we should be in all our closet duties and services, since all we do is with a view to eternity! Friends! You+ must all before long be eternally blessed or eternally cursed; eternally happy or eternally miserable; eternally saved or eternally damned; eternally accepted or eternally rejected. And therefore, what infinite cause you+ have to shut your+ closet doors often and to plead mightily with God in a corner for the lives of your+ poor, precious, and immortal souls, that they may be eternally saved in the Great Day of our Lord Jesus! O sirs! When any hindrances to closet prayer present themselves to you+, seriously remember eternity, and that will remove them.

It is related of one Pachomius that whenever he felt any unlawful desires arise in his mind, he used to drive them away with the remembrance of eternity.

The same author tells a story from Benedictus Rhexanus of an ungodly fellow who could not sleep one night: as he seriously considered death and eternity and the damned lying in hell, he could not rest, but eternity kept running through his mind. He would gladly have shaken off these thoughts, as gnawing worms. So he followed sports, pastimes, and merrymaking, and sought out companions like himself, and often sat so long over his drink that he put his conscience to sleep, and so seemed to get some rest. But when he woke, his conscience flew in his face and kept putting sad thoughts of eternity into his mind. Of all things in the world, he could not bear to be kept awake at night. But it so happened that, being sick, he was kept awake one night and could not sleep at all, and these thoughts arose in him: “What? Is it so tedious, then, to be kept from sleep one night and to lie a few hours in the dark? Oh, what is it, then, to be kept in torments and everlasting darkness! I am here in my own house on a soft bed in the dark, kept from sleep for only one night. But to lie in flames and endless misery, how dreadful that must be!” These and similar meditations were the happy means of this young man’s conversion.

I have read a notable story of one Theodorus, a young Christian man in Egypt, who, when there was a great deal of feasting, mirth, and music in his father’s house, withdrew from all the company; and when he was alone, he thought to himself, “Here is contentment and delight enough for the flesh. I may have what I desire, but how long will this last? This will not hold out long.” Then, falling down on his knees before the Lord in secret, he said, “Lord, my heart is open to You. I truly do not know what to ask except only this: Lord, do not let me die eternally. O Lord, You know I love You. Let me live eternally to praise You.”

If there is any way or means on earth to bring us to our knees before God in secret, it is serious and solemn thoughts of eternity. Oh, that the fear of eternity might fall on all your+ souls! Oh, that you+ would all seriously consider that after a short time has passed you+ must all enter an eternal state! Oh, consider that eternity is an infinite, endless, bottomless gulf which no line can fathom, no time can reach, no age can extend to, no tongue can express. It is a duration always present, a being always in being. It is unum perpetuum hodie, “one perpetual day,” which shall never see light. O sirs! This is, and must be, for a lamentation: that eternity is a thing most men never think of, or else think of very lightly—a snatch and away, as dogs are said to lap and run at the river Nile. But if ever you+ would have your+ hearts chained to your+ closets and to closet duties—as the men of Tyre chained their god Apollo to a post, so that they might be sure of him—then seriously and frequently ponder eternity, and with those forty valiant martyrs be always crying out, ὦ ἀϊδιότης, ὦ ἀϊδιότης, “O eternity, eternity!”

Mr. Wood, after some holy conversation, fell to musing and cried out before all present for nearly half a quarter of an hour on end, “Forever, forever, forever.” Augustine’s prayer was, “Hack me, hew me, burn me here, but spare me hereafter, spare me hereafter.” Certainly, if Christians would only spend one quarter of an hour every day in solemn thoughts of eternity, it would make them more in love with closet prayer than ever. Indeed, it would make them more fearful of omitting closet prayer than ever, and more careful and conscientious in carrying out all closet duties than ever.

And so, according to my weak measure, I have given out all that the Lord has at present graciously given in to my poor soul concerning this most necessary, most glorious, and most useful point of points: closet prayer. By assisting grace, I will follow this poor piece with my prayers, that it may be so blessed from on high as to work mightily for the internal and eternal welfare of reader, hearer, and writer alike.
